  - **THE EXECUTIVE CELLS TIGHTENED, ON PURPOSE, WHERE THE API ALREADY REFUSES THEM.** The note and catalogue tables and the inserts, deletes and updates of invoices, bills, receipts and payments that need `accounting:write` (Manager); `ai_insights`, the health tables, onboarding and renewals (`report:write`, `client:write`: Manager); and the UPDATE of `itr_filings`, `tax_computation_snapshots` and `tax_disallowances` (every UPDATE route there is an approval, `income_tax:approve`, Manager). The reviewed access expectation was regenerated twice and each diff read: only `exec_assigned` rows changed, only write cells the API already refuses. Nothing the application does is affected.
  - **THE CLASS IS GONE FROM THE MATRIX, AND THREE ROLLBACK CONTROLS PUT EACH SLICE BACK.** The deviations that remain are three small classes (the fee tables' read tier, fee receipts, a suspended member's own `users` row: 12 cells over 4 tables).
  - **WHAT A ROW POLICY CANNOT DO, AND SO IS A LEDGER ITEM.** `lib/data/gst.ts` sets `ca_approved` on `gstr1_returns` and `gstr3b_returns` straight over PostgREST with the approver's id supplied by the browser; the API refuses that approval below Manager, but a policy cannot tell an approval from any other UPDATE of the row, so an Executive (`gst:compute`) can still approve a return from the browser (POST-A-223). The definer functions that write these tables bypass the policies by design and ask who is calling since migration 486.

- **THE PAYROLL TIER REACHES THE OTHER PAYROLL TABLES, EACH KEYED THE WAY IT IS REALLY KEYED, AND TWO PARTS OF THE PLAN WERE WRONG** (migration 490, 14-10-2026; ledger POST-A-219). Migration 485 left twelve payroll tables readable by every member of the firm and promised "the same parent scoping". A read-only audit of every reader and writer before building found that nothing in the product reads or writes any of them under a caller's own sign-in (the payroll router is built on the service client, and so are the ECR, remittance and bonus-register services, the 24Q and annexure builders, the scheduler and the employee portal's TDS projection; the web app has no `.from()` on any of them), so the new rules bind only a direct database call, no Python had to change first, and an employee principal reads none of them, so no own-record branch was needed. 490 adds 48 RESTRICTIVE policies to thirteen tables: `<t>_employee_in_reach` (`employee_id IN (SELECT id FROM payroll_employees)`) on seven employee-keyed tables, the settlement components through their settlement and the run history through its run, a `<t>_read_tier` on three client-keyed tables (`my_permission('payroll','read','Manager') AND can_access_client(client_id) AND` the internal practice client is Partner-only), and `<t>_write_tier_*` (`my_permission('payroll','write','Manager')`, plus the client conditions on the client-keyed ones) on all of them. The write tier is separate because the parent rule asks the READ tier, and the old write policies on these tables ask the role and not the grid, so a Manager the Partner had blocked from payroll writes could still write a loan.
  - **TWO PARTS OF THE PLAN WERE WRONG, AND THE AUDIT IS WHAT FOUND THEM.** `reconciliation_findings` is not a payroll table: its `run_id` points at `reconciliation_runs`, so a `run_id IN (SELECT id FROM payroll_runs)` rule would have left it permanently empty for everyone; it is already Partner-only to read with a select-only grant and is untouched (migration 485's header lists it wrongly, and was NOT edited, because a migration's checksum is what production matches on and editing one re-runs it there). And `salary_structures`, `statutory_remittances` and `epfo_ecr_filings` have no `employee_id`, and the last two carry a NULLABLE `run_id` that is set to null when the run is deleted (a remittance is recorded without a run), so a run-scoped rule would have hidden every remittance or filing recorded without one while `OR run_id IS NULL` would have been a hole; scoping the salary template through employees would have hidden it for a client with no employees yet. They are keyed on the client. `bonus_declarations`, the employer's own determination, is the same kind of table and went in with them.
  - **A LATENT TRAP, RECORDED IN THE MIGRATION AND HERE.** Several of those readers answer EMPTY on failure by design: the salary in force (an empty answer pays everyone the master rate), the loan instalments (recovers nothing), the perquisites of a run (drops them from the section 192 estimate), and the transition and loan-movement logs swallow an insert failure. Row security that filtered every row would look exactly like "none exist", so the router must stay on the service role until each is made to tell the two apart.
  - **THE TEAM SCREEN'S SENTENCE CHANGED AGAIN.** A block on reading payroll now reaches all of the payroll records named in the notice, and a block on writing reaches them except attendance, leave and the tax declarations, which still ask the role alone (open, POST-A-219; migration 499, the last entry in this file, has since taken attendance's signed-in write away altogether). The grid guard's read scan now expects six named tables (the three client-keyed ones join employees, runs and payslips), and a new probe per kind of scoping proves a read block hides the table and a write block refuses the insert while leaving the read alone; twelve of those fail without the migration. The access matrix holds the thirteen as covered tables (they are not browser tables, so they are listed by name with the reason) and a control that re-opens them from the migration's own rollback.
  - **NOT DONE, ON PURPOSE.** `client_payroll_settings` (a Partner's switch, no salary data) and `client_statutory_identity` were not audited; leave and the declarations still ask the role for writes (attendance since 499 is refused to a signed-in session outright); the policies run a subquery against `payroll_employees` once per statement and nothing measured them on a large firm because nothing reads these tables under a caller's sign-in today.

- **AN ENGAGEMENT'S SCOPE IS READ APART FROM ITS FEES, AND A RELATIONSHIP SCORE IS THE CLIENT'S WHILE ITS FIGURES ARE THE PRACTICE'S** (14-10-2026; ledger POST-B-024, stage one). The fee tables (`fee_engagements`, `fee_invoices`, `fee_receipts`) are meant to be a Partner's to read (`billing:read`), and the database does not enforce it: an assigned Manager, Executive or Reviewer reads those clients' fee rows. A Partner-only read policy is the fix, and a read-only audit of every reader (sub-agent, 14-10-2026, not verified against production) found why it cannot simply be added: five places ask the table a DIFFERENT question, the scope of the work, as the caller, and a rule on who reads fees would have answered each with an empty list and no error. The client Compliance tab would have said "No active service engagement is recorded... Add an engagement" to a Manager about a client who has one (and a Manager cannot add one); `generate_due`, run from that tab and from the GST filing-frequency edit, would have generated only the GST baseline and never the ITR, TDS or MCA obligations, with the response looking like success; `itr_profile_for` could not have seen an audit engagement, so an audit client of a non-company type got 31 July instead of 31 October with the wrong cause named; the generate-obligations route would have answered 404 for an engagement that exists; and the relationship-health score would have pinned the payment third at 40 and printed 0 and Rs 0.00 as values.
  - **WHAT STAGE ONE DOES.** `EngagementRepository.find_scope` / `find_scope_by_id` return the four scope columns (`SCOPE_COLUMNS`: id, firm, client, service type, status) with the privileged client and the firm's own scope, never the fee, the billing cycle or the notes. The caller is authorised by the route first (rbac and `assert_client_access`, or the `allowed_client_ids` filter `generate_due` already applies), which is the contract of `db_provider.service_db`. `compliance_obligation_service` reads engagements through it and through nothing else (an AST rule over the whole module, not over three spellings), `GET /api/compliance/engagement-status` serves the Compliance tab's question (the tab used to read the fee table from the browser and carry its own copy of the status set), and `has_active_engagement` has no try/except: an engagement list that cannot be read is an error, never "no engagement is recorded".
  - **THE SCORE IS THE CLIENT'S, THE FIGURES ARE THE PRACTICE'S.** `invoice_repo.find_for_health` (four columns, privileged client, the firm's scope) feeds `_relationship_rows`, so a health score is the same for every role. `compute_relationship_health` and `compute_recommendations` take `include_fee_figures`, which the routes set from `can_user(billing, read)` (per person, so a Manager the Partner granted billing still sees them). Without it `overdue_invoices` and `outstanding_paise` are NONE, not zero, the payload says `fees_withheld`, the Insights screen prints "Not shown", and a recommendation says a fee payment is overdue without a count or an amount. A zero would tell a Manager that a client who owes the practice money owes none.
  - **A DECISION TAKEN HERE.** `ai:read` is all-staff and used to carry the fee amounts; the figures now follow `billing:read` instead, because a derived screen that shows outstanding fees to everyone is the same disclosure by another door.
  - **STAGE TWO: THE APPLICATION ASKS `billing:read` BEFORE THE DATABASE DOES, AND THEN THE DATABASE DOES (migration 495).** `/api/invoices` asked `invoice:read` and `invoice:write` (an Executive's) for the practice's own `fee_invoices`, while `/api/engagements` and `/api/billing` asked `billing` and refused an Executive: the same rows were served at one door and refused at the other. Every route in that router asks `billing` now (an AST rule over the module). The analytics profitability, concentration and revenue-vs-effort endpoints ask `billing:read` beside `analytics:read` (a Manager the Partner has granted billing keeps them, narrowed to their assigned book as before; a Manager without it is refused in words), and `/api/analytics/firm` gives a caller without it `fees_withheld` and null for its four fee figures, and gives null, not four zeros, when the read fails (it used to sit in `except: pass` over initial zeros). The two staff screens that read the fee tables from the browser (`/billing`, `/accounting/receivables`) and the profitability screen sit behind `FeeAccessGate` (the layout of each folder, the `PayrollAccessGate` shape, acting only once permissions have loaded), and a web guard finds any other file that reads one of the three tables from the browser. The migration then adds `<t>_read_tier` (`my_permission('billing','read','Partner')`) as a RESTRICTIVE SELECT on `fee_engagements`, `fee_invoices` and `fee_receipts`, the last scoped through its invoice because it has no `client_id`; the assignment scope that already existed keeps confining a granted Manager to their clients. The matrix's two deviation classes (10 cells) were deleted with a control that re-opens them from the migration's own rollback, and the Team screen's notice now says a block on reading billing hides the three tables.
  - **WHAT A MANAGER SEES DIFFERENTLY, STATED PLAINLY.** A Manager or Executive without billing access no longer sees the practice's revenue, margin or client concentration (Profitability, Concentration, Revenue vs Effort), the fee figures on Insights (a score and "Not shown"), the Fee Receivables screen or the Billing screen; the Partner can grant `billing:read` per person on the Team screen. That is the owner's accepted decision (POST-B-024, Partner-only reads), applied to every door and not only to the table.
  - **THE CLIENT-DELETE BLOCKERS WERE A SIXTH READER, AND THEY FAILED OPEN** (POST-A-231, closed 14-10-2026). `_check_delete_blockers` read the active fee engagements and the fee invoices as the caller, each inside a bare `except: pass`. `client:delete` is a Partner's by role but a Partner can grant it to a Manager, and from migration 495 that Manager reads no fee rows, so both blockers would have read zero with no error and a client with live fees would have been deleted. The two blockers are now COUNTS (`engagement_repo.count_active_for_client`, `invoice_repo.count_live_for_client`) read with the privileged client and the firm's and the client's scope, after `rbac(client, delete)` and the client scope have authorised the caller. The same function swallowed a failed read of the engagement letters and of the DSC records the same way, so every source now REFUSES the delete in words (503, "nothing was changed") when it cannot be read: a blocker that could not be checked is not an absent blocker.
  - **NOT DONE.** Production's fee-table policies were not read beyond the repository's snapshot; the migration drops-if-exists then creates, so it is correct whatever is there.

- **A FINALIZED OR PAID PAYROLL RUN, AND ITS PAYSLIPS, ARE NOT WRITTEN BY A SIGNED-IN SESSION, AND THE API STAYS THE CONTROL FOR ITS OWN WRITES** (migration 498, 15-10-2026; ledger POST-B-004, the half migration 485 left). The API refuses to touch a released run (`_assert_run_is_unreleased` guards the recompute and the delete, `update_run_status` excludes finalized and paid, and a reversal is a Partner's act that posts the opposing journals first), but the database let a signed-in session holding `payroll:write` (a Manager's, since 485) do each of the things the API refuses: rewrite a released run's totals so it no longer agreed with the journal it was posted to while the PF return, the ESIC return and the 24Q read their figures from it; rewrite a payslip of a released run; set a draft straight to `finalized` or `paid` with no journal behind it, which is also what the employee portal and every statutory builder read as released; move a released run back to `review` without reversing its books, after which the API's finalise would post a second accrual; and delete a released run, which cascades to its payslips and, through `payroll_run_transitions` (ON DELETE CASCADE), to the append-only release log migration 328 built to be the thing that cannot be erased. No screen did any of it: `apps/web` selects these two tables and has no insert, update or delete on either, and the payroll router writes both through the service client at exactly ten sites, so nothing legitimate depended on the access that is closed. Two BEFORE row triggers refuse, with SQLSTATE 42501 and a sentence that says what to do instead, an INSERT or UPDATE that leaves a `payroll_runs` row finalized or paid, an UPDATE or DELETE of one that is, and any INSERT, UPDATE or DELETE of a `payroll_slips` row whose run, before or after the statement, is finalized or paid (so a slip cannot be moved into a released run or out of one). An independent review of the diff, given only the code and the threat, found no way round it (upserts by id and by month, MERGE, UPDATE ... FROM, a data-modifying CTE, bulk statements and the `salary_slips` view all fail) and found the five failures the first full real-database run had already shown (below).
  - **IT JUDGES A SIGNED-IN SESSION, AND ASKS THE API FOR ONE THING, ON PURPOSE.** The test is `current_setting('role')` being `authenticated` or `anon`, which PostgREST sets for a caller and which stays the caller's role inside a SECURITY DEFINER function (where `current_user` would be the owner). The sibling guard 486 reads the JWT claim; the role setting is used because a caller cannot forge it. It is a deny-list of two names, so a custom JWT role or a login role that inherits `authenticated` without SET ROLE would pass; neither exists here. The payroll router's finalise, disbursement and reversal arrive as `service_role`, and a trigger that judged them like a browser would refuse the API's own work, so for `service_role` the run trigger asks only that a run does not BECOME finalized without `journal_entry_id` or paid without both journals (every one of the router's writes sets them in the same UPDATE as the status; SQLSTATE 23514, on the transition only so a run released before the columns existed is not asked). It cannot prove the journals were reversed before a run reopens: that stays the router's ordering. **This is deliberately weaker than the journal immutability triggers (251), which refuse everyone, the owner and `service_role` too, so that the only way to change a posted ledger is a migration that disables the trigger.** A released payroll run has a legitimate way back, so `service_role` must be able to write it, and the database owner is not judged either (a repair is a deliberate, reviewable act, and it is what lets a fixture seed a released run). A bug in the payroll router can still write a released run, as it could before. `platform_purge_firm` (141) runs with `session_replication_role = replica` and bypasses the triggers, which is right.
  - **WHAT FOLLOWS FROM IT, SAID BEFORE SOMEBODY IS SURPRISED.** A foreign-key cascade is judged as the session that started it: a signed-in delete of an employee who has a payslip in a released run, or of a client with a released run, is now refused, with a sentence that names the payslips or the run and not the delete that caused it. That is wanted (it stops payslips being erased against the retention duty) and neither the API nor the browser does it (`delete_client` is a soft delete, and `delete_employee` runs as `service_role` and refuses an employee with slips). A SECURITY DEFINER function that a signed-in session calls and that writes these tables is judged too, because the role setting stays the caller's: a future atomic finalise exposed as an RPC and called through the request-scoped client would be refused and has to use the service client, like the router. And a row trigger fires before row security's WITH CHECK, so for a released run the trigger's refusal replaces row security's for an actor row security would have refused as well; both are 42501.
  - **TWO EXISTING GUARDS WENT RED, AND THE FIRST FULL RUN SHOWED IT BEFORE THE REVIEW DID.** `test_262_employee_portal_rls_pg` asserted that an employee inserting a payslip into a finalized run met row-level security, and now meets the trigger first; it points at a draft run for the row-security claim and asserts either refusal for the released one. The access matrix probes every actor on every table against rows it seeds as `finalized`, so with the triggers on, every write cell on the two tables read as the trigger's refusal, four tests went red and, worse, those cells could no longer have noticed a row-security regression on the two tables. **The matrix world disables the two triggers by name** (its prepare step already strips every non-access constraint for the same reason), and says so; the freeze has its own test file (78 cases: a Partner and an assigned Manager, an unassigned Executive and the employee, upserts, moves in and out, both cascades, the `service_role` evidence checks, the owner, the vocabulary and the rollback control, which also asserts the release log really is erased without the migration).
  - **THE VOCABULARY IS WRITTEN OUT IN THREE PLACES** (these triggers, `my_payroll_run_ids()` from 323, the API's `_PAYROLL_UNRELEASED`), so a test reads the CHECK constraint and fails the day a status is added, with a message naming all three.
  - **WHAT 498 LEFT OPEN WAS CLOSED BY 499 (POST-A-234).** A DRAFT or REVIEW run, and its slips, were still writable by Manager and above over PostgREST, because that was the tier 485 gave them and the owner accepted. Nothing in the browser writes either table, and the cleaner end state is that a signed-in session writes neither and every write goes through the API, which is also what "zero business logic in the frontend" means; it reversed the write half of an accepted decision, so it was recorded as its own ledger line and done in its own migration (the entry after this one). The review of 498 listed what was open: a Manager could stamp `finalized_at`, `paid_at`, `payment_reference` and totals on a draft; **the finalise journal is built from the run row's totals, so a Manager can choose the figure a Partner's finalise posts**; a Manager could INSERT a release-log row claiming a transition that never happened (498 stops the log being erased, not forged); and the loan-recovery, remittance and ECR-filing rows of a released run were writable at the tier. A refused attempt also leaves no audit row, because the statement aborts before the AFTER audit trigger.

- **A SIGNED-IN SESSION WRITES NONE OF THE PAYROLL TABLES THE BROWSER NEVER WRITES, AND THE POLICIES STAY AS THE SECOND LOCK** (migration 499, 17-10-2026; ledger POST-A-234, found by the independent review of migration 498). 498 froze a RELEASED run and its payslips against a signed-in write. What it could not touch was everything not yet released, and the review listed it: a Manager could write a draft or review run and its payslips directly, stamp `finalized_at`, `paid_at` and the totals, and **the finalise journal is built from the run row's totals, so a Manager could choose the figure a Partner's finalise then posts**; could INSERT a `payroll_run_transitions` row claiming a release that never happened (498 stops the log being erased, not forged); and could write the loan-recovery, remittance and ECR-filing rows of a released run, the salary history, the loans and perquisites a run is computed from, and the employees. A search of `apps/web` for every payroll table found exactly THREE that the browser writes, `leave_balances` (the attendance screen's upsert) and an employee's own tax declaration and its items (the portal's declaration tab); every other payroll table is read from the browser and written by the payroll router, which uses the privileged client at every write site. The write grants were not one decision: they came table by table from the migration that created each table and from the repair sweeps that granted lists of tables again (041, 055), and 485 and 490 then put the API's Manager-and-above write tier into the database as row policies, as the defence against a direct call with a person's own sign-in; they assumed the grants would stay. 499 takes the grants away, on the evidence that nothing needs them, and the policies stay. Production's catalogue, read on 17-10-2026, held exactly the write grants a migration-built database does, plus TRUNCATE for `authenticated` and `anon` on all seventeen (a hosting-platform default no migration grants, not reachable through PostgREST, revoked anyway). 499 revokes INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC on seventeen tables (employees, runs, payslips and the run history; what a run is computed from, attendance included; what a run leaves behind, remittances and ECR filings included) and ends with a check of the EFFECTIVE privilege (table level, column level, through PUBLIC or another role) that fails the migration, and so the deploy, if any write is left. Reading is untouched, `service_role` is untouched, and the 485/490 policies and the 498 triggers stay: a later migration that grants a list of tables again undoes a revoke and does not undo a policy or a trigger. **WHAT IT DOES NOT COVER, AND SAYS SO.** `leave_balances` and the two tax-declaration tables are still written from the browser and still ask the role alone (ledger POST-A-235 moves the leave upsert behind the API); the payroll SETTINGS tables (a client's payroll switch, its statutory identity, its professional-tax registrations, the firm's slabs) are not among the seventeen and keep their own grants (POST-A-219 item 3), so the Team screen's sentence is scoped to the records it lists and no longer says every payroll table.
  - **THE SECOND LOCK IS STILL MEASURED, FROM ITS OWN EXPECTATION.** With the privilege gone the access matrix reads "no privilege" in every write cell of the seventeen tables (153 table-by-actor rows, 423 cells, all write cells and nothing else changed), so it can no longer see a policy move. `fixtures/rls_expected_second_lock.json` holds what the matrix held for those tables before 499, and `test_the_second_lock_decides_when_the_first_is_lifted` gives the privilege back in a copy of the world (the migration's rollback) and asks every actor again; a no-database test holds that file to exactly the tables the migration revokes. The two controls that re-open 485 and 490 apply 499's rollback first and are measured against that file, because against the main expectation eight of their named cells went red from the privilege alone and proved nothing about the policy rollback. The per-person grid keeps the probe that a block on `payroll:write` stops a direct insert by row security (with the privilege given back) and adds the one that, with the privilege in place, an assigned Manager even WITH an explicit grant is refused by the privilege and still reads what exists.
  - **THE MODULES THAT MEASURE THE POLICIES GIVE THE PRIVILEGE BACK IN THEIR OWN THROWAWAY DATABASE** (`tests/_signed_in_payroll_writes.give_the_signed_in_writes_back`, which applies 499's rollback file, so it cannot drift from what the migration took away): with the privilege in place every signed-in write is refused before a policy or a trigger is asked, so the employee portal, attendance, release-log, one-off-earnings, 498-trigger and role-write-policy modules would otherwise measure nothing. The first full run after the migration showed it: six modules went red.
  - **THE RULE IS HELD, NOT THE LIST.** A test with no database reads the browser's direct writes and requires the payroll ones to be exactly the three (a new screen that writes a fourth fails and has to decide in the open); a real-database test requires that the payroll-family tables on which a signed-in role EFFECTIVELY holds a write privilege are exactly those three, whatever a later migration adds with an explicit GRANT (naming itself); the migration is shown to remove what a hosting platform's defaults give (TRUNCATE, `anon`), a write reaching a role through PUBLIC and one through a column grant, and to FAIL when a grant made by another grantor leaves a write in place; and the rollback control re-opens all seventeen. **THE GUARD'S LIMIT:** the migration-built database carries none of the hosting platform's default privileges, so the rule does not see the grant a new table would get from those defaults in production; a new payroll table needs its own REVOKE in its migration.
  - **THE TEAM SCREEN'S SENTENCE CHANGED A THIRD TIME.** It said a block on writing payroll is enforced by the database for all of the payroll tables except attendance, leave and the tax declarations. It now says a block on writing payroll is applied by the PracticeSync server, where those records are written; that the database refuses a signed-in browser session any write to the employee, run, payslip, attendance, salary, loan, settlement, bonus, remittance and ECR-filing records; and that leave balances and tax declarations can still be saved from the browser and are judged by the person's role and not by this block. The payroll settings tables are named only in the comment above the sentence, not in the sentence.
  - **WHAT IT DOES NOT DO.** `leave_balances` is still upserted from the attendance screen over PostgREST, and the declaration tables are written from the browser only by the employee, on their own declaration (Rule 26C makes Form 12BB the employee's own statement, which is why 297 admits them to their own unverified declaration); staff write declarations through the payroll router with the privileged client. Both browser writes still ask the role alone. Moving the leave-balance upsert behind the API is the ledger line this leaves (POST-A-235); the declarations stay by design. The `payroll_run_transitions` log is now unwritable from a session, so its INSERT policy is the second lock only.

- **A KNOWLEDGE-BASE VERSION FOLLOWS ITS ARTICLE, AND HISTORY IS NEVER REWRITTEN BY A SIGNED-IN SESSION** (POST-A-009, migration 501, 17-10-2026). `knowledge_article_versions` is where an article's CONTENT lives (`knowledge_articles` holds the title, the scope and a pointer). Migration 073 gave it one policy, `firm_id = get_my_firm_id()`, and granted `authenticated` SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE; migrations 079 and 483 put the rules that matter (a client-scoped article is visible to a Partner, a Manager and staff assigned to the client; the practice's own internal-client articles are Partner-only; writing takes `knowledge:write`, Manager and above) on the ARTICLE table alone. So a signed-in session on its own JWT, which is how the API itself reads them (`services/knowledge_service` is on `db_provider.request_db`), could read the text of an article it could not see, and any member of the firm could UPDATE or DELETE a history row: the "immutable history, a restore writes a new version" promise lived in the API only. Migration 501 does three things. **The privilege:** `authenticated` keeps SELECT and INSERT (the API only reads and appends) and loses the rest; `anon` and PUBLIC lose everything; `service_role` is untouched. Foreign-key cascades from the article, the client and the firm still remove the versions, which is not a privilege of the caller. **The visibility:** a RESTRICTIVE policy for every command requires that the parent article is visible to the caller, as a subquery that runs under the article table's own policies, so the assignment scope, the internal-practice Partner-only rule and the firm rule apply to versions WITHOUT being copied, and a later change to who may see an article moves the versions with it (including whatever the Manager-scope decision for the knowledge base becomes). **The write tier:** a RESTRICTIVE INSERT policy asks `my_permission('knowledge', 'write', 'Manager')`, the call migration 483's article policies make, so a per-person grant or block in the access grid is honoured in both places. A closing check of the EFFECTIVE privileges and of both policies fails the migration, and so the deploy. **Not in the access matrix, deliberately:** that matrix is the tables the BROWSER reaches (its table list is the parser's, and a test holds the two equal), and nothing in `apps/web` names this one; `tests/test_501_..._pg.py` is the proof (an Executive assigned to one client reads the versions of the firm article and that client's and nothing else; a Manager reads every client's and not the practice's own; a Partner reads the firm's four and the other firm's Partner none of them; an Executive cannot append even to an article they can see; nobody signed in updates, deletes or truncates; the migration's own rollback puts every hole back). Production held no article and no version on 17-10-2026, so no existing row changed who could see it. NOT VERIFIED: the API's knowledge routes were not driven end to end on a real user JWT after the change (what is held is the policy and privilege behaviour on a real database); nothing in the browser calls the table.

- **SEVENTEEN POLICIES ONLY PRODUCTION HAD ARE DROPPED, AND NOTHING THAT WORKS TODAY RELIED ON THEM** (POST-A-015, migration 502, 17-10-2026). The 13-09-2026 production snapshot held seventeen policies no migration declares (migration 319 deliberately declared none): seven `firm_iso_ai_*` and ten `firm_iso_wf_*`, all PERMISSIVE, for every command, for PUBLIC, with a predicate on `current_setting('app.current_firm_id', true)` — a setting nothing in this product sets, so each is `firm_id = NULL` and admits nothing. They were harmless and a trap: invisible to anyone reading the migrations, missed by a `DROP POLICY` by declared name, and they start admitting another firm's rows the moment anything (a function, a pooled connection) sets that setting (`tests/test_502_..._pg.py` shows it: a signed-in member who sets `app.current_firm_id` to another firm's id reads that firm's rows while one exists, and none after the migration). All seventeen were confirmed present in production's catalogue on 17-10-2026, and the replacement was checked table by table before dropping: fifteen tables keep their real `firm_id = get_my_firm_id()` policy (for `workflow_action_logs` and `workflow_steps` the same through the parent row; `workflow_templates` keeps its system-template branch), so the dropped policy was an OR beside it that admitted nothing. **The two exceptions are `workflow_conditions` and `workflow_triggers`, whose ONLY policy in production was the `firm_iso_wf_*` one** (it admitted the conditions and triggers of SYSTEM templates to any signed-in member): migration 062 declares both fail-closed on purpose ("no permissive policy is added... reachable only through the service-role backend"), and no code reads either table (a search of `apps/api` and `apps/web` finds only a docstring), and a database built from the migrations does not even have them, so dropping makes production what 062 says and removes a read nobody uses. The migration is `DROP POLICY IF EXISTS` for each and a closing check that fails the deploy if any `firm_iso_*` policy is left; the rollback recreates the seventeen exactly as production held them (its predicates were read from the catalogue; a table that does not exist in the database it is run against is skipped, which is why the proof creates those two tables itself). NOT VERIFIED: nothing was run against production; the schema-drift fixtures (`tests/fixtures/production_guards_2026-09-03.json`) still list the seventeen because they are point-in-time snapshots, and the test that compares them asserts only the directions that break something.

- **AN APPROVAL REQUEST IS CLAIMED BEFORE ITS ACTION RUNS, SO TWO DECISIONS CANNOT BOTH ACT** (POST-A-007, 17-10-2026). The maker-checker engine (`services/approval_service`, table `approval_requests`, migration 083) read a request, checked it was pending, ran the registered handler (a user created, a role changed, an assignment moved) and only then wrote `approved` unconditionally. Two approvals landing together both passed the check and the handler ran twice, and a rejection or a cancellation could land after an approval whose action had already run and overwrite it. The decision is now CLAIMED first: `approval_repo.decide_if_pending` is one `UPDATE ... WHERE status = 'pending'` and returns the row to exactly one of two simultaneous callers; the loser is refused with 409 and runs nothing. `approve` claims, then runs the handler, then records its result. `reject` and `cancel` use the same claim, so while an approval's action is running the request already reads `approved` and a rejection is refused. No migration: the status vocabulary is unchanged (a separate `approving` state would have needed one), at the cost that a request reads `approved` for the moments its action runs. **WHAT THIS DOES NOT PROMISE, STATED BECAUSE THE FIRST DRAFT OF THE HEADLINE OVERSTATED IT.** (1) If the handler RAISES (`except Exception`, deliberately not `BaseException`, so an interpreter shutdown or a cancellation is not turned into a database write on the way out) the request is put back to `pending` with the decider cleared, so the approval can be tried again and the failure reaches the caller unchanged; a handler is one repository call or two and, as before the claim existed, a failure part-way leaves what it already wrote, so the retry is the same attempt again and not a guarantee of "once". If even the put-back fails, the log says so at ERROR. (2) If the PROCESS DIES between the claim and the result being written, or the result write itself fails, the request stays `approved` with no result for good, and the API answers a second approval with 409: nothing re-runs it. Which of "the action ran" and "it did not" is true for such a row is not recorded, and an `approving` state with a lease would be the fix (a migration; not built). **THREE REQUEST TYPES WERE RETIRED IN THE SAME CHANGE** (found by the independent review of this diff): `coa_create`, `coa_update` and `coa_delete` called `domain.accounting_service`, the in-memory chart that `routers/accounting.py` was already moved off because a change made there "was reported as saved and was not", so an approved request recorded success and wrote no account. Nothing raises one (no screen calls `api.approvals.create`, no job does), so nothing real was lost; `create_request` refuses them (400), a stored one cannot be approved (409, before the claim, so it stays pending) and can still be rejected or cancelled (`RETIRED_TYPES`). **THE APPROVALS SCREEN SAID MORE THAN IT DID**: its subtitle read "Sensitive actions (user & role changes, client assignments, master Chart-of-Accounts changes) require Partner approval", and nothing in the product makes any of those wait for an approval: the users and assignments routes act on their own role checks and `api.approvals.create` has no caller. It now says what the screen does (a Partner approves or rejects requests for user and role changes and client assignments), and a refused decision (somebody decided first) reloads the list and shows the message beside it, where it used to hide the list and leave a stale row. **DECIDED, NOT CHANGED:** a Partner who raised a request can still approve it, because a rule that maker and checker must differ would lock a single-partner firm out of its own governance; a firm with two Partners that wants the rule needs a setting, not a default, and that is not built. `tests/test_an_approval_is_decided_once.py` drives two real threads against the mock store (whose claim is under a lock standing in for the statement's atomicity), checks the conditional update on the table-shaped double, forces the empty claim on all three paths, and pins the retired types; the threaded and empty-claim tests fail against the old service. NOT VERIFIED: two concurrent requests over HTTP against the real table (PostgREST's `Prefer: return=representation` on an update that matches no row answers `[]`, read from the pinned `postgrest` 0.18.0, not observed); what is held is the statement's shape and the service's use of it.

- **A CLIENT'S DOWNLOAD OF THEIR OWN FEE DOCUMENT READS THE PRACTICE'S ROWS WITH THE PRIVILEGED CLIENT AFTER OWNERSHIP IS PROVED, AND THE ROWS IT CAN READ STAY ON THEIR OWN SIGN-IN** (POST-A-236, POST-A-237, POST-A-238, 17-10-2026). The portal serves two PDFs the practice issues to its client: the fee statement and a fee invoice. Under `USE_USER_JWT` (the production default) both ran every read on the portal contact's own sign-in, and a portal contact has no `users` row, so row security hides the internal client's `clients` row, the invoice lines and the e-invoice record from them. The statement read its letterhead from `clients`, found nothing and answered 500 "PDF generation failed"; the invoice read its supplier from `clients` and answered 404 for an invoice the client owns. Migration 443 had recorded the first as "flagged, not fixed" and left a test asserting the failure. Now `portal_data_service.statement_pdf` reads the letterhead with the privileged client (`holder_db`) once `resolve_fee_scope` has proved the contact's own client, and `invoice_pdf_service.get_sales_invoice_pdf` takes `rest_db` for the supplier, the lines and the e-invoice record, while the invoice row and its customer are still read on the contact's own sign-in, so row security stays a second check on whose invoice it is (another client's invoice is not found even with the privileged client handed in). The reads that need the privileged client are the ones a real-Postgres test shows the contact cannot make; `tests/test_a_client_downloads_their_fee_documents_pg.py` runs the real functions through a duck-typed client that sends each query as the contact, produces both PDFs, and reads the line description and the IRN off the rendered page. **The same review found the year-to-date on a payslip counted EVERY run on the staff download** (POST-A-238): a draft or review run has deducted nothing (PAY-04), so an earlier month still unreleased put tax nobody withheld into a figure that has to agree with Form 16, and the same slip read differently on the employee's download, where row security shows only released runs. `load_ytd` counts `PAYROLL_RELEASED` runs plus the run the document itself is for (`include_run_ids`), because a watermarked draft payslip is a preview of its own month. **The same review found that making the invoice PDF work would have turned a DRAFT fee invoice's download from a 404 into a tax invoice carrying a placeholder number, and that the portal's invoice LIST had always shown drafts** (the policy and the service had no status filter): the portal now serves only what the client has been given (`portal_data_service._ISSUED_TO_THE_CLIENT`: issued, part-paid, paid, or cancelled after it was issued), in the list and in the ownership gate, as an allow-list so a status added later stays hidden until someone decides it belongs to the client. **The same list also stated the amount owed without the credit and debit notes** (found by the independent review of the staff preview, POST-A-244): `collections_service.assess_invoice` computes what is owed as total + debit notes - paid - credit notes, but `list_invoices` selected only `total_paise` and `paid_paise`, so the figure fell back to total - paid. In the states the product can reach, a PART-credited invoice (`partially_paid`) was owed and overdue at its pre-credit amount, and a FULLY credited one (the credit-note route marks it `paid` once paid + credited covers the total) was listed with its full amount in the Outstanding column against a Paid badge; a cancelled invoice also printed total - paid as outstanding, because the figure reads no status, and `safe_invoice` says 0 for it now. It selects `debit_note_paise` and `credited_paise` (`tests/test_a_clients_dues_are_net_of_credit_and_debit_notes.py` drives it through a double that projects columns the way PostgREST does, which the shared double did not; all eight of its tests fail against the previous select). NOT DONE: the e-invoice and statement PDFs the staff send by mail are unchanged (they run on a staff sign-in); the privileged reads are keyed on the firm and on ids the ownership gate has already vouched for, and nothing here widens what a portal contact can SELECT.

- **A SYSTEM WORKFLOW TEMPLATE IS READ BY EVERY FIRM AND WRITTEN BY NONE OF THEM, AND A USING-ONLY POLICY IS ALSO ITS OWN CHECK** (migration 503, 17-10-2026; ledger POST-A-249, found by the independent review of the copilot-prompt change POST-A-047). Migration 157 gave `workflow_templates` the policy `firm_isolation`: PERMISSIVE, FOR ALL, `USING (firm_id = get_my_firm_id() OR is_system = true)` and **no `WITH CHECK`**, and `workflow_steps` the same shape through its template (`firm_isolation_via_template`). For INSERT and UPDATE Postgres uses the USING expression as the check, so the second half admitted any row that SAYS it is a system template: a signed-in member of ANY firm could insert a template with `is_system = true` under any `firm_id` (including another firm's), and update or delete a system template every firm reads, or write the steps beneath one. The repository's own read is `firm_id = mine OR is_system = true` (`get_analytics`, `list_templates`), so a planted row appeared in every other firm's template list, in its workflow analytics and in the prompt its copilot's workflow report builds from them. The second policy on the table, `workflow_templates_firm_isolation` (migration 062, `firm_id = get_my_firm_id()` both ways), is right for the firm's own rows and did not stop an own-firm row being written WITH `is_system = true`, which the shared read then published. Production's catalogue, read on 17-10-2026, held both policies exactly so, `authenticated` held INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on both tables, and there was no workflow template at all; no code path writes `is_system = true` as a signed-in session (the repository's create paths write false; the rows with true are mock data), so nothing that works today moves. **What 503 does.** It drops the two policies and replaces them with: a SELECT-only policy for system templates (a member reads their own firm's rows through 062's policy and any system row through this one); three RESTRICTIVE policies on `workflow_templates` (INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE) requiring `is_system = false`, so whatever permissive policy admits a row, a system row is never inserted, updated, deleted, or made by an update; on `workflow_steps`, a read policy through the template (own firm or system) and INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE policies that require the template to be the member's own firm's and not a system one (a step cannot be moved onto another template either, because the UPDATE check is asked of the new row). It ends with a check that fails the migration, and so the deploy, if a permissive policy that admits `is_system = true` to a write remains on either table or the three restrictive policies are not there. `service_role` bypasses row security and is untouched, which is the seed path for a system template. **Held by** `tests/test_503_..._pg.py`: sixteen real-database probes run as `authenticated` with a member's own JWT claim (a member reads their own and the system templates and steps and nothing of another firm's; cannot plant a system template under their own firm or another's, write another firm's template, change or delete a system template, turn their own into a system one, or write the steps of a system or another firm's template; still creates, edits and deletes their own firm's templates and steps; `service_role` still writes a system row), plus the migration run twice, its closing check shown to fail on a planted permissive door, and the negative control: the migration's own rollback re-opens the cross-firm plant and the hijack and re-applying the migration closes them again. **What it does NOT do (POST-A-248):** a member can still write their own firm's templates, runs, approvals and failures without `rbac()`, because the grants stay: the workflow repository writes with `db_provider.request_db`, which on a request under `USE_USER_JWT` is the caller's own session, so the privilege cannot be revoked until the repository's writes move to the privileged client, with a parent-ownership check on the methods that address `workflow_steps` and `workflow_action_logs` by a child id alone.

- **THE AI TABLES ARE WRITTEN BY THE API ALONE, A CONVERSATION IS ITS OWNER'S, AND NOBODY CAN AUTHOR ANOTHER MEMBER'S REPLAYED HISTORY** (POST-A-250, migration 505, 18-10-2026). `ai_conversations`, `ai_messages`, `ai_summaries`, `ai_recommendations`, `ai_feedback`, `ai_actions` and `ai_context_windows` were granted INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE to `authenticated` by migration 095 (and `ai_recommendations` again by 287), and `repositories/ai_copilot_repository.py` wrote them through `db_provider.request_db`, which under `USE_USER_JWT` (the production default) is the caller's own session. A firm-isolation row policy was the only control, so any signed-in member of a firm, whatever the role, could write another member's conversation or plant an `assistant` message in it, and `AICopilotService._build_messages` replays the last eight stored turns into the next prompt as the model's own. The same hole had a second face on the API path: the v2 router found a conversation by firm and id alone, so any member who knew an id could read it, post into it or archive it. **What changed.** Every write in the repository goes through `_write_db()` (the privileged client) and every read stays on `_get_db()`; every update names its firm (the title update had no firm filter at all and had leaned on row security), `get_conversation` and `archive_conversation` take the owner (`ai_conversations.user_id` holds the caller's SIGN-IN id, the way `audit_log.actor_id` does and `created_by` does not), the router passes the caller on get, archive, send and feedback, and `increment_message_count` is called with the privileged client (migration 486's wrapper still asks whose conversation it is for a signed-in caller). Migration 505 then revokes INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC on the seven tables and checks the effective privileges (table, column and through PUBLIC) before it finishes; reading, `service_role` and the row policies are untouched. `tests/test_the_ai_tables_are_written_only_with_the_privileged_client.py` reads the repository's AST (every write rooted at `_write_db()`, every read at `_get_db()`, every update filtered by firm), drives a read client that refuses every write, and checks the owner on each route, each guard mutation-tested; `tests/test_505_..._pg.py` proves the privilege on a real Postgres (a firm Manager is refused with "permission denied for table X" on each of the seven, `service_role` keeps its writes, `increment_message_count` still runs for the service role and for the conversation's member, the closing check fails the migration when a grant cannot be revoked, and the rollback puts every statement back through). **Not done, and ledgered:** `ai_insights` and `ai_memory_triggers` carry the same migration-095 grants but their routes write with the caller's session, so revoking first would break them (POST-A-257, done afterwards by migration 507; see the entry after this one); the `workflow_*` tables are POST-A-248; and **a signed-in member can still READ every conversation of their firm straight from the database** (select grant, firm-isolation policy): the owner rule is the API's, and tightening the policy to the owner is POST-A-258. The repository's AST scan follows a chain that starts at `_get_db()` or `_write_db()`; a client held in a variable and written through later would be invisible to it (there is none).

- **THE LAST FROZEN DEVIATION OF THE ROLE-BY-TABLE MATRIX IS CLOSED: A SUSPENDED MEMBER DOES NOT READ OR RENAME THEIR OWN `users` ROW** (POST-A-013, migration 506, 18-10-2026). Migration 468 made a suspended member (`is_active = false`) and a session signed out on purpose (the JWT's `iat` before `sessions_revoked_at`) nobody to the database by teaching `get_my_firm_id()`, `get_my_role()` and `get_my_user_id()` to answer NULL. Two policies did not go through a helper: migration 153's `users_own_row_select` and `users_own_row_rename` compared `auth_user_id` with `auth.uid()` directly, so a suspended member still read their own row (role, firm, e-mail, the revocation timestamp) and could still rename themselves (the one write the column grant of 246 leaves a signed-in role). The exposure was small (own row only) and the matrix had recorded it as `suspended-member-keeps-their-own-user-row`, two cells over one table, but it broke the rule that a suspended member reaches nothing but reference data. **Before changing it, what needs that row was checked and nothing does:** the API's sign-in path reads it with the service role and refuses a suspended member itself (403), and no screen reads `is_active` or `sessions_revoked_at` of the signed-in member over PostgREST. **What changed:** both policies gain `id = (SELECT public.get_my_user_id())` beside the sign-in-id test. They cannot call `staff_session_is_live` directly, because migration 468 revoked it from every role a request arrives as (which is why the helpers exist), so the liveness rule is still stated in exactly one place. Derived from the only migration that defines them (153; 246 and 287 mention them and do not redefine them), and production holds the same two policy names (permissive, `authenticated`). `tests/test_506_..._pg.py` proves a live member reads and renames their own row and no other, a suspended member reads and renames nothing (an UPDATE that matches no row, not an error), a signed-out session is refused when the token predates the revocation, admitted when it follows it and refused when the token carries no readable `iat`, the two policy names are the only ones, the migration is idempotent and its rollback brings the leak back. **The matrix's `DEVIATION_CLASSES` is empty now**, which is the goal: every one of its 3,560 cells agrees with the five rules. Its machinery stays on purpose (it is how a gap found later is written down with a ledger line in the commit that finds it, and deleting it would touch the expected-file writer and four tests of a guard CI runs on every migration), and the assertion that used to demand a non-empty list was removed with the last entry; `test_the_matrix_turns_red_when_a_known_hole_is_put_back[suspended-own-row-506-reopened]` puts this one back on purpose.

- **THE INSIGHT AND MEMORY TABLES FOLLOW THE SAME RULE, AND THREE OF THEIR SEVEN DO NOT EXIST ON A DATABASE BUILT FROM SCRATCH** (POST-A-257, migration 507, 18-10-2026). Migration 505 locked the seven copilot tables; six more carried the same migration-095 grants (060 for the profiles, 320 for the history) and were written with the caller's own session: `ai_insights` (the insight generator, and `PATCH /api/insights/{id}/status`) and everything `repositories/memory_repository.py` writes, `ai_memory_triggers`, `client_profiles`, `client_profile_history`, `firm_profiles` and `year_end_reports` (`pattern_anomalies` was granted SELECT alone, so a signed-in write to it already failed and only a job could write it; it is in the list so the migration's own check asserts it). No screen writes any of them (the ai-insights screen READS `ai_insights`, which the `select` grant keeps), so any signed-in member could change another member's insight status, resolve a trigger, rewrite a client's compliance profile or plant a year-end report directly over PostgREST. **What changed:** both repositories write through `_write_db()` (the privileged client) and read through `_get_db()`; every write names its firm and an update addresses a row by firm AND id (the profile retire step and its snapshot read gained the firm filter they had leaned on row security for); an insight's `update_status` takes the firm as a REQUIRED argument on the database path (it was optional) and `create` refuses a row with none; migration 507 revokes INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC on the seven and checks the effective privileges before it finishes. **The trap worth recording:** migration 070 creates `ai_memory_triggers`, `pattern_anomalies` and `year_end_reports`, and it is on the runner's `BASELINE_FAILURES` list (070, 071 and 095 among others stop part-way on a database built from scratch, and the harness tolerates it), so on a scratch database only four of the seven exist while production has all seven. A plain `REVOKE ... ON public.ai_memory_triggers` therefore failed the migration on exactly the database the required `migration apply` check builds, while it would have applied cleanly in production. 507 asks `to_regclass` for each table first (the rollback too), and its real-Postgres test follows what exists (`_present`) and SKIPS the three absent tables' probes by name rather than passing on nothing: **those three tables' revoke is therefore proved on production after the merge, not by the scratch-database test** (their writers are covered by the AST scan and the behavioural tests, which do not need the table). Any future migration that names a table 070, 071 or 095 builds must do the same. `tests/test_the_ai_tables_are_written_only_with_the_privileged_client.py` now scans all three repository files (every write rooted at `_write_db()` or a local bound to it, every read at `_get_db()`, every update filtered by firm on its OWN chain when written inline), drives each writer against a read client that refuses writes, and four source mutations of it fail as they should.

- **THE WORKFLOW TABLES TAKE NO WRITE FROM A SIGNED-IN SESSION, A WRITE THAT NAMES A TEMPLATE OR A RUN BY ITS ID ASKS WHOSE IT IS, AND A CONVERSATION IS READ ONLY BY THE MEMBER WHO HAD IT** (POST-A-248, POST-A-258, 18-10-2026; migrations 509 and 510). **(1) Migration 509** revokes INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC on the eleven `workflow_*` tables (`workflows`, `workflow_templates`, `workflow_steps`, `workflow_instances`, `workflow_approvals`, `workflow_executions`, `workflow_failures`, `workflow_schedules`, `workflow_triggers`, `workflow_conditions`, `workflow_action_logs`) and checks the EFFECTIVE privileges, as 499, 505 and 507 do. Read on production on 17-10-2026, `authenticated` held all four on every one of them with a permissive firm-isolation policy on nine, so any signed-in member of a firm, whatever the role, could edit a template, change a run, mark an approval decided or forge a failure row over PostgREST without `rbac()` (a forged decision runs nothing, because the approval's action runs only through the API, but the row and the audit trail lie). It is not the payroll shape: `repositories/workflow_repository` took `db_provider.request_db`, which under `USE_USER_JWT` (the production default) IS the caller's session, so the build moved the repository's WRITES onto the privileged client (`_write_db`, 26 sites, derived from the source by `tests/test_the_workflow_tables_are_written_only_with_the_privileged_client.py`, which also fails any other module that writes a workflow table) and left READS on the caller's session and row security. **Taking row security away as the second lock means each write now does what the policy did.** `workflow_steps` and `workflow_action_logs` carry no `firm_id`, so for them the PARENT row was the only firm check: a write that names a template (`_insert_steps`, `_replace_steps`, `_create_step`, `_update_step`, `create_instance`, `create_schedule`) or a run (`log_action`, `update_action_log`, `log_execution`, `log_failure`, `create_approval`) by its id asks first, through the privileged client, that the template or run is the firm's (`_assert_owns_template`, `_assert_owns_run`, one function per table so the firm-scope reader can see the table name), and a write that names no firm is REFUSED with `WorkflowOwnershipError` and not let through. `_replace_steps` asks before it writes anything, because a replacement with no new steps inserts nothing and its removal still addresses the old steps by template id alone. `update_action_log` names its run as well as the log id, so a log id from another run is not touched. `update_schedule_run` addressed a schedule by id alone and the scheduler calls it with no request token; it takes the firm now (the two frozen firm-scope lines for it are deleted), and the engine and the scheduler pass the firm they hold (asserted from their source). **(2) Migration 510** adds one RESTRICTIVE SELECT policy to `ai_conversations` (`user_id = auth.uid()`: the column holds the SIGN-IN id the API stores, not `users.id`) and one to `ai_messages` (its conversation is the caller's), ANDed with the firm-isolation policies that stay, so a member's own JWT no longer reads another member's chat, including the client-scoped ones the API would refuse. DECIDED: a Partner does not read others' conversations, as the API does not allow it today; a standing firm-wide reader of who asked the copilot what is a surveillance channel nobody agreed to, and a firm that needs it gets a deliberate, audited route. **Deploy order:** the migration job runs as soon as the tests pass while Render rebuilds, so for the minutes the OLD API still runs against the revoked grants a workflow write from a request fails with 42501; the new API works either side of the migration. **Verified:** 36 Python tests (the source guard, a negative control of it, 11 refusals of another firm's template or run that write nothing, 6 refusals of an unnamed firm, the owner's writes, the log-through-its-own-run and schedule-firm cases), 11 + 12 real-Postgres tests (effective privileges on every table that exists on a scratch database, a Manager's INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE refused by the PRIVILEGE on each, the policies untouched and the migration idempotent, and each rollback restoring the old behaviour so the refusals were the privilege and the policy). **Not verified:** nothing was driven through PostgREST with a real signed-in token (the proof is the privilege and the policy in a real database, as for 499, 505 and 507), and the screens that start a workflow run were not driven in a browser. **Closed afterwards (migration 512, the entry below; POST-A-260):** the policies were read on production and each of the five was firm-wide for a member's own session. `ai_context_windows`, `ai_summaries`, `ai_feedback` and `ai_actions` are no longer readable by a signed-in session at all, and `ai_recommendations` stays readable on purpose because its assignment-scope policy narrows it.

- **NO SIGNED-IN SESSION READS THE FOUR AI TABLES THAT HAVE NO READER OF THEIR OWN, AND THE CLIENT ACTIVITY FEED REFUSES A REWRITE OR A DELETE EVEN FROM THE API** (POST-A-260, POST-A-016, 18-10-2026; migration 512). **(1) The tables.** The read policies of the five AI tables migration 510 left unread were read from production on 18-10-2026: each carries one permissive `FOR ALL TO public USING (firm_id = get_my_firm_id())` policy and `authenticated` held SELECT, and `ai_recommendations` also carries a RESTRICTIVE assignment-scope policy (`can_access_client(client_id)`). The one that mattered is `ai_summaries`: it caches the executive dashboard and the compliance summary for an hour under a key built from the asker's client scope, and a Partner's firm-wide answer is stored under NO scope, so a member reading the table with their own sign-in read the Partner's row, which names every client (`churn_signals`) and carries the practice's own revenue figure that the product withholds from everyone but a Partner everywhere else (G1). The cache key keeps the API from SERVING one scope's answer to another; it never applied to a direct read of the table. **A row policy cannot narrow it**: the scope key is a digest of the asker's allowed client ids computed in Python (`AICopilotService._scope_key`), and re-deriving it in SQL would be the second copy of the scope rule the posting-kernel rule exists to prevent. So the privilege goes instead, as in 499, 505 and 507: migration 512 revokes ALL (SELECT, REFERENCES and TRIGGER, which 505 left, as well as the write verbs) on `ai_context_windows`, `ai_summaries`, `ai_feedback` and `ai_actions` from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC, and checks the EFFECTIVE privilege (table and column level, through PUBLIC or a membership). `ai_context_windows` (the data injected into a conversation, so the conversation's content in another shape), `ai_feedback` and `ai_actions` (no writer since 1 October 2026) have no reader anywhere, so none gets a policy. `ai_summaries` has exactly one reader, `AICopilotRepository.get_summary`, which now reads through `_summary_read_db()` (the privileged client) and still names the firm, type and freshness; the repository scan allows `_get_db` for every other read and `_summary_read_db` for that table alone, and a second test fails if a second reader of the table appears anywhere in the API. **`ai_recommendations` is DELIBERATELY LEFT READABLE**: the API reads it with the caller's session and the restrictive assignment-scope policy is what narrows it to the caller's clients, a recommendation with no client being firm-level on purpose. **(2) The feed.** `client_timeline_events` was created as the append-only record and nothing in the database enforced it: 494 took `authenticated`'s table-level UPDATE and gave back `is_pinned`, but `service_role` could rewrite or delete any event. A BEFORE UPDATE OR DELETE row trigger now refuses a delete and any update except `is_pinned`, `updated_at` and a ONE-WAY soft delete (`deleted_at` and `deleted_by` going from NULL to a value; a hidden event cannot be restored or re-dated, and a soft delete cannot carry another change with it). The comparison is the whole row as JSON with those four columns removed, so a column added later is protected without anyone remembering to list it. **The table's owner is let through** (`pg_has_role(current_user, <owner>, 'USAGE')`): a back-fill such as migration 437's `financial_year` correction is a maintenance migration and runs as the owner, and on production the owner is `postgres` and neither `service_role`, `authenticated` nor `authenticator` is a member of it (read on 18-10-2026). **The trigger function runs as its INVOKER on purpose**: a SECURITY DEFINER one would see the owner as `current_user` and let every caller through. `platform_purge_firm` (the operator's erase-a-firm function) is SECURITY DEFINER and sets `session_replication_role = replica`, so erasing a whole firm is unaffected, and a test calls it. TRUNCATE is revoked from the signed-in roles (a platform default PostgREST does not expose). **Deploy order:** the new API reads the cache with the privileged client, which works either side of the migration; the OLD API reads it with the caller's session, so for the minutes between the migration applying on the main run and the new API starting, the executive dashboard, compliance summary and client intelligence answers can fail with a permission error. Nothing is lost and no data is exposed. **Verified:** 34 new tests in one file, 6 that need no database (the browser reads none of the four, the repository's `get_summary` is the only reader of the cache, nothing reads the other three, the migration and the test name the same four tables) and 28 that run against real Postgres (27 pass and one is skipped on a migration-built database, whose table has no `updated_at` column): no signed-in privilege of any kind on the four, at table or column level; `service_role` keeps its four verbs; `ai_recommendations` still readable; a Manager's own session refused a read of each by the privilege; the trigger is a per-row BEFORE UPDATE OR DELETE invoker function; a delete refused; eight kinds of rewrite refused; the pin toggle and a first soft delete allowed; restore, re-date and a soft delete carrying another change refused; the owner can still back-fill and delete; a signed-in staff member still pins and is still refused a rewrite or a delete by the privilege; a firm purge still removes its events; idempotent; and the rollback restoring the old behaviour, which is the negative control for every refusal. The repository scan gained three tests, and it fails against a summary read on the caller's session. **Not verified:** nothing was driven through PostgREST with a real signed-in token (the proof is the privilege and the trigger in a real database), and the executive dashboard was not driven in a browser after the change. **Still open:** the eight tables of POST-A-018 and the browser-written actor of a timeline event (POST-A-228).

- **EIGHT TABLES ARE WRITTEN BY THE API'S PRIVILEGED CLIENT AND BY NOBODY SIGNED IN, AND A WRITE THAT USED TO LEAN ON ROW SECURITY NOW ASKS WHAT THE POLICY ASKED** (POST-A-018, 18-10-2026; migration 513). `credit_note_allocations`, `invoice_sequences`, `scheduler_runs`, `task_dependencies`, `task_tags`, `task_templates`, `task_timeline_events` and `user_capacity` were granted write privileges to `authenticated` by migrations 095 and 287 although no file in `apps/web` touches them. Read from production on 18-10-2026 the grants were not the same on all eight: INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on `credit_note_allocations`, `user_capacity`, `task_tags` and `task_dependencies`; INSERT and UPDATE on `invoice_sequences`; INSERT on `task_timeline_events`; nothing on `scheduler_runs` and `task_templates` beyond the platform's TRUNCATE (`anon` holds REFERENCES, TRIGGER and TRUNCATE on all eight and no data privilege). A firm-isolation row policy was the only control, so any signed-in session of any role, with no `rbac()` in the way, could set the firm's next invoice number (Rule 46(b) wants a consecutive serial: a number reused is a duplicate tax invoice, one skipped is a gap to explain), write or delete the application of a credit note to an invoice, edit workload capacity, relabel a task, or forge an entry in a task's history. **Only three modules wrote them on the caller's session** (the AST of the whole API found eight such writes; the rest already used the privileged client: the scheduler's run log, the task-template repository and the branding route): the credit-note issue route (the allocation and its undo), `capacity_repository.upsert` and `task_extras_repository` (tags, dependencies, timeline events). They write through the privileged client now, and `tests/test_the_eight_tables_are_written_only_by_the_api.py` is the rule rather than a list of files: it follows each module's aliases and accessors to a fixed point and fails any write on one of the eight whose client does not resolve to `service_db`, `get_service_supabase` or an accessor that returns one (with a negative control, and the browser asserted to touch none). **Taking row security away as the second lock means each write does what the policy did.** `task_dependencies` carries no `firm_id` of its own (migration 063 scopes it through `tasks`, and its policy asked the caller's session whether the task was the firm's), so `add_dependency` and `remove_dependency` now take the firm as a REQUIRED keyword and refuse, writing nothing, unless every task named is the firm's; `remove_tag` takes the firm as a required keyword too (it was optional, and the only caller left it out); `log_event` refuses a missing firm (the column is nullable, the old policy would have refused a NULL one and the privileged client would not); `user_capacity.user_id` has no foreign key, so setting a capacity asks that the person is a member of the firm and the route answers a stranger with the same 404 as a person who does not exist; the credit-note allocation delete names its firm. Migration 513 then revokes INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE on the eight from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC and checks the EFFECTIVE privilege (table, column, through PUBLIC); reading, `service_role` and the policies are untouched. **DECIDED NOT TO DO what the ledger line also asked, the conversion of seven `TO public` policies to `TO authenticated`**: with the write verbs gone and no SELECT held by `anon`, a policy's role list decides nothing a role can do, and rewriting production's policies is the change the production-snapshot guard exists to watch. **Deploy order:** the new API works either side of the migration; for the minutes the OLD API still runs against the revoked grants a task tag, dependency or timeline event, a workload capacity edit and the application of a credit note fail with a permission error (the credit note's invoice update is then rolled back by its caller, as it is on any failure of the allocation). **Verified:** 16 new Python tests (the scan, its negative control, the browser, the credit-note writes, and the behaviour of each repository write with a session double that refuses every write beside a privileged double: a dependency naming another firm's task refused both ways round and writing nothing, a dependency removed only for the firm's task, a tag removed only for the firm named, the three required keywords, an event never written for no firm, a capacity set only for a member and a stranger answered 404) and 11 real-Postgres tests (no write privilege of any kind for a signed-in role on the eight, `service_role` keeps its three verbs, `authenticated` still reads all eight, a Manager refused INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on each by the privilege, the policies unchanged and the migration idempotent, and the rollback restoring the verbs production held so each refusal was the privilege). Against the previous code the scan finds the eight session writes. **Not verified:** nothing was driven through PostgREST with a real signed-in token, and no screen that tags a task, adds a dependency, sets a capacity or issues a credit note was driven in a browser.

- **A MISSING ROW AND A ROW IN SOMEBODY ELSE'S CLIENT SAY THE SAME WORDS, AND A RULE OVER EVERY ROUTER HOLDS IT** (POST-A-017, 18-10-2026). The status code was already the same (404, so existence is not disclosed) but the body was not: `sales_invoices._assert_invoice_scope`, `purchase_bills._assert_bill_scope`, `banking._assert_row_scope` and about twenty-five more row-addressed handlers answered "Invoice X not found" for a missing row and let `assert_client_access` say its generic "Not found" for a row in a client the caller is not assigned to, so a caller holding an id (they leak in URLs, exports and support threads) could tell a real one from an invented one by the wording. `core.authz.assert_client_access` takes a keyword `detail` (default "Not found", so every caller that names none behaves exactly as before) and each such handler now passes the SAME text its own missing-row 404 carries: 16 scope helpers (banking, compliance_ops, engagement_letters, gst_portal, lifecycle, memory_intelligence, payroll, purchase_bills, recurring_invoices, recurring_journals, recurring_purchase_bills, relationships, sales_invoices, task_recurring, workflow_builder and the shared banking row helper) and 10 handlers that look a row up inline (ais, banking x2, billing x2, compliance, income_tax, payroll, tasks). `tests/test_a_missing_row_and_a_hidden_row_say_the_same_thing.py` states the rule rather than a list of doors: in every router, an `assert_client_access` call that FOLLOWS a differently worded 404 in the same function must repeat that wording (compared by syntax, so the f-string and the literal both count). An assertion placed BEFORE any lookup runs on a client the caller supplied, says nothing about a row's existence and is not flagged. **Two functions are exempt and each says why**: `ai_copilot_v2.send_message` (the 404 is for the caller's own conversation, the assertion is on the payload's client) and `workflow_builder.manually_trigger` (the assertion is on the payload's client, not on the template looked up); a third test fails when an exemption stops being needed. The ledger line also named knowledge, tds, gst and mca. The tds, gst and mca workspaces already answer a missing row and a hidden one from one loader (`_visible_or_none` / `_load_or_none` returns None for both, and the handler words it once), so they needed no change. **Knowledge did not hold, and its difference was a status as well as a wording**: an article in a client the caller cannot see answered 403 "Not authorised for this client" (a 404 "Not found" for the internal practice's) while a missing article answered 404 "Article not found". `_load_article_or_404` now asks `_assert_client_access(hide_as=...)`, which answers a caller who may not even VIEW the client's content with the missing-row 404 and its words; a reader who CAN view an article but may not write it still gets the 403, because they can already read it. A client id the caller supplies (a client's instruction list, creating an article for a client) keeps its 403: the id was theirs, not a lookup of a row. **Verified:** 11 tests in the new file and 5 more in the knowledge scope file (the rule over all routers, a vacuity floor of 20 handlers that pass `detail=`, the exemptions still firing, a negative control for the old shape and one for the fixed shape, the not-an-oracle placement, and the three helpers the finding named driven for real with a scoped Manager: missing and unassigned give the same body, an assigned client's row is still reached, and the default wording is unchanged); against the previous code 5 of the 11 fail, among them the scan naming 28 functions, and 5 of the knowledge file's fail. Seventeen existing scope-test files substitute a stand-in for `assert_client_access`; each stand-in now accepts `detail` and raises with it, so it matches the real signature instead of hiding a change to it. **Not verified:** the wording of each of the 26 helpers was changed by a script and checked by the rule, but only three of them were driven through a request; no screen was driven in a browser. No migration; the response body of a refusal changes for a caller who could previously tell the two cases apart, which is the point.

- **A LEAVE ALLOTMENT AND A CLIENT-TIMELINE EVENT ARE WRITTEN BY THE SERVER AS THE SIGNED-IN PERSON, AND THE SERVER'S OWN TIMELINE WAS RIDING ON THE VERY GRANT BEING REMOVED** (POST-A-235, POST-A-228, migration 515, 18-10-2026). Two tables the browser wrote itself. The attendance screen upserted `leave_balances` over PostgREST, where the only rule was the ROLE, so a Manager whom a Partner had blocked from payroll:write on the Team screen could still change an employee's leave allotment. Four screens (compliance, documents, portal, the journal editor) inserted into `client_timeline_events` choosing the actor type, id and name, so a staff member could write an entry in a client's activity feed naming someone else (the audit log is a separate append-only table and was never affected). Both now go through the API and migration 515 revokes INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE on `leave_balances`, and INSERT and TRUNCATE on `client_timeline_events`, from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC, with a closing check on the EFFECTIVE privilege (table level, column level, through PUBLIC), `to_regclass` guards, and a rollback that restores what production held on 18-10-2026 (read from its catalogue: INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE on the first, INSERT on the second). **`PUT /api/payroll/leave-balance`** sits behind `payroll:write` and so behind the per-person grid, resolves the employee to a client the caller may act on (a stranger's employee answers exactly as a missing one, "Employee not found"), refuses a row of three empty boxes (it would say "recorded" and hold no entitlement; migration 428), refuses a fractional, negative or absurd day before the database sees it (the columns are INTEGER), writes the CALLER's firm, and records what it replaced in the audit log. **`POST /api/timeline/events`** has no field for the firm or the actor: the firm is the caller's, the actor type is "user", the actor id is the signed-in person's sign-in id (the convention `audit_log` uses) and the name is their full name or email; a firm or actor the browser sends anyway is ignored, so a tab left open across the deploy is not broken by it; a link must be a path inside the product (a single leading "/", so neither `//host` nor `https://` can put an outside link on a client's feed); a client the caller may not act on answers as a missing one; and a failure is a 503 with a sentence, not a swallowed warning. `TimelineEventInput` in the browser no longer has a firm, an actor or a pin to choose, so the type checker finds any caller that still names one. **The trap worth keeping**: `timeline_service` wrote with `get_supabase()`, which under `USE_USER_JWT` (the production default) is the CALLER's own session, so every server-written timeline event (they are non-fatal, so a refusal would have been a log warning and nothing else) depended on the INSERT grant this migration removes. It writes with the privileged client now (`_write_db = db_provider.service_db`, with the firm named last in the payload so the firm-scope guard sees it), and a test asserts the module never asks for the caller's session. `log_timeline_event` still swallows a failure by design;
