`record_event`, which the door uses, does not. **The Team screen's sentence** now lists leave balances among the records the database refuses a signed-in browser session to write; only an employee's own tax declaration (Rule 26C, migration 297) remains browser-written and asks the role alone. **Deploy order**: the migration job runs after the tests while Render rebuilds, so for those minutes the old API's timeline events are refused and, until the new browser build is served, a screen's timeline write and the leave save fail with a permission error; neither blocks the action (every timeline write is non-blocking, and the leave box keeps what was typed and shows the error). **Verified:** 36 mock tests (the browser writes neither table and only pins an event; each door's refusals and what it writes; the notice) of which 35 fail against the previous source; 15 real-Postgres tests (no effective write for either signed-in role at table, column or PUBLIC level, the pin and reading unaffected, an assigned Manager refused by the PRIVILEGE on four probes, platform defaults and PUBLIC and column grants removed, the closing check failing the migration when a grant by another grantor cannot be revoked, a missing table skipped, idempotent, and the rollback restoring the grants so each refusal is shown to have been the privilege); the 499 guard's set of browser-written payroll tables shrank to the two declaration tables. **Not verified:** nothing was driven through PostgREST with a real signed-in token, and neither the attendance screen nor a timeline-writing screen was driven in a browser. **Not done on purpose**: the journal editor still sends an event describing a posting the server performed (a title and description the browser chooses); the server now sets who said it, not whether the event is true, and writing that event from the posting itself is a separate change.

- **A TASK'S STATUS, ASSIGNEE, CLIENT, DUE DATE AND DELETION ARE WRITTEN THROUGH THE SERVER, AND THE SERVER NOW ANSWERS IN WORDS WHAT THE BROWSER USED TO SWALLOW** (POST-A-227, 18-10-2026). `lib/data/tasks.ts` wrote `tasks` straight over PostgREST (`updateTaskStatus`, `deleteTask`, `updateTask`, `createTask`), so the screens that matter most skipped every rule the PATCH route holds: the status machine (a completed task stays completed, review is passed through), the check that an assignee belongs to the firm (the foreign keys only require a `users` row, so a cross-firm assignee id was accepted directly), the audit log, the task timeline and the notification to a new assignee; the helpers also ignored the database's reply, so a refused write looked saved until the next load, and a delete of a task still linked to a reminder or a compliance record (`reminders.task_id` and a compliance table's `task_id` are plain foreign keys) was refused by the database with an error nobody read. The four helpers call `api.tasks.update/create/delete` now and throw the server's own sentence, and the tasks screen shows it. **What the API gained to make that possible**: `TaskUpdate.client_id` (a move needs the caller to act on the NEW client, 404 "Client not found" for one that is hidden or another firm's, audited); an explicit null clearing `due_date` and `assigned_to` and NOTHING else (absent still means leave it, so a form that sends the whole task cannot wipe a title; `model_fields_set` tells the two apart) with a blank box read as null; a value equal to what is held being no change (the same status is not a transition, which the machine would have refused, and a form sends the whole task); clearing an assignee logged as "unassigned" on the task timeline and "unassign" in the audit log; `due_date` being a calendar date or nothing (a 422 that names the field, where it used to be a Postgres date error halfway through the write, and the web test that guarded the browser's own `|| null` is rewritten around the API); create writing BOTH assignee columns, as every template- or recurrence-made task and every reassignment does (`/api/workload` reads `assignee_id` first); `GET /api/tasks/transitions`, serving the machine so a screen offers only moves the server accepts (the detail panel's status list and its move buttons read it; the server still refuses the rest in words); and **`DELETE /api/tasks/{id}`**, behind `task:delete` (Manager and above, the same tier migration 260 gave the table), 404 "Task not found" alike for a missing task, another firm's and one in a client the caller may not act on, a 409 in words for a task a reminder or compliance record still points at, the whole row written to `audit_log`, and the task's tags, dependencies (either direction) and timeline events removed with it (none has a foreign key to `tasks`, migration 063, so they were orphaned by every browser delete). **A hard delete, on purpose**: `tasks` has a `deleted_at` column, but analytics, workload, capacity and health read the table without filtering on it, so stamping would leave a deleted task counted. **A behaviour change the screens now surface rather than hide**: bulk "mark complete" and the status dropdown go through the machine, so a task that is still "to do" cannot be completed in one step (todo, then in progress, then completed); a bulk action reports "N of M were not completed: <the server's first reason>" and puts the board back. **Not done on purpose**: no migration, and the signed-in write on `tasks` is NOT revoked, because the API's own `task_repo` writes with the caller's session (the row policies and the role tier of 260 are the second lock there); moving the repository onto the privileged client is a separate change with its own firm-scope audit. **Verified:** 29 mock tests (the browser writes no task row; due-date and blank-assignee validators; every PATCH rule above; create's two columns; each delete refusal and what a successful one writes and removes; the routes' tiers; the repository's firm requirement; the satellites removed with the privileged client and the firm) and the rewritten web test; the frozen delete-result allowlist in the web suite is now empty. **Not verified:** nothing was driven in a browser or through PostgREST with a real signed-in token, and a delete of a task with a real reminder was not run against a database.

- **A LOST AUTHENTICATOR IS RESET THROUGH THE APPROVAL ENGINE, BY A PARTNER WHO IS NOT THE ASKER, AND NOTHING OF IT HAS BEEN RUN AGAINST THE LIVE SIGN-IN SERVICE** (POST-A-006, POST-A-240, 18-10-2026). MFA is TOTP only and enrolled in the browser, with no backup code and no way for anybody else to take an authenticator away, so a Partner or Manager who lost a phone was locked out until someone with the sign-in service's dashboard removed the factor by hand, and that is also why MFA could not be widened to more roles or tables (POST-B-013, POST-B-023): a rule that can lock people out for good needs a way back first. It is a request type (`mfa_reset`) of the maker-checker engine, not a second mechanism (`services/mfa_reset_service`, hooked into `services/approval_service` by `prepare`, `check_decider`, `audit_extra` and `after_approval`, all optional per type). **Who asks:** anyone for THEIR OWN authenticator (the locked-out person is at the password level, which is all `POST /api/approvals` needs: the create route is deliberately NOT behind `mfa_guard`, the decide routes are, and a test holds both), a Partner for anyone's, nobody else for another person's, and asking for ANOTHER person's needs the asker's own second factor (a stolen Partner password must not be able to put a colleague's name in front of the one person who can approve it; `prepare` refuses a password-level Partner in words). **What is stored is the server's:** the browser sends a reason and either `self: true` or a `users.id`; the name, e-mail and summary a Partner reads are written from the member's own row, a person of another firm is not found, a person who never signed in has nothing to reset, one request per person waits at a time (asked of the database by person, not read out of the firm's latest hundred, and a request more than seven days old no longer blocks a new one), a suspended person has nothing to reset, and a request nobody could decide is refused when it is made, in words. **Who decides:** a DIFFERENT Partner, checked at the decision and not only at the route: the decider must be a Partner whatever the role string is spelt (`approval:approve` can be granted to a person one at a time) and at the second factor, the person whose authenticator it is never decides it (so P1 asking for P2's reset cannot let P2 approve it either), and the person who asked must still be an active member, and still a Partner for a request about somebody else. The one exception is the firm's only active Partner resetting somebody who is not a Partner, because there is nobody else, and the audit entry says `decided_by_the_asker`. The only Partner's OWN authenticator cannot be reset here (the refusal says it is a step for whoever administers the sign-in service), and a request about your own authenticator is never decided by you. A request older than seven days is refused on approval. **What approving does:** `core/auth_admin.remove_all_authenticators` lists the account's factors and deletes each (idempotent, so a retry after a partial failure finishes it), then `sessions_revoked_at` is stamped (and the sign-in check's thirty-second member cache is cleared so the stamp is seen at once) so the next sign-in is a password followed by the existing prompt to set up a new authenticator; ⚠️ **a sign-in the person already has open can carry on until its token expires on the paths that read the token directly, and every screen says so rather than claiming a sign-out everywhere**; a failure of the sign-in service answers 503, leaves the request pending, revokes nothing and records in the person's audit trail how many factors were already gone; when a reset IS approved every other request still waiting for the same person is closed, because one approved days later would remove the NEW authenticator and sign the person out again; both the request's and the person's audit entries name the decider by their auth id. **Where it is asked from:** the sign-in challenge ("Lost your authenticator app?", the only place a locked-out person is) and the Team screen's member menu (a Partner, for a member who has signed in). **On the Approvals screen** the reason is shown for this type only (another type's free text is not a reason a Partner can trust), a pending reset says how long ago it was asked and whether the asker had a second factor or a password only (`askedWithText`, from the `asker_aal` the server stored; a request from before the field is "not recorded", which is neither answer), and approving it asks first, in the danger style, telling the Partner to confirm with the person by a call or in person; a "no" sends nothing. ⚠️ **THE LIBRARY TRAPS (two, and the first version of this fixed only one):** gotrue 2.11.4 declares the delete call's parameters as `{user_id, id}` and builds its URL from `factor_id`, so a call written as its type says deletes `.../factors/None`; and its listing call validates the reply as `{"factors": [...]}` while the service answers a BARE ARRAY, so the first real approval would have failed inside the listing and answered 503 for a reason nobody could see. `core/auth_admin` therefore builds both requests itself through the admin class's own authenticated request method, reads the listing as either shape (anything else is an error and never "no factors"), and judges a delete by its status alone; a test drives the real admin class on a stand-in transport (the URL, the method, both reply shapes, an empty delete reply) and fails if a library release moves the private method it relies on. `tests/test_the_authenticator_reset_has_one_door_in_each_language.py` holds the rule that only `core/auth_admin.py` calls the factor API and that the browser spells the type once. **Not verified, and said so:** nothing was run against the live sign-in service (the container cannot sign a real user in), so the first real reset, by two real Partners, is a step for a person (rolled into PRE-B-006's rehearsal). **Deliberately NOT done:** recovery codes (a second secret to lose and to phish, and a reset a Partner decides leaves a record of who decided it); an e-mail to the person whose authenticator was reset (the firm's own mail is off unless somebody says on; the signal they get is the sign-out and the prompt); resetting the only Partner's own authenticator; and wiring the six older request types (user and role changes, assignments) to a screen, which stays a decision for when a firm asks (the engine now has a caller, so POST-A-240 is narrowed rather than closed).

- **THE DECLARATION TABLES ASK THE PER-PERSON GRID, THREE CLIENT-LEVEL PAYROLL TABLES ARE WRITTEN BY THE API ONLY, AND A PARTNER BLOCKED FROM READING PAYROLL IS REFUSED AT THE FINALISE DOOR** (POST-A-219, migration 519, 18-10-2026). Migrations 485 and 490 put the payroll read and write tier on the other payroll tables through `my_permission('payroll', ..., 'Manager')`, the function `rbac()` resolves through, so a Partner's per-person block on the Team screen (403/415) is honoured by the database as well as by the route. Four things were left. **(1) `payroll_it_declarations` and `payroll_it_declaration_items` still asked the ROLE** (`my_role_at_least('Manager')`, and `'Partner'` for a delete) in their six restrictive write policies, so a Manager a Partner had blocked from `payroll:write` could still write an employee's declaration directly. They are the one pair of payroll tables a signed-in session may still write (Rule 26C makes Form 12BB the employee's own statement and the employee has no staff principal to go through the API with), which is why 499 could not simply revoke the write. Each policy keeps its shape and its EMPLOYEE arm exactly (`employee_id IN (SELECT my_employee_ids()) AND proofs_verified IS FALSE`) and only the STAFF arm changes to `my_permission('payroll', 'write', <the same minimum role>)`: THE MINIMUM ROLE IS UNCHANGED, so with an empty `user_permissions` (production holds none) behaviour is identical and the grid only narrows or widens for the person it names. **A DELETE ASKS `payroll:finalize`, NOT `payroll:write`** (an independent read of this migration found the first version asked write): the grid has no delete action for payroll and a grant that names an action wins over the minimum role (415), so asking write would let a Partner who granted an Executive payroll write, to key monthly inputs, hand them DELETE on every declaration and by cascade its items over PostgREST, which was Partner-only before this and which no API route does. `finalize` is Partner-only by default and a Partner can still grant or deny it. TRUNCATE is revoked from the signed-in roles on the two declaration tables as well, with a closing check. **(2) Five routes that ask `payroll:finalize`** (finalize, the loan write-down, disburse, reverse and a leaver's settlement record) **also ask `payroll:read`, first**, through a route-level dependency (`_NEEDS_PAYROLL_READ`): a Partner whom the Team screen had blocked from reading payroll but not from finalising it passed the finalize guard and failed later on a message about something else. `tests/test_a_finalise_act_also_asks_for_payroll_read.py` holds the rule (every route asking finalize asks read, and asks it first, with a floor on the count, driven for real with read refused and finalize allowed, and the same request not refused with both allowed as the negative control). **The ratchet `tests/test_write_requires_write_permission.py` (no mutating route is satisfied by a read-level action) had to change with it**: it flagged any mutating route that declared a read-level action at all, which these five now do, so it flags a route when it declares only read-level actions, or a read beside actions that a Reviewer (a read-only role) also holds (`time_entry:write`, `report:export` are not write guards); a read beside an action that EXCLUDES a Reviewer (finalize, compute) is a second guard, not the route's tier. A negative control on a small app flags a POST on read alone and a POST on read plus a Reviewer-held action, and does not flag read plus finalize or write alone. The first version of this refinement only asked that the actions not ALL be read, which an independent read of it showed let a weak second action through. That is the rule the Form 24Q-from-books route was already allowlisted by hand for (tds:compute plus payroll:read); the entry is gone from `ALLOWED`, so that route is now held by the rule as well. **(3) `client_payroll_settings` (a Partner's on/off switch and the attendance due day), `client_statutory_identity` (the TAN, EPF establishment code, ESIC employer code and LIN the statements print) and `client_pt_registrations` were not audited by 485 or 490.** Read on 18-10-2026: they are READ by the payroll router, by the TDS router and by the Form 3CD and payslip services (the statement's TAN is needed by whoever holds `tds:compute`, an Executive, so a payroll read tier would be WRONG for them and reads are deliberately left as they were); they are WRITTEN only by the payroll router with the privileged client and by no screen in `apps/web`; and yet each carried firm-only reads and, for writes, only the role (a Manager the grid had blocked could change the client's TAN directly), and `client_payroll_settings` held column-level INSERT and UPDATE for `authenticated` (332 took the three enablement columns out of reach on purpose and left the rest). Migration 519 REVOKEs INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE on all three from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC, which removes the column grants with the table grant; its closing check asks the EFFECTIVE privilege (table level, column level, or reaching a role through PUBLIC) and fails the migration if a write remains; the restrictive row policies stay as the second lock, and `service_role` is untouched. ⚠️ **A test that measures a row policy or a column grant as a signed-in role on one of these tables now needs the privilege back, or every write is refused by the privilege before the policy is asked and the module measures nothing**: `tests/_signed_in_payroll_writes.give_the_client_payroll_writes_back` gives only the grants back, from the part of the rollback file below a marker line (the rollback's second half), and test 325, 326 and 332 call it after seeding; the privilege itself is measured by `test_519_*_pg.py`, so the rollback file's marker is part of the contract. ⚠️ **Known and left, found by the same independent read:** the declaration guard triggers (292 and 297) still treat `my_role_at_least('Manager')` as "staff, skip the column guard", so a Manager the grid has blocked who is ALSO the linked principal of an employee row (`payroll_employees.auth_user_id`) passes the employee arm for their own unverified declaration and the trigger then skips its column guard, which lets them write their own `*_verified_paise` columns and `status = 'verified'`. It is narrow and not a regression (it was equally possible before 519); it is recorded on POST-A-219. **(4) NOT DONE and said so:** the policies call a plpgsql function per row through `payroll_employees`, and the original line asked for `EXPLAIN` on a large firm before any reader moves onto a caller's sign-in; there is no large firm in this environment and none of these reads runs as a caller's sign-in today, so that stays an open line (POST-A-219 is reworded, not closed). Nothing signed-in writes the three tables, so no screen or API path is cut off at deploy; the five finalise routes only add a refusal for a person the grid has already blocked from reading.

- **A SCOPE CHECK BELOW A MOCK-MODE EARLY RETURN IS NOW A TREE-WIDE RULE, AND A NARROWING THAT IS BOUND MUST BE READ** (POST-A-011, 18-10-2026). `tests/test_guard_placement_client_scope.py` names two placements that turn a correct client-scope check into none: (A) the check sits AFTER an early return on a mock or no-database branch, and (B) it sits inside a `try` whose handler swallows `Exception`. B was a tree-wide scan; A was tested only for the timeline, receipts and purchase-payments routers, the three places it was found by hand. The harm of A is specific and is a test blind spot more than a production hole: `_USE_MOCK` is the mode the in-memory CI job runs in, so a guard below a mock branch is the guard no test can exercise, and a mock branch that returns STORED rows shows another client's data to the very run that is supposed to prove the guard. `tests/test_a_scope_check_is_not_placed_after_a_mock_branch_that_reads_stored_data.py` holds it over every router: an `if` at the top of a function whose condition names mock mode or no database (`_USE_MOCK`, `MOCK`, `db is None`, `not db`) and whose body returns, followed by a scope check with none before or inside it, must be INERT (it calls nothing but the response wrapper and the request's own `model_dump` and names no `MOCK_*` store, so it echoes the request or a constant and exposes nothing). Measured: 474 such branches, 80 followed by a check, none reading stored data. **What counts as a check is a closure over the MODULE'S OWN calls from the three seed names** (`assert_client_access`, `can_access_client`, `assert_partner_for_internal_id`), so a router's own `_assert_run_scope` or `_assert_engagement_client_mock` counts. ⚠️ **The first draft closed over names across the whole tree and was useless**: 2,757 names became "checks" because a common name like `get` calls one somewhere, so almost every call was a check and the rule could never fail; the module-local closure is the version that can. The price is that a guard imported under its own name from another module is not recognised, so the branch must then be inert, which every current one is. **The second rule is that a name bound from `effective_client_ids`, `filter_by_client` or `_scoped_client_ids` is READ later in the same function**, in the routers and the services (68 assignments, all read): `test_router_client_scope.py` checks call sites by NAME, so `allowed = effective_client_ids(...)` keeps satisfying it even after the code that applied `allowed` is stripped. Both rules carry floors (400 mock branches, 60 followed by a check, 50 narrowings) so a scan that stopped seeing them cannot pass, and were each shown to fail on a mutation (a mock branch returning a `MOCK_` store failed one test; a renamed, now unread narrowing failed the other). **Not held:** that a name which is read is read into the right place (a value handed to a call that ignores it satisfies the rule), which would need data flow into the callee; the per-router runtime scope tests hold the behaviour and this holds the placement. `payment_service.history()`'s second check is left as it is, because a check twice is not a gap. No behaviour changed.

- **THE APPROVAL ENGINE CARRIES ONLY A REQUEST TYPE SOMETHING RAISES, AND ITS ACTION LIVES IN ITS OWN SERVICE MODULE, NOT IN THE REGISTRY** (POST-A-240, 19-10-2026). `services/approval_service` had seven request types and six had never had a caller in any screen or job: user create, user activation, role change, and assignment create, remove and transfer. Each handler was a few lines in the engine module that wrote `user_repo` or `assignment_repo` directly, so each skipped what the direct route asks first: that the user (and for an assignment the client) belong to this firm, the invitation a new user needs, and the route's own audit entry. Worse, `user_repo.update(...) or {}` returned an empty result when the id matched no user in the firm, so the request read `approved` and nothing had happened, the same false success that retired the chart-of-accounts types on 17-10-2026. Anyone with `approval:request` (Executive and above) could raise one by a direct API call, and a Partner would see "Change role of <id> to Partner", an id and no name. **Retired rather than wired**, a decision taken under the standing instructions: wiring them is a feature for a firm that has not asked for it, and the direct routes carry their own permission checks (`team:write`, `assignment:write`) and audit. Production's `approval_requests` was read before the change (19-10-2026, read-only): no row of any type, so nothing is orphaned. **What a caller sees now:** `create_request` answers 400 with a sentence for a retired type ("no longer accepted ... made directly, with its own checks"), kept apart from the unknown-type sentence so a typo is still called unknown; a stored row of a retired type can still be rejected or cancelled and approval is refused 409 in words (`RETIRED_TYPES`, which now holds nine); `GET /api/approvals/types` lists only `mfa_reset`. **The rule, not the six names,** is held by `tests/test_the_approval_engine_does_not_carry_out_a_staff_or_assignment_change_itself.py`: no registered handler is defined in the engine module, and the engine module names no staff or assignment repository, each scanner shown to fail on source written to break it. `test_approval_service.py` and `test_approval_api.py` now drive the engine with a test-only request type, so the engine (pending until a Partner decides, the action runs once, firms are isolated, the role matrix) is held apart from any action, as `test_an_approval_is_decided_once.py` already did; the firm-scoped write the old handler test protected is held on the repository. **If a firm ever wants a second person on these acts,** build it as a request whose action lives in its own service module and calls the same function the route calls, with `prepare` validating when the request is MADE (as the authenticator reset's does), not as repository calls beside the registry. The flood and per-person cap on creating requests that POST-A-006's review noted as not done for the older types is moot: only `mfa_reset` can be raised now, and it refuses a second pending request for the same person. **Not done:** hiding the Approve button for a stored retired request (none exists in production, and the refusal is in words). No migration.
- **THE DATABASE ASKS THE BANKING TIER WHEN A CLIENT'S BANK LINES ARE READ, AND THE HEALTH SCORE AND THE GST RETURN READ THEM WITH THE SERVER'S ACCESS** (migration 524, POST-A-005 bank half, 20-10-2026). The API reserves `banking:read` to Executive and above and every banking route asks it through `rbac()`, which honours a Partner's per-person yes or no (403); nothing at the database asked it, so a Manager a Partner had blocked from Banking, and a Reviewer assigned to the client, could `select` every narration, counterparty name and amount on the client's statements over PostgREST with their own sign-in. 524 adds one RESTRICTIVE SELECT policy to each of `bank_transactions`, `bank_statements`, `bank_transaction_splits`, `bank_reconciliations`, `bank_reconciliation_matches` and `bank_matching_rules`, asking `my_permission('banking', 'read', 'Executive')` (the floor `PERMISSIONS` states, pinned by `tests/test_the_per_person_grid_reaches_the_database.py`). Postgres applies a SELECT policy to the rows an UPDATE or DELETE has to find, so a person who cannot read a line cannot change it either, and an INSERT is not exempt: the API inserts with the new row asked back (`return=representation`, which is INSERT ... RETURNING) and Postgres checks that row against the SELECT policies too, so a person who may write Banking and may not read it is refused at the database on every insert. **THE GRID CAN SAVE THAT STATE, SO THE DOOR REFUSES IT IN WORDS:** the Team drawer has one toggle per action, a Partner can untick Read and leave Write or Approve at the role default, and `rbac("banking", "write")` then passed, after which the services (which read under the caller's own sign-in) met a 404 for a line they could not see, a batch route that reported "0 changed" as a success, or the insert refusal above worded by the central speaker as a fault of the server. Every banking route now asks `banking:read` FIRST as a router-level dependency (`routers/banking.py`, the payroll finalise tier's rule from POST-A-219 applied to a whole router), so that person is told at the door, in the words every other refusal uses; `tests/test_a_banking_route_also_asks_for_banking_read.py` holds it over the route table (a floor on the routes, the dependency declared on the router so a route added later cannot forget it, and a sample of each kind driven for real with a negative control). **THE CALL IS WRITTEN `(SELECT public.my_permission(...))` AND THAT IS A MEASUREMENT, NOT STYLE:** `my_permission` is a STABLE plpgsql function that reads `user_permissions`, and a policy that calls it bare has it called once per ROW; on 100,000 rows the same `count(*)` under the same sign-in took 5.4 s with no tier policy, 61.1 s with the bare call and 6.3 s wrapped (an InitPlan, evaluated once). Migration 485 wrote the bare form on payroll tables, which hold hundreds of rows; a client's bank lines run to about 300 a month on the repository's own figure (`domain/banking/exceptions.py`), thousands a year and hundreds of thousands for a firm over a few years, and the bare form costs about 0.55 ms for every row a read scans. `test_the_banking_tier_is_asked_once_per_query_and_not_once_per_row` reads the plan a signed-in person gets for each of the six tables (an InitPlan, and no `my_permission` inside a Filter). **A READ THE TIER HIDES COMES BACK AS ZERO ROWS, NOT AS AN ERROR, SO THE READERS WERE FOUND BEFORE THE POLICY WAS WRITTEN.** Two outside the banking module ran under the caller's own sign-in for people who need not hold Banking: the health score (`accounting_quality` and the six-month hard override would have counted a blocked Manager's empty answer as a clean bank, the false-clean a failed read is already refused for) and the GST return build (`_bank_lines_declaring_gst`: a bank charge on which the CA declared GST would have been left out of the return, wrong and silent). Both read through the service client now (`routers/health._bank_db`, `_service_db() or db` in the return builder), after the route's `assert_client_access`. The health read selects an id and a date. **The GST read also selects the line's description, which the GSTR-3B drill-down names the line by.** That text is already in the narration of the line's posted journal entry (`journal_entries.narration`, "Bank: ..."), which 524 does not gate: `journal_entries` and `journal_lines` stay readable to every assigned staff role straight from the database, and over the API the ledger serves them under `accounting:read`. The read asks `posted_journal_id` not null, so the unposted lines (the ones carrying a counterparty nobody has matched to a party) are not in it. **524 hides the six TABLES, not every copy of a posted line's text, and one API route still hands that text to a person without `accounting:read`:** the drill-down (`GET /api/gst/gstr3b/detail`, `gst:read`, every staff role) names a posted line on which GST was declared by its description, for a Manager whose Banking was blocked or a Reviewer who holds `gst:read` and not `accounting:read`. Left as it is on purpose: the figure is the return's, the CA reviewing a return needs to see which line made it, and the same text is one query away for that person at the database. `tests/test_a_health_score_and_a_return_read_the_bank_lines_with_the_servers_access.py` drives them over a request client holding no rows beside a service client holding the line, and states the rule over the router's source. The accounting dashboard's "Unmatched Bank Txns" tile read the table from the browser and would have printed 0. **It now asks the server, not the table:** `GET /api/banking/unmatched-count` (behind `banking:read`, which resolves the person's grid on the request; the `match_status = 'unmatched'` count the tile always meant, not the Entries tab's `to_do`), because deciding from the browser's permission map alone left a window: the map is resolved at sign-in and on each token refresh, so a person a Partner blocked an hour ago still passed it and the database answered zero rows, a printed 0. The map now only decides whether to ASK (a person it says cannot read banking is told "Not part of your access" without a request, and the card is a plain card with no tab stop, not a button that does nothing); a refusal or any failure is a dash (`lib/accounting/unmatchedBankTile`, four states, and an answer that is not a whole non-negative figure in a successful envelope is unknown, never zero). The page no longer reads `bank_transactions` from the browser at all, which the guard script asserts. The server's own answer shares the API's 30-second cache of a person's grid with every other banking screen, which is the one window left. **`tests/test_a_reader_of_a_clients_bank_lines_is_known_and_says_whose_access_it_reads_with.py` is the durable half:** every file that reads one of the six tables is enumerated from the source into one of three kinds (the banking module behind `rbac("banking")`, the service-client readers, the two chosen readers), and a new one fails until somebody decides which; every banking route asks `banking:read` first (the router-level dependency), and the Partner-only reopen of a reconciliation (`accounting:approve`) asks it too, so a Partner who blocked their OWN Banking read is refused at the door, a refusal and not a wrong figure; the guard's list of banking-module services is not "reached only through `routers/banking.py`": `bank_posting_service.settle_on_post` is also reached from `journal_posting_service._dispatch_deferred` (the `accounting:approve` route, which runs on the SERVICE client, `routers/accounting._prod_db`, so the policy never applies to it) and `bank_entry_service` from the trusted-rules job, which the guard lists as a service-client reader. The role-by-table matrix lost one cell (a Reviewer's read of `bank_transactions`), gained a rollback control, and the 487 control now rolls 524 back first because two layers close a Reviewer's update on a bank line. **Deliberately NOT done, and the Team notice says so:** `bank_accounts`, the account master, is read from the browser by the payment pickers on receipts, vendor payments and fixed assets for people who use those screens without Banking, and the server's readers of it (`resolve_payment_account`, post-dated cheques, payroll disbursement, opening balances) run under the caller's sign-in and would see a chosen account as one with no ledger and post to the default Bank ledger, a wrong posting and not an error; gating it needs those readers moved behind an access check first. `bank_statement_column_mappings` holds column headings only. The assignment scope of the three children that carry only a firm policy (`bank_transaction_splits`, `bank_reconciliation_matches`, `bank_matching_rules`) is a separate gap, not claimed closed. `bank_posting_service.settle_on_post` (a legacy bank-origin draft approved by `accounting:approve`) is not affected: the approval route reads and settles on the service client (`routers/accounting._prod_db` is `service_db_or_none`), and the policy applies to a signed-in session only. Documents and the Storage bucket behind them stay POST-A-005's open half. Not run against production; the policies apply there when the migration merges.

- **EVERY CLIENT TABLE ASKS THE ASSIGNMENT SCOPE OR IS ON A SHRINK-ONLY LIST THAT SAYS WHY NOT, AND THE BANK'S CHILDREN TOOK IT FIRST** (migration 527, POST-A-005's third item, POST-A-299, 20-10-2026). Migration 084 gave the tables that then had a `client_id` a RESTRICTIVE assignment scope, and its loop never ran again; tables created since carry a firm policy and, unless a migration since added one, nothing per client. The guards held only the tables the BROWSER reads (`test_a_table_the_browser_reads_is_assignment_scoped_pg`) and the tables a NEW migration creates (`test_a_client_table_a_migration_creates_is_assignment_scoped`). The rest were left on a stated premise, "nothing reaches them from the browser, and the backend bypasses row security", and **both halves are false**: PostgREST answers for every table `authenticated` is granted whatever the browser reads, and the API runs under the caller's sign-in when USE_USER_JWT is on, the production default. The 14-10-2026 decision POST-B-016 ("leave them") already deferred the question to the extension of the role-by-table matrix (POST-A-008); this is the settlement for the bank's children and the rule for the rest. Twenty-five tables with a `client_id` carried a firm policy and nothing per client (the 23 still listed as pending and the two bank tables closed here; the column mappings, which did too, are left on purpose, and the matches have no `client_id`); a Manager assigned to no client could read another client's rows in them, and change them where the table takes writes, with their own sign-in. 527 closes the bank's children: `bank_transaction_splits` and `bank_matching_rules` (each with a NOT NULL `client_id`) take the two RESTRICTIVE FOR ALL policies their parents carry, the assignment scope and the internal practice's Partner-only guard (074), written in the once-per-query form of 525 and 526; `bank_reconciliation_matches` has no client and `bank_transaction_splits` has one nothing ties to its line's, so both take `<table>_parent_visible`, an EXISTS over `bank_transactions` read as the caller (so the firm, the assignment scope, the internal-practice guard and 524's banking tier all apply inside it): a match is exactly as visible as its line, and a person assigned to client A cannot write a split with client A's id onto client B's bank line. Six policies in all, each written with its name as a literal because `test_guards_match_production_pg` excuses a policy production does not hold yet only when a migration names it in plain text (the first draft built the names in a loop and would have turned the required real-Postgres check red; the independent review found it). **`bank_statement_column_mappings` is deliberately NOT scoped**: `find_mapping`'s firm-wide second look (BANK-26) reuses a layout drawn for one account on another account of the practice, for a co-operative bank no adapter knows, and a scope on reads would end that for everyone but a Partner (the first draft scoped it; the review found it). The table holds column headings and positions, not a client's figures, and its writes carry a role tier; it is an `own` entry. What is wrong beside it is that `save_mapping` updates the layout of ANOTHER account when the firm-wide look finds one instead of recording one for this account (POST-A-300). **The rule is a catalog test with a frozen list:** `tests/test_every_client_table_is_assignment_scoped_or_says_why_pg.py` asserts that every public table with a `client_id` that `authenticated` can reach (a table grant or a column grant) is SCOPED, meaning that for each command it is granted a RESTRICTIVE policy asking `my_assigned_client_ids()` or `can_access_client` covers that command or ALL, or is in `tests/fixtures/assignment_scope_exceptions.json` with a kind and a reason; a listed table that gained the scope, left the schema or no longer reaches `authenticated` must leave the list (shrink-only), a `parent` entry is verified (a policy of the table asks the parent named), an `own` entry is verified (the policy it names exists), and a `pending` entry names the ledger item that closes it. The list on 20-10-2026 holds 38: seven payroll tables scoped through `payroll_employees` and `account_period_balances` through `clients` (`parent`), `client_instructions` and `knowledge_articles` (a Manager is firm-wide in the knowledge base by design), `notifications` (the recipient's), the two reconciliation tables (a Partner's) and the column mappings (`own`), `user_client_assignments` (`source`), and 23 `pending` under POST-A-299, AIS data and the statutory identity first. The tests of 527 itself: the six policies exist and are RESTRICTIVE FOR ALL in the set form with the Partner branch in the USING and the WITH CHECK; six people (a Partner, a Manager and an Executive assigned to client A, a Manager assigned to nothing, a suspended Partner, a Partner of another firm) read 3, 1, 1, 0, 0 and 0 rows of each child table seeded for client A, client B and the internal practice; an assigned person inserts, updates and deletes their own client's rows and is refused for another's (an insert errors, an update or delete matches nothing), including a split of their own client written onto another client's line and an update that moves a row to another client or line; a Partner writes every client's rows, the internal practice included; a Manager assigned to the internal practice as well still does not read its rows; the rollback returns the firm-wide read and a re-apply hides it again; and the plan asks the caller's clients once. **Deliberately NOT done:** the 23 pending tables (a table reached by a non-staff principal, a portal contact or an employee, needs that branch kept, as 262 did for payroll, so each family is read before it is scoped), `bank_accounts` (524 says why) and the documents/Storage half of POST-A-005. Not run against production before merge: the six policies apply when the migration merges, on three small tables, under a 15-second lock timeout that fails the migration rather than queueing requests.

- **ELEVEN CLIENT TABLES ONLY THE API USES AND THREE THAT HANG FROM THEM TOOK THE ASSIGNMENT SCOPE, A ROW WITH NO CLIENT IS NOT THE PRACTICE'S, AND THE CATALOG RULE NOW FOLLOWS A CHILD TO ITS PARENT** (migration 528, POST-A-299's first tranche, POST-A-302, 20-10-2026). The catalog rule migration 527 added named 23 `pending` tables with a `client_id`, a firm policy and nothing per client; 528 closes eleven of them, the ones only the API uses and whose every reader asserts the client first: `ais_uploads`, `ais_records`, `ais_reconciliations` (the Annual Information Statement by PAN), `income_tax_asset_blocks`, `schedule_iii_ratio_explanations`, `schedule_iii_ratio_inputs`, `schedule_iii_unbilled_reviews`, `properties`, `recurring_invoice_templates`, `onboarding_checklists` and `tally_migration_jobs`. Each takes the two RESTRICTIVE FOR ALL policies the parents carry, `<table>_assignment_scope` and `<table>_internal_partner_only`, in the once-per-query form of 525 to 527, in the USING and the WITH CHECK (so a person can neither insert for a client they cannot see nor move a row to one); five tables take `<table>_parent_visible` (two of them are among the eleven and three have no client of their own: fourteen tables in all), an EXISTS over the parent read as the caller: `ais_records` (upload), `ais_reconciliations` (record), `tally_migration_items` (job; every ledger, customer and vendor parsed out of the client's Tally file), `onboarding_checklist_steps` and `recurring_invoice_runs`. 27 policies, each written with its name as a literal for the drift guard (527's entry says why). `recurring_invoice_template_lines` was left alone because its own policy already reads the template as the caller, and a test keeps that sentence true. **Rows with no client stay open to the firm**: `properties` and `tally_migration_jobs` may carry a NULL `client_id`, which the 084 form (and `can_access_client`, and the routes' `filter_by_client`) has always admitted; an assigned person can blank the client of their own row, as for every nullable-client table since 084 (the fix would be NOT NULL on those columns, not a policy). **THE INTERNAL-PRACTICE GUARD HAS TO SAY SO TOO, AND 074'S NOTE WAS TRUE OF ONLY HALF OF ITS ROWS** (found by two of the three independent reviews of this change, shown on a throwaway database): the guard compares the row's client with `firms.internal_client_id`, `NULL IS DISTINCT FROM NULL` is FALSE, and a firm whose onboarding never provisioned its internal practice (it is non-fatal; migration 405 measured one of two firms in that state) therefore had every client-less row hidden from, and refused to, every non-Partner: a Manager could not create a firm-level Tally job, and its items vanished with it. 074's comment that a NULL internal client never hides is true of a row that has a client. The two nullable tables here say `client_id IS NULL OR` in the guard's USING and WITH CHECK, and **`knowledge_articles_internal_partner_only` (migration 079), where a firm-wide article carries no client, had the identical live defect and is given the same branch by an ALTER POLICY in the same migration (its rollback restores 079's expression)**; the other nine tables have a NOT NULL client and need nothing. The test sets `internal_client_id` NULL and reads and writes the client-less rows of the three tables, and the fixture keeps the other state too. **A Tally job whose client is deleted becomes a client-less job** (`ON DELETE SET NULL`), and with it the parsed ledgers, customers and vendors under it are open to the firm (POST-A-303); before 528 every job was open to the firm. **One list changes for a member who is not a Partner, and it is the point**: `GET /api/relationships/properties` with no client asked for every property of the firm and the route did not narrow it (the recurring-template, onboarding and Tally job lists already did, through `filter_by_client`), so the intelligence page showed an Executive every client's properties; the database now hands it the caller's clients' and the client-less ones. Nothing else changes for an assigned Manager or Executive, a Partner, or the scheduler (the recurring-invoice sweep has no caller and runs with the service role). **THE CATALOG RULE NOW HAS A SECOND POPULATION, because the first could not see this class**: a table WITHOUT a `client_id` that hangs from a client table (an allocation, a line, a version, a log, a step) names no client of its own, so a rule over `client_id` columns never looked at it, and 18 of them carry a bare firm policy: `credit_note_allocations`, `debit_note_allocations`, `purchase_credit_note_allocations`, `sales_debit_note_allocations`, `customer_payment_events`, `document_extractions`, `engagement_events`, `itr_filing_versions`, `notes_to_accounts`, `onboarding_tasks`, `recurring_journal_runs`, `recurring_purchase_bill_runs`, `workflow_approvals`, `workflow_executions`, `workflow_failures`, `year_end_checklist_items`, `year_end_checklists` and `year_end_reviews` (POST-A-302; they are the `children` list of `tests/fixtures/assignment_scope_exceptions.json`, shrink-only, under the same rule). The children are found by the foreign keys, transitively (and `firms` is not followed: it points at its own internal client, and that arrow reaches every table of the firm), and what a policy reads is taken from `pg_depend`, the tables and functions its expressions refer to, never from its text (the first attempt matched `FROM parent` case-sensitively and missed the alias and the line break). **The classifier is a pure function with tests in the mock-mode job** (`unscoped_commands`): a command is safe when a RESTRICTIVE policy covering it asks the assignment scope or reads a client table (so the parent's own scope applies inside), or when no PERMISSIVE policy covering it ADMITS THE WHOLE FIRM, meaning one computed from the row, the firm and the role alone (`get_my_firm_id`, `get_my_role`, `my_role_at_least`, `my_permission`), and a command with no permissive policy is closed. **A role tier is not a scope**: a restrictive `my_permission(...)` policy beside a bare firm policy leaves the command open to every member at that tier, for every client. A policy that calls any other function (`my_employee_ids`, `my_portal_receipt_ids`) is a personal door and does not admit the whole firm. Deliberately NOT done: the firm-level usage checks that read as the caller (POST-A-303), the cross-tenant client check (POST-A-304), the twelve client tables still listed (the statutory identity, the payroll settings and the PT registrations are read by the payslip and portal PDF paths and need an employee or portal branch first; the year locks, engagements, FX, stock ledger, party credit, demo filings and portal sessions each need their readers checked), the 18 children (the next migration, in which every parent is already scoped except `engagements`, which `engagement_events` hangs from and which must be scoped first or in the same migration: a child that reads only a parent with no scope protects nothing, and the catalog now fails that), the tables that name a client without a foreign key (`task_tags`, `task_timeline_events`, `ri_cross_client_signals`, POST-A-305), and `schedule_iii_ageing`, a SECURITY DEFINER function that reads `schedule_iii_unbilled_reviews` from its caller's arguments and whose result does not change. The proof is `tests/test_the_staff_only_client_tables_take_the_clients_assignment_scope_pg.py`: for each of the fourteen tables, the policies, six people's reads (with the NULL-client row and an assignment to the internal practice), an assigned Manager's insert for their own client and refusal for another's (and a row of their client hung from another client's parent), the moves, the updates and deletes, a Partner's inserts and moves for every client, the rollback and a re-apply, a second run, and the plan of every table. The review's other findings are in the tests: a refusal that a unique key would also have given is checked with the competing row removed, the guard's and the scope's WITH CHECK are exercised by a Manager assigned to the practice, and the lists carry a ceiling so that a new gap cannot join without a constant being raised in a diff. What the child rule cannot see is written in its docstring: it reads what a policy refers to and not what it admits.

- **THE ENGAGEMENT LETTERS TOOK THE ASSIGNMENT SCOPE, TWENTY TABLES THAT HANG FROM A CLIENT'S ROW NOW FOLLOW IT, AND A LETTER NUMBER IS THE FIRM'S AND IS READ WITH EVERY LETTER IN VIEW** (migration 529, POST-A-299's `engagements`, POST-A-302, POST-A-305's two task tables, 21-10-2026). `engagements` is an engagement letter: the fee, the scope of work, the signatory and the signing link of a client, or of a LEAD who is not a client yet, which is why its `client_id` is nullable (migration 115, and the route's own comment that a letter raised against a lead is deliberately a firm-level resource). It carried a firm policy and nothing per client, so a member of the firm assigned to no client read every letter of the firm over PostgREST and could change or delete one or raise one for a client that was not theirs. 529 gives it the two RESTRICTIVE FOR ALL policies of the other client tables in the once-per-query form of migrations 525 to 528 (`engagements_assignment_scope`, `engagements_internal_partner_only`), in the USING and the WITH CHECK, and in the form of a table whose client may be NULL: a letter with no client stays open to the firm, and **the guard says `client_id IS NULL OR` for the reason 528 gives for `properties`** (the comparison with a firm that has no internal practice is `NULL IS DISTINCT FROM NULL`, FALSE, and would hide every lead's letter from, and refuse it to, every non-Partner of such a firm). **Twenty more tables name the row they hang from and not a client, and carry a firm policy only**, so the parent's scope never reached them: `engagement_events` (engagements), `credit_note_allocations` and `sales_debit_note_allocations` (the note and the invoice it settles), `debit_note_allocations` and `purchase_credit_note_allocations` (the note and the bill), `customer_payment_events` (customer_payments), `document_extractions` (documents: what was read out of an uploaded file), `itr_filing_versions` (itr_filings), `onboarding_tasks` (onboarding_workflows), `recurring_journal_runs` and `recurring_purchase_bill_runs` (their templates), `workflow_approvals`, `workflow_executions` and `workflow_failures` (workflow_instances), `year_end_checklist_items`, `year_end_checklists`, `year_end_reviews` and `notes_to_accounts` (year_end_engagements), and `task_tags` and `task_timeline_events` (tasks, which carry the column and **no foreign key**, so neither catalog population could see them). Each takes `<table>_parent_visible`, one RESTRICTIVE FOR ALL policy asking that the row it hangs from is one the caller can see (an EXISTS over the parent read as the caller, so the parent's own scope and guards apply inside it) in the USING and the WITH CHECK. **The four allocation tables hang from TWO rows and ask both**, so a row naming a note of the caller's and an invoice of another client's is refused, **and three more may name a second row and ask it when it is set** (a recurring-journal run its journal entry, a recurring-bill run its bill, a note to the accounts its financial statement version: found by the independent review, which wrote a run under its own template naming another client's bill and got it accepted, with an existing hidden id answering differently from one that does not); **a foreign key that may be NULL (`customer_payment_events.payment_id`, `onboarding_tasks.workflow_id`) says `IS NULL OR`**, so a payment event not yet matched to a payment and a task not yet put on a workflow stay open to the firm as before. **Three of the twenty are absent from the production snapshot of 03-09-2026** (`notes_to_accounts`, `year_end_checklists`, `year_end_reviews`; the live project may or may not have them since) and present in a database built from the migrations, so their policies are created inside a DO block that asks `to_regclass` first, with the name written out as a literal in each statement (the drift guard excuses a restrictive policy production does not hold yet only when a migration names it in plain text) and a test applies and rolls back the migration on a database without them. **THE ONE THING THAT BREAKS, AND THE ROUTE CHANGE THAT SHIPS WITH IT**: `_next_engagement_no` allocates the next letter number as the newest number of the FIRM plus one, against a unique index on (firm_id, engagement_number), and it read as the caller. Once the table is scoped the newest letter a Manager can see is the newest of THEIR clients' and the leads', so the next number can be one another client's letter already holds, the insert fails on the index (a 422 carrying the raw database text) and fails again on every retry; with letters 0001 (the Manager's client), 0002 (a lead's) and 0003 (another Manager's client) the Manager's next number was 0004 before the scope and 0003 after it. It was found by reading every reader of the table before the scope was written. The number is a sequence of the firm, so `routers/engagement_letters._numbering_db` hands the allocator the privileged client when there is one and the caller's own only where there is no other (mock mode); the route only supplies the number, every other read of the table is by id after `assert_client_access`, and `tests/test_an_engagement_number_is_allocated_from_every_letter_of_the_firm.py` (mock-mode, so it runs in the required job) holds it, including an AST check that no call of the allocator passes the caller's client straight in. Nothing else changes for an assigned Manager or Executive or a Partner: no screen reads any of the twenty-one tables over PostgREST with a `.from(...)` (one embeds one, the client's lifecycle page selecting `tasks:onboarding_tasks(*)` under an onboarding workflow, which follows the workflow it hangs from), and every route that reaches them asserts the client of the row first. A scoped caller's workflow analytics counts, which were the firm's, are the caller's clients' now. **SIX OF THE TWENTY-ONE ARE READ-ONLY TO A SIGNED-IN SESSION** (`credit_note_allocations`, `task_tags`, `task_timeline_events` and the three workflow logs are granted SELECT and nothing else; the API writes them with its privileged client), so the proof for them is the policies, the reads and the plan, and a test fails the day a write grant is added to one of them, or taken from another, so that the list `READ_ONLY` is not a place where a table can hide. **THE CATALOG RULE LEARNED A NAMED LINK**: `NAMED_LINKS` in `test_every_client_table_is_assignment_scoped_or_says_why_pg.py` names a child whose link to its parent carries no foreign key (`task_tags` and `task_timeline_events` to `tasks`), is typed by hand, and is checked against the schema (`test_a_named_link_is_a_real_child`) so a rename is a red test and not a table that quietly leaves the population; `ri_cross_client_signals`, whose `client_ids` array points at no table at all, is still outside it, and the third population that would find such tables is POST-A-305. With 529 the shrink-only `children` list is EMPTY (ceiling 0) and the table list holds 26 entries, 11 of them still `pending` (ceilings 26 and 0). Deliberately NOT done: **nine parents carry the assignment scope and not the internal practice's Partner-only guard** (`debit_notes`, `purchase_credit_notes`, `sales_debit_notes`, `onboarding_workflows`, `tasks`, the two recurring templates, `workflow_instances`, `itr_filings`), so a member assigned to the practice reads the practice's rows in them and in their children, which follow the parent exactly; whether the practice's own tasks and workflow instances are meant to be a Partner's alone is a product decision as much as a policy (the guardrails of `docs/architecture/11-revenue-ops-and-knowledge.md` say Partner-only, the tables were never given the guard), so it is POST-A-306 and not part of this migration, and the test applies the practice assertion only to the tables whose parents carry the guard. **`run_due` of the recurring journals is not confined to the caller's clients** (POST-A-307): with no `client_id` it runs every active template of the firm with the privileged client, which its twin for bills does not; it is a route fix and not a policy. **What the independent reviews found beyond this diff** (POST-A-307 and POST-A-310 below were fixed afterwards, see the entry whose headline begins "THREE ROUTES ASKED A FIRM-LEVEL QUESTION") (three reviewers, one on readers and writers, one trying to get round the scope with real SQL, one on the rule, the tests and the claims): **a Partner of one firm can grant any member of another firm access to any client of that firm, and the grant is invisible to it** (`partners_insert_assignments` checks the row's firm and the caller's permission and neither the user nor the client; `my_assigned_client_ids()` reads by user alone; reproduced by the reviewer and again by me; POST-A-308, high, the next migration); the letters carry no role tier and an assigned member can move a row into the part that is open to the firm (POST-A-309); and two lifecycle routes compute a lead's stage over its letters as the caller (POST-A-310, the defect of the letter number again). Also left alone: the eleven client tables still `pending` (the statutory identity, the payroll settings and the PT registrations need an employee or portal branch first), and the `ri_*` tables (a grant to remove, POST-A-305). The proof is `tests/test_the_engagement_letters_and_twenty_children_follow_the_clients_assignment_scope_pg.py`: for each of the twenty-one tables the policies, six people's reads (a Partner, a Manager and an Executive assigned to client A, a Manager assigned to nothing, a suspended Partner, a Partner of another firm) over rows for client A, client B, the internal practice and the rows with no client or no parent; for each writable table an assigned Manager's insert under their own client's row and the refusal under another's (and, for an allocation, with either one of its two rows of another client), the moves, the updates and deletes, a Partner's inserts for every client; for the letters the lead, the practice guard exercised by a Manager assigned to the practice (so the guard alone refuses the insert), a firm with no internal practice, and the three year-end tables dropped. A seeded allocation names a second invoice or bill of its client, so the row a test inserts never meets the seed on the pair's unique key (`debit_note_allocations` has a unique key on the pair, and a first run of the test inserted the pair the seed already held).

- **A GRANT OF ACCESS NAMES A MEMBER AND A CLIENT OF THE FIRM THAT WRITES IT, AND A GRANT THAT DOES NOT GRANTS NOTHING** (migration 530, POST-A-308, 21-10-2026). Two tables decide what a member of a firm may reach, and a Partner writes both: `user_client_assignments` (which clients a member who is not a Partner may see, read by `my_assigned_client_ids()` and `can_access_client()`) and `user_permissions` (what a member may do beyond or below their role, read by `my_permission()`). Migration 478 made writing the first a Partner's act and 403/415 the second's; both ask that the ROW's `firm_id` is the caller's firm and nothing about the member the row names or, for an assignment, the client, and every reader looks the table up by the member alone, with no firm. **So a Partner of firm G could write (firm G, a member of firm F, a client of F), or (G, a member of F, 'assignment', 'write', true), and F's member read that client's rows from then on, or assigned themselves the rest, while F's own Partners could not see or remove the row, because its firm is G.** The independent review of migration 529 found the first and I reproduced it (F's Manager read 0 of F's customers before and 1 after); **the independent review of the first draft of this migration found the second one table over, and walked it end to end: G's Partner gave F's Manager `assignment:write`, the Manager assigned themselves F's other two clients, and their customers went from one to three.** The same row can take a permission away (`granted = false`). It needs the member's UUID and, for an assignment, a client's, which nothing serves, or a person who holds an account in both firms (a freelance CA who is a Manager in one practice and the Partner of their own; one login cannot sit in two firms, so that is two logins). The API was not the way in (`routers/assignments.py` and `services/user_permission_service` check the pair against the caller's firm); it is 478's finding one door further in, the rule being a property of the URL while the table took any firm-matching row. 530 does four things. **Two SECURITY DEFINER helpers, `user_is_in_my_firm(uuid)` and `client_is_in_my_firm(uuid)`, are asked in the WITH CHECK of `partners_insert_assignments`, `partners_update_assignments` and `firm_user_permissions`**, so a grant cannot be written, or re-pointed, onto a member or a client of another firm; **they are definer functions because a plain subquery cannot do it**: `public.users` shows a Partner only their own row to their own sign-in (`users_own_row_select`, migration 153, the reason the assignment router reads members through the repository), so a policy that asked `users` as the caller would refuse every legitimate grant to somebody else. Each answers about the CALLER'S firm only (`get_my_firm_id()`, NULL for a suspended or signed-out member, so false), so a foreign id and an id that does not exist answer the same word; they do confirm to anyone signed in that an id they already hold is in their own firm (the internal practice's client included), nothing about another; EXECUTE goes to `authenticated` and `service_role` only. **`my_assigned_client_ids()`, `can_access_client()` and `my_permission()` then count only a row written in the member's own firm** (and, for an assignment, whose client belongs to that firm), so a row already in either table, which the policies cannot reach, grants and takes nothing from now on; all three are replaced whole from their last definitions (526, 468, 468) with one condition added, keeping their grants. **`my_assigned_client_ids()` takes the member and the firm ONCE, as scalar sub-selects, and joins the client**: the first draft called `get_my_firm_id()` in the WHERE and the review measured a Manager with 3,000 assignments at 160 ms against 2 ms before, and a pin of the form is in the test because a plan cannot see inside a SQL function. **The API-side readers changed in the same commit, because they do not go through those functions**: `core/authz.assigned_client_ids`, `is_client_assigned` and `core/auth._permission_overrides` read the two tables with the privileged client and no firm, so a row already planted would have kept granting through every route (the review showed the SQL equivalent returning the planted client while the database refused it; the knowledge base's `is_assigned` passes the firm now), and `assignment_repository.create` looks for an existing pair in the caller's firm only, so a planted row of another firm is no longer reported to a Partner as the assignment they just asked for. A source rule (`tests/test_a_grant_of_access_is_read_in_the_members_own_firm.py`, mock-mode) fails any function that reads either table without naming a `firm_id`, and is shown to fail on a snippet that has none. Three lines left the frozen firm-scope list (`tests/fixtures/firm_scope_allowlist.txt`) because three of those reads are scoped now (`_permission_overrides`, its caller and the repository's probe). **Nothing is deleted**: the migration raises a WARNING (`migration 530: N assignment row(s) and M permission row(s) name a member or a client of another firm than their own`) with the number of assignment and permission rows that name a member or a client of another firm than their own, and removing them is a decision about data. **It was a NOTICE in the first draft and reached nobody, which the review of the final draft showed by applying it through the real runner**: `scripts/db/apply_migrations.py` runs psql at `client_min_messages=warning` and discards a clean run's output, so the claim that the apply log says whether any row exists was false. The runner now keeps a line that opens with `migration <number>:` from a successful file and puts it in the report (`messages` in `--json`, a `NOTE` line in the text report, a notice annotation on the run's summary page under Actions), and a test applies the migration through the runner over four planted shapes and reads the line from the JSON. Only that prefix is kept, so a warning Postgres raises on its own never reaches the report; `docs/deploy-migrations.md` says so for the next migration that has a fact to report. Left, and recorded: a planted row still occupies the table's unique key and only the other firm's Partner can delete it (it grants nothing now; whether to delete the ones that exist is decided from the warning), the database lets a Partner assign a non-Partner to the internal practice client where the API refuses (POST-A-306), and the same missing check on the other tables that carry a firm and a client or a member (POST-A-304). The proof is `tests/test_a_grant_of_access_names_a_member_and_a_client_of_the_firm_that_writes_it_pg.py`: the other firm's Partner is refused each shape of the cross-firm row in both tables (and accepted for a row that is all their own, the same statement), the firm's own Partner is refused a foreign member or client, an update cannot re-point a row, a planted row grants nothing by any of the three functions or by the customers it would have opened or the escalation it would have allowed (and does with the migration rolled back, the negative control inside the file), the helpers answer about the caller's firm only and are not callable by the public key, the warning counts both tables over rows whose only defect is the member, the client or both and removes nothing, and reaches the runner's report, the rollback restores the policies and functions exactly, and a signed-in scan still asks the caller's clients once. **The three API readers fail closed**: a member the request cannot place in a firm has no assigned clients and no override (an empty set, never an unfiltered read), and the firm has no default in `is_client_assigned` or `_permission_overrides`, so the next caller cannot leave it out; the source rule only asks that a function names a `firm_id`, which a default of `None` would have satisfied. **The review of the final draft also found the tests blind in six places, now closed**: no permission-table twin of the cross-firm refusal whose member is the caller's own, no row whose only defect is the client's firm or the row's firm (each condition of `my_assigned_client_ids()` and `can_access_client()` was redundant with the other for the planted rows), a count that could ignore the member or the client side, a rollback whose `can_access_client` nothing read back, refusals that `_count_op` reported as `err` whatever the cause (every refusal is now asserted to be SQLSTATE 42501 with the row-security message), and no assertion that the helpers are definer functions with a pinned search path. **Two instruments that read these tables moved with it**: the role-by-table matrix seeds a real member on its grant rows (its insert probe copies a seeded row, and `_parent_pk` leaves a foreign key to `users` empty, so without a member every copy would be refused for naming nobody and the Partner's cell would stop measuring the role), and the suspended-member test names `user_is_in_my_firm` among the functions that read `users` for the member a request NAMES rather than for the caller, with the reason (the caller is still resolved by `get_my_firm_id()`, so a suspension reaches it).

- **THREE ROUTES ASKED A FIRM-LEVEL QUESTION AS THE CALLER AND ANSWERED FOR THEIR SLICE OF THE FIRM, AND A RUN-ALL WAS NOT CONFINED TO THE CALLER'S CLIENTS** (POST-A-303's two usage checks, POST-A-307, POST-A-310, 21-10-2026). The rule this file already records for a firm-level question is that it is answered with the firm's access after the route has authorised the caller (`engagement_letters._numbering_db` is the worked example, migration 529's finding); three more places had the other shape, and none of them needed a migration. **The two "is this still used?" checks** (`firm_hsn_library._find_hsn_usage`, behind the purge and the bulk delete, and `service_catalogue._find_service_usage`, behind the delete) were handed the caller's client, and every table they read is assignment-scoped (the lines follow their documents: the invoices, credit notes and bills since 084, the debit notes since 370, the recurring templates since 528), so a Manager assigned to some clients had the check run over THEIR clients' documents only and a code, or a service, that another client's issued invoice still used was reported unused and deleted. The line stores the code as text, so nothing is orphaned; the guard exists to keep a used code visible for the audit trail, and a guard that answers for part of the firm is not one. Each module has a `_usage_db` that returns the privileged client when there is one and the caller's own only where there is no other; the ownership lookup and the delete itself stay on the caller's client (a test fails if either wanders onto the privileged one), the HSN check's every query carries the firm (directly or through the document it hangs from; a test with another firm's line proves it) while the service check is keyed on the service's own id, which the ownership lookup has just vouched for, and carries no firm filter of its own (a line of another firm naming this firm's service id cannot arise through the app, and adding the filter would have meant a firm column on documents the existing tests seed without one), and the service check still ignores a draft or cancelled document (the HSN check never did: any line carrying the code blocks). **The lifecycle's two reads of a lead's engagement letters** (`revert_lead_after_engagement_closed`, which recomputes the stage from the most advanced live letter, and `convert_lead`, which picks the most recently signed one and refuses to convert without one) read with the caller's client, so since migration 529 a letter naming a client the caller is not assigned to was missing from what they read and the signed letter was "not found" (a 409) or a stage fell further back than the evidence says: the defect of the letter number again, a newest-or-most-advanced computation over rows the caller can only partly see. `_letters_db` reads them with the privileged client, after the lead is looked up under the firm and a named client is checked. **The one UPDATE in that route, which links the signed letter to the client just made, is a write the caller does as themselves and is deliberately not widened, and the review of this change found what that leaves**: a signed letter the caller cannot see is now FOUND (it used to be a 409), the update of a row the database hides answers with no row and no error, and the conversion went on as though the letter were linked. `_link_signed_letter` reads the answer and says so ("the letter belongs to a client you are not assigned to, so ask a Partner to link it"), and a refused update says why; the lookup is `_signed_letters_of_lead`. `tests/test_a_leads_stage_is_worked_out_over_every_letter_of_the_lead.py` walks the module and fails any SELECT of `engagements` that does not go through `_letters_db`, any `.table("engagements")` held in a variable (which the select rule could not see), and any use other than the link's UPDATE. **The recurring journals' "run all due"** (`POST /api/recurring-journals/run` with no `client_id`) called a service that lists every active template of the FIRM with the privileged client, so a Manager assigned to one client started a draft, a run row and an audit line for every client of the firm; its twins for bills and invoices already narrowed the call by `effective_client_ids`, and the route now does the same (one unrestricted call for a Partner, one per assigned client otherwise, and an empty set means nothing runs and never "no filter"; the response keeps the shape the screen reads). Nothing is posted by that route either way: the harm was whose drafts and whose audit line. Each fix has a mock-mode test with a recording double and a control that shows the defect when the fix is taken out (the HSN purge, bulk delete and service delete refuse a used code the caller cannot see and, with no privileged client, delete it; the revert does not downgrade a lead whose signed letter the caller cannot see and, with no privileged client, does; a Manager assigned to one client generates one template's draft and a Manager assigned to nothing generates none). **Left open, and said so**: the other half of POST-A-303, a Tally migration job whose client is deleted becomes a firm-level job (`tally_migration_jobs.client_id` is ON DELETE SET NULL and a client-less job is open to the firm), which needs a decision between a marker that keeps the row Partner-only and deleting the job with its client; and **two findings of the independent review, filed and not fixed here**: the same defect as the letter number in three more sequences read as the caller (the vendor-payment number, the next bank-account ledger code and the proposal number; closed by the entry that follows this one) and three line tables the usage checks never read (POST-A-312). A recurring-journal "run all" for a Manager assigned to very many clients makes one service call per client, as its twin for bills does; a single call filtered on the whole set would avoid the loop and was not done.

- **A NUMBER THAT IS ONE LINE FOR THE WHOLE FIRM IS READ WITH THE WHOLE FIRM IN VIEW, AND WHICH SERIES THOSE ARE IS READ OFF THEIR UNIQUE KEY** (POST-A-311, 21-10-2026). A "newest plus one" number is only right if the reader sees every row the database will compare the new number with. `services/numbering.NUMBER_SERIES` records, for each of the seven series `next_sequence` reads back (the credit, debit, sales-debit and purchase-credit notes, the receipt, the vendor payment and the asset code), the columns its UNIQUE constraint covers besides the number, and `next_sequence` refuses a scope that is not exactly that. For a series numbered per (firm, client) the caller has that client's access, so their own view of the table IS the series. For the one numbered for the FIRM alone, `purchase_payments` (migration 159: `("firm_id",)`), it is not: the table carries the assignment scope, and with `USE_USER_JWT` on (the production default) a Manager assigned to client A reads only A's payments, and none of the practice's own internal client, which only a Partner reads (migration 074). When client B held `VPMT-2627-0005` the Manager's next number could be one B already had; the insert failed on 23505 and the routes answered 409 "A payment numbering collision was detected; please retry." after the journal had been posted and compensated, on the single-bill route and on `create_payment_core` (the multi-bill payment, the bank match queue, the cheque conversion) alike, and each time the person retried it recomputed the same number, because their own maximum never passes the numbers they cannot see: the defect of the engagement-letter number (`engagement_letters._numbering_db`, migration 529) in a second place. `numbering.is_firm_wide(table)` is the question, answered from `NUMBER_SERIES` itself (a series whose scope lacks `client_id`), and `_firm_wide_db` reads such a series with the privileged client when there is one and the caller's own where there is no other (mock mode, a test double), after the route has authorised the caller, to return a number and nothing else. A per-client series is never moved off the caller's client, and a test runs every entry of `NUMBER_SERIES` through the rule with the expectation taken from the recorded scope and not from the function that classifies it, so a firm-wide series added later is held without anyone remembering to add it and a series wrongly classified is caught. **Two numbers that are not in that table had the same shape and are fixed the same way, and in all three the privileged read is made INSIDE the function that finds the number**, so a second caller that passes the request's client cannot bring the defect back. `banking._next_bank_account_code` scans "the whole firm" for the next free chart-of-accounts code, while `chart_of_accounts` carries the assignment scope and is unique on (firm_id, account_code) and (firm_id, account_name), so an Executive or a Manager proposed a code another client's ledger already used, all five attempts of `_ensure_bank_ledger` proposed the same one (shown with an insert that refuses a taken code the way the key does: five attempts, one code, then the account is saved without its ledger, and with the fix one attempt), and the account stayed unlinked. The ledger row is still INSERTED by the caller (a test fails if the insert wanders onto the privileged client), and the scan is now read in keyset pages (`core.db_paging.fetch_all`) because PostgREST cuts a response at about a thousand rows without saying so, and a code missed there is the same wedge. `lifecycle._next_proposal_no` takes the firm's newest proposal by creation time; `proposals` is assignment-scoped and has no unique key on `proposal_no`, so the result was not a failed insert but a duplicate proposal number on a document sent to a prospect, whenever the firm's newest proposal belonged to a client the caller is not assigned to (`proposals.client_id` is nullable, migration 059: a proposal to a lead has none and is visible to everyone).
