Two stale sentences went with it: the cheque-conversion docstrings said the register's own table was the only privileged read, and they now name this one, which returns a number and no row; and the comment above `NUMBER_SERIES` called it every auto-numbered series, when the sales-invoice and reverse-charge numbers are found by their own per-client modules, the fee-invoice number by a database counter and the engagement-letter and proposal numbers firm-wide. **Deliberately not done:** the per-(firm, client) series stay on the caller's client, the lead number is a mock-mode counter no table stores, and the numbers a Partner is handed are unchanged, because a Partner's view is the firm in either case. **Found by the independent review and filed, not fixed here:** the trial-balance import chooses account codes and matches account names over this client's accounts and the firm-level ones while both unique keys are the firm's (POST-A-313), the same blind spot in a sibling. A number read and then inserted can still race another request for the same number; a database-side sequence would remove that and is a change of the numbering scheme, so it is not built. `tests/test_a_number_for_the_whole_firm_is_read_with_the_whole_firm_in_view.py` holds it (25 tests: the rule over `NUMBER_SERIES`; the vendor-payment, bank-code and proposal numbers each with the firm filter's value asserted against another firm's higher number, the newest proposal chosen from several, the scan paged past a thousand rows, and a control that shows the defect when no privileged client is given).

- **THE STATUTORY IDENTITY, THE PAYROLL SETTINGS AND THE PT REGISTRATIONS TOOK THE ASSIGNMENT SCOPE, AND THERE WAS NO EMPLOYEE OR PORTAL BRANCH TO KEEP** (migration 531, POST-A-299's second tranche, 21-10-2026). `client_statutory_identity` (a client's TAN, EPF establishment code, ESIC employer code and LIN, the identifiers a TDS statement is filed and a payslip is printed under), `client_payroll_settings` (whether payroll is switched on for a client, by whom and when, and the day its inputs are due) and `client_pt_registrations` (its professional-tax certificate numbers by state) carried a NOT NULL client, a firm policy and a role tier on writes (Manager and above), and nothing per client. Migration 519 had already taken every write away from the signed-in role (they are written by the API alone, with the service role), so what was open was the READ: every member of the firm, a Reviewer included (read before and after on a throwaway database: 1, 1 and 1 rows before, 0, 0 and 0 after) and whatever clients they are assigned to, listed all three for every client of the firm over PostgREST, and for the internal practice, which 074 makes a Partner's. 531 gives each the two RESTRICTIVE FOR ALL policies the other client tables carry (`<table>_assignment_scope` and `<table>_internal_partner_only`) in the once-per-query form of migrations 525 to 530, in the USING and in the WITH CHECK. **The scope is written in the one shape every scope since 527 has, `client_id IS NULL OR` branch included, although the client is NOT NULL here and the branch can never be true**: the first draft left it out as dead, and the real-Postgres test of migration 526 failed, because 526's rollback recognises only that shape and a scope without it blocks `DROP FUNCTION my_assigned_client_ids()` ("cannot drop function ... because other objects depend on it"). Found by the full real-Postgres run, not by the new test. **The caveat the ledger item carried for these three, "an employee or portal branch to keep", was checked and does not exist**: the firm policy asks `get_my_firm_id()`, which is NULL for anyone without a `users` row, so an employee or a portal contact never read them (a test reads them as a signed-in principal with no member row, before and after, and gets none), the employee's payslip reads the employer block with the service role after proving ownership (POST-A-220, `routers/portal_employee.py`), the payroll router reads and writes them with the service role (`routers.payroll._db` is `service_db_or_none`, so its answers do not change at all), Form 3CD clause 34 reads the TAN with the service role as well (`routers.income_tax._db`), and no screen reads them over PostgREST. The reads that do run under the caller's sign-in are staff reads of a client the route has already checked: the deductor block of a TDS statement (`routers.tds._deductor_sources`) and the employer block of the staff payslip routes (`services.payslip_pdf_service.load_employer`). **One edge, said in the migration:** a Manager or Executive assigned to the internal practice passes the route's client check and then meets the practice guard on the identity row, so the deductor block reads as "no TAN recorded"; their read of the `clients` row for the practice was already hidden by the same guard, and payroll is barred for the practice. **The write side is held two ways.** A test asserts that the signed-in role, anon and PUBLIC still hold nothing but SELECT on the three, at table level and at column level (`role_table_grants` does not list a column grant, which is the shape 326 and 332 first gave `client_payroll_settings`), and, with INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE granted in a throwaway database, that the policies are the second lock: an assigned Manager writes their own client's row and is refused another's, the practice's, and one for a client they are not assigned to; an Executive and a Reviewer are refused by the role tier; an update or a delete of another client's row matches nothing; a row cannot be MOVED to a client the person may not see; a Manager assigned to the practice is refused its row by the guard alone; and a Partner writes for, and moves a row between, every client and the practice. **Four things the independent review found the first 35 tests could not see, and the 41 now do:** a guard written `<>` instead of `IS DISTINCT FROM` hides every row from every non-Partner of a firm whose `firms.internal_client_id` is NULL (migration 405 measured one of two firms in that state; the test sets it NULL and asks again); a scope that widened its Partner branch to include a Reviewer passed (the fixture had no Reviewer); a scope or a guard that called `get_my_role()` or `my_internal_client_id()` once per row passed (the plan test now looks for them in the Filter line and for the InitPlans that carry them); and a scope whose NULL role defaulted to a pass was invisible, because the stranger, the suspended Partner and the person assigned to nothing read none through the FIRM policy and would have read none if 531 did not exist (a permissive `USING (true)` policy added in the throwaway database now removes the firm policy from the question). Run with the migration replaced by an empty statement 28 of the 41 tests fail (22 of the first 35 did). The rollback drops the six policies and returns the firm-wide read exactly. `test_332` assigns its Manager to the client under test (it measures column grants, and two of its inserts hit the new scope). Left on the shrink-only list, kind `pending` (POST-A-299): the year locks (deny-all today: POST-A-314), the two FX tables, the stock ledger, the two party-credit tables, the demo filings and the portal sessions, **whose WRITES are the urgent part (POST-A-299)**. `tests/test_the_statutory_identity_and_the_two_payroll_settings_tables_take_the_clients_assignment_scope_pg.py` holds it.
- **THE YEAR LOCK OF A CLIENT WAS INVISIBLE TO EVERY CHECK MADE UNDER A SIGN-IN, AND IT IS NOW READ BY THE MEMBERS WHO POST INTO THE CLIENT AND WRITTEN BY THE API ALONE** (migration 532, POST-A-314 and the third tranche of POST-A-299, 21-10-2026). Migration 289 wrote the only policy of `client_year_locks` as `firm_id = (auth.jwt() ->> 'firm_id')::uuid`, the shape migration 154 found on three other families of tables: this project issues no `firm_id` claim, so the predicate is NULL for everyone, a Partner included, and row security refused every row of the table to every signed-in session in both directions. With USE_USER_JWT on (the production default) that has two consequences. **(1) Finalising a year-end failed half way:** `routers/year_end.py` and `routers/year_end_reviews.py` write the engagement as `locked` and then call `year_lock_service.set_client_lock` with the request's own client, and the insert of the lock row was refused after the engagement row was written, so the engagement read locked and the client's year stayed open. **(2) A finalised year read as OPEN to every check made under a sign-in,** because `period_closure_reason` and `period_lock_reason` (migrations 361 and 267) are SECURITY INVOKER and read the table directly. **The ledger line for this item first said the posting kernel "is not fooled" because `post_journal_atomic` is a definer function; that was wrong, and the correction is the part worth keeping.** `post_journal_atomic` does not ask the closure question at all (only the manual-journal EDIT function does); the kernel's gate is `services/phase2_journal_service._create_journal` calling `period_lock_service.closure_reason(db, ...)` in Python with the request's own client, and the pre-checks of invoices, bills, credit and debit notes, receipts, fixed assets, inventory, the manual journal and the imports call `assert_open` the same way, so under a sign-in none of them saw a finalised year. Production holds no year-end engagement and so no lock row, which is why nothing was harmed: the first firm to finalise a year-end would have finalised it in name only for every path that asks. **Two doors never ask, and 532 does not close them (closed by migration 533, the next entry; POST-A-316, high, found by its independent review and reproduced on a throwaway database as an assigned Executive, Manager, Reviewer and Partner):** `post_journal_atomic` and `settle_receipt_atomic` are SECURITY DEFINER functions a signed-in person may call with their own token and neither reads the lock table, and the API's plain rupee customer receipt posts through `settle_receipt_atomic` and not through the kernel, so a finalised year stopped the kernel and the document pre-checks and did not stop those. The durable fix is inside the two functions (a closure check after the caller check, read as their owner) and is its own change on the hottest write path; `post_journal_atomic` is also not wrapped by the accounting:write role-pair check of migration 486, so a Reviewer can call it (the same ledger item). Reproduced on a throwaway database: the administrator reads one lock row and the sentence, a signed-in Partner reads 0 rows and NULL. **What 532 does.** READS: the dead policy is replaced by the canonical firm policy (`get_my_firm_id()`, FOR ALL, no token claim) and the two RESTRICTIVE FOR ALL policies every client table carries (`client_year_locks_assignment_scope` and `client_year_locks_internal_partner_only`, in the once-per-query form of 525 to 531), so a Partner reads every lock of the firm and a Manager, an Executive or a Reviewer assigned to a client reads that client's locks and nobody else's; the two invoker functions therefore answer for exactly the clients the caller can reach through the API. WRITES: `year_lock_service.set_client_lock` reads and writes the table with the privileged client (`_lock_table_db`: `db_provider.service_db_or_none()` where there is one, the caller's own in mock mode, `None` staying `None`) behind the routes' own checks (a Partner finalises and a Partner reopens, after the engagement's scope check), and the signed-in roles lose INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, TRUNCATE, REFERENCES and TRIGGER (anon loses everything), as 499, 515 and 519 did for the payroll tables. The reason for revoking and not only adding a role tier is the audit trail: a lock deleted over the database leaves no `client_year_lock` row in `audit_log` and needs no reason, and the API demands one. As the SECOND lock, three RESTRICTIVE write policies (`client_year_locks_role_insert|update|delete`) ask for a Partner, so a later GRANT, or a default privilege nobody noticed, does not let an assigned Executive close or reopen a year; a test grants the writes in a throwaway database to show it. **Deliberately not done:** making the two functions SECURITY DEFINER (a definer function with arguments that a signed-in user can execute must ask who is calling, migration 486, and the kernel calls these on every posting; fixing the table's visibility is the smaller change and is what `firms` and `filings`, the other tables they read, already have), the order of the year-end routes (POST-A-315), and the two doors above. **The existing guard in `test_the_year_lock_pin_is_hashed_limited_and_unreadable.py` that said the module must not contain the words `request_db`, `get_supabase` or `db_provider` was restated as the rule it stood for:** no function of the module except `_lock_table_db` may reach for a client accessor, and none is imported at module level, so the PIN functions still use only the db they were handed. `tests/test_a_clients_year_locks_are_visible_to_the_members_who_post_into_it_and_written_by_the_api_only_pg.py` (26 tests: the policies; who reads which locks, with a Reviewer, a suspended Partner, another firm's Partner, a principal with no member row, a person assigned to the practice and a firm with no internal practice; what the two period functions answer to each of them for each client; the defect reproduced by rolling the migration back; the grants, and again from a database that granted every privilege to PUBLIC, anon and authenticated, as production's defaults do; the writes granted back in a throwaway database; the apply log's count of an engagement that reads locked with no lock row; the rollback, which restores migration 289's policy exactly as the catalog printed it, and a second run; the plan), `tests/test_a_year_end_is_finalised_and_reopened_under_a_request_sign_in_pg.py` (6 tests that drive the real `final_approve`, `reopen` and status routes, the kernel's `_create_journal` gate and `assert_open` against a real database as `authenticated` and `service_role` through `tests/_signed_in_postgrest.py`, a PostgREST-shaped client that runs each call in the database as a chosen role and raises on anything it does not model) and `tests/test_a_clients_year_lock_is_written_with_the_privileged_client_only.py` (8 tests: the privileged client is the one used; a lock and an unlock touch exactly one client's one year of one firm, with neighbours that differ in exactly one of the three filters, which are now the only isolation on those statements; the name of the table is used in code by two modules and written by one function, whatever the spelling) hold it. Not drawn in a browser: no screen reads or writes the table.

- **THE TWO DATABASE FUNCTIONS THAT WRITE THE LEDGER ASK WHETHER THE YEAR IS CLOSED, AND THE ONE THAT NEVER ASKED WHO MAY POST ASKS THAT TOO** (migration 533, POST-A-316, found by the independent review of migration 532 and reproduced on a throwaway database on 21-10-2026). "Closed" is the CA's own deliberate act (the firm locked the financial year, or the client's year-end was finalised) and `period_closure_reason` (migration 361) answers both. The posting kernel asked it before every posting, but the ledger is written through two SECURITY DEFINER functions a signed-in person may call with their own token, and neither asked: `post_journal_atomic` (which restated the journal table's row policies and nothing about the period) and `settle_receipt_atomic` (migration 486's guarded wrapper), which the API's plain rupee customer receipt posts through and the kernel never sees. So a receipt dated inside a finalised year was accepted for everyone, and any member who could reach a client could call either function directly and post a dated entry into a closed year (reproduced as an assigned Executive, Manager and Reviewer, and as a Partner). `post_journal_atomic` was also the one definer function 486 did not wrap with a role-pair check, so a Reviewer assigned to a client, whom the API refuses on every route that posts, could post. **WHAT 533 DOES, BY RENAME, AS 486 DID:** `post_journal_atomic` becomes `post_journal_atomic__unguarded` (the service role's alone, migration 418's body untouched, asserted against the last defining migration found by scanning the directory) and a wrapper with the original name asks `assert_caller_acts_for` (the caller's firm, a client they reach, the internal practice a Partner's, and a permission pair through `my_permission`, which counts the per-person grid), then `period_closure_reason` for the entry's date as the function's owner, then calls the original. `settle_receipt_atomic` keeps 486's wrapper and gains the same closure check on the journal's date and on the receipt's own date; the wrapper is asserted to be 486's text with that one block added. **A REVERSAL STAMPS ONLY AN ENTRY OF ITS OWN CLIENT** (found by the independent review of this migration and reproduced): the original body flips `is_reversed` on the entry named in `reversal_of` for the payload's firm and asked nothing about its client, so an Executive assigned to client A could hide client B's entry (or one of A's own in a finalised year) from every statement, the TDS register and section 43B(h), block its genuine reversal with a 409 and free its reference number; the wrapper now requires the original to be an entry of the payload's firm and client, and does not ask its date, because a reversal dated in an open year of an entry in a closed one is the remedy the closure's sentence offers. **THE CLOSURE IS THE CLOSURES, NOT THE FILED-RETURN BRANCH,** for the reason migration 361 and the kernel record (June's bank work happens after June's returns are filed). **THE PAIR LIST IS A UNION BY NECESSITY, ORDERED LOWEST TIER FIRST:** the function cannot know which route called it, so it admits a caller holding any of six pairs, read route by route off the handlers that write a journal entry through the request's own sign-in: banking:write (Executive: the bank module), accounting:write (Manager: sales and purchase documents, notes, receipts, payments, fixed assets, CWIP, recurring and manual journals, imports), client:write (Manager: creating or editing a customer or vendor posts the opening-balance journal), year_end:approve (Manager: posting an approved adjustment), accounting:approve (Partner: cancelling a document or reversing a receipt) and payroll:finalize (Partner: the payroll accrual, disbursement and settlement). The lowest tier is Executive, so a Reviewer, a Client-role member and a member whose grid denies every pair cannot post, and nothing the API lets a role do through a posting route is refused. **THE FIRST READING OVER-COLLECTED AND THE INDEPENDENT REVIEW CORRECTED IT:** taking every non-read pair of every router that posts put invoice:write (it guards sending a document), year_end:write (drafting an adjustment), payroll:write, billing:write (fee billing never touches the ledger) and banking:approve into the list, so an Executive denied every pair a posting route asks could still post over PostgREST through invoice:write, and a Reviewer granted only billing:write could. A mock-mode test keeps the list honest in both directions: every pair is a real pair of `core.permissions` with its real lowest role, none is open to a Reviewer or a Client, the five wrong pairs are absent, and every router that reaches a posting module (it names a posting primitive or imports a service or domain module that does) either has a write pair in the list or is on a reviewed list of sixteen routers that post nothing through the caller's sign-in, each with its reason, so a NEW router has to be classified and the build names it. **A DATE THE CHECK CANNOT READ IS A REFUSAL AND NOT A REASON TO SKIP IT** (the kernel's ACC-27 rule, in SQL): an unreadable, empty, missing or infinite entry date, and an unreadable, missing or infinite receipt date, are refused with a sentence; an empty payload keeps its old sentence. **THE PLAIN RECEIPT ASKS FIRST, IN PYTHON:** `receipt_service._assert_open_where_a_receipt_feeds_a_return`, which both receipt doors call (the foreign receipt posts through the kernel but writes before it does), calls `period_lock_service.assert_not_closed`, the closures-only twin of `assert_open`, for every client and before the filed-return branch, before a receipt number is taken or a row is written, with the closure's own sentence and a 422; the database check is the second lock and is what a direct call reaches, and when it is the one that speaks (a year closed between the two) `period_lock_service.closure_sentence_in` recognises the closure's sentence inside the error and the receipt answers 422 with it. The statement-count ratchet (`PLR0915`, a per-function size that may only shrink) would not take a third statement in either receipt door, so the closure question went inside `_assert_open_where_a_receipt_feeds_a_return`, which both doors already call and which `test_both_receipt_paths_ask_before_they_write` pins by name; it asks the closures first and of every client, then the filed-return lock for the clients whose advances reach a return. **THE DIRECT CALL IS STILL LOOSER THAN THE ROUTES, AND SAYS SO:** the function cannot tell a manual journal from a document's journal (the caller names `source_type` and every other column the body honours), so a person holding any one pair can post any balanced entry for a client they reach over PostgREST; taking that door away (the kernel posting with the privileged client, EXECUTE revoked from signed-in users, the shape of migrations 499 and 519) is POST-A-317. A consequence worth knowing: the online-payment webhook posts its receipt dated today through the same door, so if the client's current year is finalised or the firm has locked it the receipt is refused (422) and the captured money waits; the firm-lock case always behaved this way. The kernel still asks first, so a posting through the API is refused in the kernel's words. **WHAT IT DOES NOT DO:** it does not ask the filed-return branch, does not touch `edit_posted_journal` or `discard_posted_journal` (they already ask `journal_period_lock_reason`), and changes no table, policy or grant on a table. **TWO REFUSAL SENTENCES CHANGED IN WORDING** and nothing in the application matches them: a client the caller cannot reach now reads "is not one the caller can reach" (it was "is not assigned to the caller") and a JWT with no member row reads "has no live user record" (it was "has no user record"), because 486's guard is in front of the body now; `test_journal_posting_privileges_pg.py` asserts the new wording. **THE RULE FOR THE NEXT PERSON:** to change the logic of `post_journal_atomic`, replace `post_journal_atomic__unguarded` (derive its body from the migration that last defined the original name, 418 as this is written), never the wrapper; a `CREATE OR REPLACE` of the public name from an older migration would put the unguarded body back, and the real-Postgres test (a Partner refused a finalised year, a Reviewer refused an open one) is what notices, because the body's own caller check would satisfy the 486 shape test. Tests: `tests/test_a_posting_into_a_closed_year_is_refused_by_the_two_ledger_functions_pg.py` (real Postgres: each person and the backend against both functions and both closures, a filed return that is not a closure, each of the six pairs live and eleven that guard something else admitting nobody, the reversal stamp, infinite dates, the neighbour year and the other client, nothing written on a refusal, the role pair and the grid in both directions, the dates, the duplicate, both reversals, the shapes, the rollback and the defect it brings back) and `tests/test_the_two_ledger_functions_ask_the_closure_and_the_pairs_the_posting_routes_ask.py` (mock mode: the order inside each wrapper, the pair list against the permission matrix and the routers, the receipt's refusal before any read, the database's refusal answered as a 422). The service role's own posting of the same dates is covered because the kernel already refused it: the check is the same sentence from the same function.

- **THE MONTHLY BALANCES THE REPORTS READ ARE WRITTEN BY THE DATABASE'S OWN TRIGGERS AND THE SERVICE ROLE, AND A SIGNED-IN SESSION CANNOT EDIT A CLOSED YEAR'S FIGURES BY WRITING THEM** (migration 534, POST-A-318, found by the independent review of migration 533 and reproduced on 22-10-2026). `account_period_balances` is one row per client, account and month, the debit and credit totals of the posted ledger, kept by two triggers (`apb_on_entry_post`, `apb_on_line_insert`, which call `apb_apply_posted_line`) and healed every night by the balance-cache audit through the service role. The trial balance, profit and loss, balance sheet, ratios, budgets, cash flow and the year-end statements read these buckets DIRECTLY and not the journal, so they are what the CA sees and signs. The table was granted INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE to `authenticated` and its only policy was `firm_client_isolation` (the client belongs to the caller's firm), with no role tier, and `apb_apply_posted_line` carried the default PUBLIC execute right. The review ran `UPDATE account_period_balances SET debit_paise = 99999999` as an Executive assigned to the client on a bucket of a finalised year (UPDATE 1) and called the function as an assigned Reviewer: no journal entry, no closure check, no audit row, and the reports moved while the ledger did not. A forged bucket also made `apb_assert_no_drift()`, which compares EVERY firm's buckets with its ledger and is called by the manual-journal edit and discard functions, refuse for every firm until the nightly audit healed it. **WHAT 534 DOES, IN THE SHAPE OF 499, 513 AND 519:** REVOKE INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE on the table from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC and check the EFFECTIVE privilege (table, column, through PUBLIC); REVOKE EXECUTE on `apb_apply_posted_line` from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC and grant it to `service_role` explicitly, because the trigger calls it as whoever fired the trigger; and REVOKE EXECUTE on the four maintenance functions migration 251 meant for the service role (`apb_rebuild_client`, `apb_drifted_clients`, `apb_rebuild_drifted`, `apb_assert_no_drift`) from `authenticated` and `anon` BY NAME, because 251 revoked them from PUBLIC only and Supabase's default privileges grant a new function's EXECUTE to those two roles directly (a database built from the migrations has no such default, so the older test of 251 passed whether or not production held the right; nothing signed-in calls them, and the edit and discard functions call them as their owner). A trigger function's own EXECUTE right is checked when the trigger is created and not when it fires, and neither trigger function can be called directly, so they need no change. **WHAT STILL WRITES THE TABLE, AND WHY NOTHING BREAKS:** posting, editing, discarding and reversing a journal entry run inside SECURITY DEFINER functions (`post_journal_atomic`, `settle_receipt_atomic`, `edit_posted_journal`, `discard_posted_journal`), so their triggers fire as the owner whatever role called them; the service role keeps INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE (the API's privileged client, the scheduler, `apb_rebuild_client`). Reading is untouched: the reports read the buckets as the caller, scoped to the caller's clients through `clients` (the catalog rule's `parent`). Nothing in `apps/web` writes the table (it names it in comments only) and the only Python writer is `services/balance_cache_service.recompute_client`, called from `jobs/scheduler.py` with `db_provider.service_db`. **NOT DONE, ON PURPOSE:** no write policy as a second lock (with no verb granted a policy decides nothing; 499 and 513 carry none and 519 carries none on the three client payroll tables, the shape followed here); TRIGGER and REFERENCES are left as the platform has them (rights over the table's definition, which PostgREST does not expose); the rollback restores what the migrations granted, which is not necessarily what production held (its own ACL was not read); `apb_assert_no_drift()` still compares every firm at once, which no member can now make fail, and narrowing it to the firm in hand is a change to the manual-journal functions. Tests: `tests/test_534_the_monthly_balances_are_written_by_the_database_and_the_service_role_only_pg.py` (14 cases, real Postgres: the effective privileges at every level, proved again from a database that granted everything to PUBLIC, anon and authenticated first, the function's execute right and the four maintenance functions', each of four roles refused every write and the call by the privilege with a closed year's bucket untouched, the reads unchanged and scoped, posting as an assigned Executive, a manual journal edited and discarded and a reversal, a receipt settled, a journal written directly by the service role and the rebuild all keeping the buckets equal to the ledger, and the rollback bringing the forgery back, a re-apply ending it and a second apply harmless) and `tests/test_the_monthly_balances_are_written_only_by_the_nightly_audit.py` (6 cases, no database: the AST of every module of the API, `scripts/` included, writes the table in one function and names it in no way that hides a write (a query kept in a variable, the name in a constant, `from_`, a module-level statement, an upsert, SQL text: the guard is shown each shape and must fire), nothing calls an `apb_` function, the job hands all three entry points the privileged client, the browser does not `.from()` the table).

- **THE WRITERS OF FOUR TABLES MOVE TO THE PRIVILEGED CLIENT IN A CHANGE THAT SHIPS BEFORE THE MIGRATION THAT FORCES THEM TO, BECAUSE THE OLD API AGAINST REVOKED GRANTS FAILS SOFT AND WRONG** (POST-A-319 and POST-A-299's fourth tranche, 22-10-2026). Migrations 535 and 536 take the signed-in write away from `filings`, `party_credit_balances`, `party_credit_ledger` and `inventory_stock_ledger`; this change moves their writers to the privileged client first and takes no grant away itself. The privileged (service-role) client can do everything the request's client could, so it is safe on today's grants and on the revoked ones. **WHAT MOVED:** `gst_filing_record_service.record_filing` (`_filings_db`), `party_credit_service` (`_credit_db`, taken first by its four writing methods), and the stock ledger's writers (`inventory_service.ledger_writer`, used by `_insert_and_cache`, `_set_ledger_journal_entry_id` and `seed_opening_balances_batch`, and by `inventory_location_service.transfer`); reads stay on the request's client so row security is still the second lock on them, and the compare-and-set on `service_catalogue`, the document tables a credit application updates and the chain read stay on it too. Every statement the privileged client runs on the credit tables names the firm and the client (it carries no row security), and `record_filing` asks that the client is the firm's when the clients differ. **WHY A CHANGE OF ITS OWN:** the independent reviews of 535 and 536 each measured what the OLD API does against revoked grants, and it is not a clean failure: the stock callers (`apply_sale_to_inventory` and the other `apply_*`, `seed_opening_balances_batch`) swallow the refusal by design, `_insert_and_cache` has already moved the item's cached quantity and version when the ledger insert is refused, so an invoice issues with no ledger row, no COGS journal and a cache ahead of the chain, the opening-stock batch posts its journal before its insert so the general ledger carries inventory the ledger lacks, a bank overpayment's grant fails after the transaction is posted, and a GST return marked filed writes its status and fails to write the lock row. A merge puts the Python on Render at once and the migration job runs only after the push's own required checks (about 45 minutes; 532's followed its merge by 44), which is the usual order, but nothing enforces it and a failed or delayed deploy reverses it. So the writers ship alone and each revoke follows in its own change at least an hour later; **do the same for any table whose writers are moved: the Python first, the grant second.** **THE SWITCH IS NOT A FALLBACK:** `service_db_or_none()` is None only when `SUPABASE_URL` is unset; with it set the helpers ignore the client they were handed and return the process-wide service client, so a test double that sets a fake URL needs `service_db_or_none` patched (two filing tests were adjusted for exactly that). **A SOFT-DELETED FILING IS REVIVED, NOT UPDATED:** the lookup finds a `filings` row whether or not it was soft-deleted (an assigned member can soft-delete one over PostgREST until 535, and the period functions read live rows only), and the update writes `deleted_at` back to null; updating a row the lock cannot see would have reported a locked period that was open. **NOT DONE, ON PURPOSE:** `_set_ledger_journal_entry_id` updates by id only (every caller passes the id of a row it inserted a line earlier) and `transfer` relies on the position check (a function scoped by firm and client, which returns nothing for a foreign item) for the item's ownership, where `_insert_and_cache` has the caller's own compare-and-set; the stock cache moving before the ledger insert is closed by the fixed-assets-and-inventory entry on a refused ledger insert (POST-A-321). **WHAT THE PRIVILEGED CLIENT DOES NOT CARRY, CHECKED:** it has no `auth.uid()`, so `trg_audit_capture` (migration 111), which writes an `audit_log` row only when there is a signed-in actor, would skip these writes; none of the four tables carries that trigger in production (read on 22-10-2026: `filings` has only `trg_filings_updated_at`, the other three have no trigger at all), `created_by` on the credit and stock tables is passed explicitly, and the mark-filed callers write their own `log_event`. **WHAT NOTHING HERE CAN CONFIRM:** the sandbox cannot see which commit Render is serving (`/health` does not expose it; the release is named only in Sentry and in Render's own dashboard), so "at least an hour later" is a delay and not a check. Before a revoke merges, the Render dashboard (or the owner) must show the merge commit of this change as live; and once a revoke has applied, rolling the API back below this commit reproduces the soft failure above. **A LIVE ROW IS REFRESHED BEFORE A SOFT-DELETED TWIN:** the table has no unique key, so when both match, refreshing the dead one would leave two live rows claiming the period. Tests: `tests/test_the_record_that_a_return_was_filed_is_written_with_the_privileged_client_only.py` (12 cases, no database) and `tests/test_the_party_credit_tables_and_the_stock_ledger_are_written_with_the_privileged_client_only.py` (20 cases, no database), both reading the AST through ONE scanner (`tests/_table_uses.py`; two copies had drifted, and one could not see a table name held in a constant): every module of the API, `scripts/` included, names the only functions that write each table and the client each writes through, and the scanner is shown a query kept in a variable, a constant table name, a keyword, an alias of the method, a tuple of tables, a helper argument, a dict value, `from_`, a module-level statement, an upsert and SQL text and must fire on each (a dict KEY and a comparison are labels and do not); the helpers are the only functions of those modules that name a privileged client, however imported; the privileged receiver is used ONLY to write the three tables (a read, the catalogue compare-and-set or a document-table update moved onto it is refused, because each relies on row security); nothing in the browser touches the tables and `demo_filings` is named by nothing; the refusing double is itself tested against every write verb, and records a refused attempt before it raises so a writer left on the request's client is seen even where the caller swallows the refusal; and driven through a request client that refuses the writes as `authenticated` will, every credit operation, stock movement, transfer and opening-stock batch still succeeds, each credit statement names the firm and the client, a failed application restores the balance, a re-recorded return revives a row that IS soft-deleted (the double honours `.is_()`, so a lookup that asked for live rows only fails), a live row is refreshed before a soft-deleted twin, the lookup names the type and the period it records, and a client the firm does not hold is refused.

- **THE RECORD THAT A RETURN WAS FILED IS WRITTEN BY THE API ONLY, BECAUSE THE PERIOD LOCK IS WHAT THAT ROW IS** (migration 535, POST-A-319, found by the independent review of migration 533 and reproduced on 22-10-2026). `filings` is what `period_lock_reason` and `journal_period_lock_reason` read to refuse a posting, an edit or a discard inside a period whose GSTR-1 or GSTR-3B has gone to the portal; the sales invoice, purchase bill, credit and debit note, advance-GST receipt, fixed asset and manual-journal pre-checks all depend on it. The table was granted INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE to `authenticated` and its policies are the firm's and the assignment scope, with no role tier: an assigned Reviewer read "GSTR-3B covering this date was filed on 20 Jul 2025", ran `DELETE FROM filings WHERE client_id = ...` (DELETE 1), and the same function then answered open. A lock any assigned member can remove is not a lock, and nothing records that it was removed. **WHAT 535 DOES, IN THE SHAPE OF 499, 513, 519 AND 534:** REVOKE INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE on the table from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC and check the EFFECTIVE privilege (table, column, through PUBLIC), proved again from a database that granted everything first; SELECT is untouched, because the period functions are SECURITY INVOKER and read the table as the caller, scoped to the caller's clients by the policies already there; the service role keeps its writes; no write policy is added as a second lock, because with no verb granted it would decide nothing. **THE WRITER IS ALREADY ON THE PRIVILEGED CLIENT:** nothing in `apps/web` writes the table and the only Python writer is `gst_filing_record_service.record_filing`, which the change before this one moved to the privileged client (the entry before this one carries why the writers ship first, the revival of a soft-deleted row and the deploy order), so this migration takes nothing away from a caller that still needs it. The rollback restores what the migrations granted (041 and 095: SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE to `authenticated`), which is not necessarily what production's platform defaults gave (anon, TRUNCATE): production's ACL was not read. Tests: `tests/test_535_the_record_that_a_return_was_filed_is_written_by_the_api_only_pg.py` (13 cases, real Postgres: the effective privileges at every level and from the worst case, four roles refused every write by the privilege with the row surviving and both period functions still calling the date closed, reads unchanged and scoped, the real `record_filing` as an assigned Manager with the service role as the privileged client closing the date, idempotent, reviving a soft-deleted row and closing the date again, refusing another firm's client, refused by the database without a privileged client, and the rollback bringing the lifted lock back, a re-apply ending it and a second apply harmless); the mock-mode guard of the writer is the previous change's.

- **A WRITER THAT HANDS THE CALLER'S OWN CLIENT TO A TABLE THE CALLER MAY NOT WRITE PASSES IN MOCK MODE AND IS REFUSED IN PRODUCTION, AND THE STAFF COST RATE AND THE FEE INVOICE WRITES WERE TWO SUCH** (POST-A-320, found by the independent review of migration 533 and confirmed against production on 22-10-2026). Three writers used the request's database client on tables whose signed-in write had been taken away on purpose, so with per-user sign-in on (`USE_USER_JWT`, the production default) each was refused with 42501 for every caller, a Partner included, while every double in the suite accepted it. Production's grants were READ (`has_table_privilege`, `has_column_privilege`) rather than inferred from the migrations: the signed-in role holds no UPDATE on `fee_invoices` (any column, migrations 172 and 245: the browser used to force-set `status = 'Paid'` whatever the receipt) and none on `users.cost_rate_paise` (migration 153 left it `UPDATE(full_name)` only), and none of INSERT or UPDATE on `compliance_tasks` (170); `fee_invoices` keeps INSERT and DELETE. **WHAT MOVED:** `billing_service.set_staff_cost_rate` (`PUT /api/billing/staff-cost-rates/{id}`, an API route no screen calls yet; the API client carries its wrapper) and `InvoiceRepository.update`, `delete`, `soft_delete` and `change_status` (`POST /api/invoices/run-overdue-check`, which the billing screen calls, `DELETE /api/invoices/{id}` and `PATCH /api/invoices/{id}/status`, which no screen calls) now write with the privileged client behind the routes' own `rbac` and scope checks (the nightly sweep was never affected: it has no sign-in). The privileged client carries no row security, so **EVERY STATEMENT NAMES THE FIRM**: the repository's write methods take `firm_id` as a required keyword and `_writer(firm_id)` raises on a missing one, the overdue sweep passes each row's own firm (the nightly job names each firm and the manual check the caller's; the form with no firm named has no caller today and is still confined by each row's own), and three lines of the frozen firm-scope list went (a shrink, as intended). `InvoiceRepository.create` stays on the caller's client because INSERT is still granted; if a later migration revokes it, it moves the same way. **FIVE THINGS CHANGED, ON PURPOSE:** (1) the cost-rate update used to answer success with the requested value when it matched nothing (a member of another firm, or nobody) and now answers 404, and it returns the two fields the mock returns and not the whole `users` row the privileged client hands back; (2) the status route refuses a bare `Paid` with a 422 that says to record a receipt, because since migration 172 an invoice is Paid when its receipts cover its total (`record_fee_receipt_atomic` moves `paid_paise` and the status together) and the route, unreachable in production until now, would have started letting a Paid invoice carry nothing received, the lie that migration closed; (3) **THE SAME LIE FROM THE OTHER SIDE, FOUND BY THE INDEPENDENT REVIEW:** the route checked only the target status, so a Partner could have moved a Paid invoice (or one with receipts) back to Draft, and the delete route, which trusts "Draft only", would then have soft-deleted it with its receipts orphaned. The moves a person may make are now a table (`_MANUAL_STATUS_MOVES`: Draft to Issued, Issued to Overdue and Overdue to Issued), nothing moves out of Paid, an invoice that has had money received moves only between Issued and Overdue (which is what the nightly check does), the delete route refuses a Draft that carries receipts (`record_fee_receipt_atomic` does not ask the status), and a status write is conditional on the status the route read (`only_from`), so an invoice a receipt covered between the read and the write is not overwritten with Overdue by the sweep and the route answers 409 instead of a success that changed nothing; (4) **THE WRITES NOW LEAVE AN AUDIT ROW THE DATABASE NO LONGER WRITES:** migration 111's audit trigger returns early when there is no `auth.uid()`, and the privileged client has none, so the delete route and the cost-rate route record the change themselves (`log_event`, the auth id as actor; the cost-rate row says a rate was set and not the rate), as the status route already did; (5) `billing_service.list_staff_cost_rates` read `users` through the request's client, and `users` shows a signed-in caller their own row only (migrations 153 and 506), so under per-user sign-in the Partner's list of the firm's staff cost rates held one person; it reads through the privileged client, pinned to the firm, like its sibling. **THE THIRD WRITER WAS NOT FIXED, AND WHY:** `compliance_repository.create` (`POST /api/compliance/seed`) writes the retired "System B" table `compliance_tasks`, which production holds at 0 rows (the canonical `compliance_records` holds 190) and which has no `days_remaining` column, so the seed route's insert cannot succeed even with the privileged client; no screen calls it. Propping a retired table up with a privileged writer would only have made a dead route fail with a different error, so it is filed as POST-A-322 (retire the three System B routes, the repository and then the table) and the unconfirmed remainder of the old item, the tables whose migration grants SELECT only but whose routers write with the request's client, as POST-A-323. Tests: `tests/test_the_cost_rate_and_the_fee_invoice_writes_use_the_privileged_client_pg.py` (29 cases, real Postgres: the premise in a database built from the migrations and the refusal reproduced, the rate set, cleared and refused for another firm, the overdue sweep for one firm, the form with no firm and one client, status and delete reaching only the firm's own invoice, the missing firm refused, both routes driven, every refused and every allowed status move, the 409, a Draft with receipts not deleted, the conditional write and the sweep that finds an invoice paid since it read it, the cost-rate list against the firm and against the caller's own sign-in, and the control that hands the writers the caller's own client and sees every write refused) and `tests/test_the_fee_invoice_and_cost_rate_writers_use_the_privileged_client_and_name_the_firm.py` (34 cases, no database, in the required job: the AST of every module names every writer of `fee_invoices` per function and the client each uses, every caller names the firm, the cost rate is written and listed through `db_provider.service_db()`, driven through a request client that refuses the writes every write lands on the privileged client and names the firm, the status moves and the delete rules, and the audit rows). To find the next one, ask production (`has_table_privilege('authenticated', t, 'UPDATE')`) for the table and read the writer's client: a table with no signed-in write and a writer built on `request_db` is this defect.
- **THE PARTY-CREDIT TABLES AND THE STOCK LEDGER ARE WRITTEN BY THE API'S PRIVILEGED CLIENT AND READ BY THE ASSIGNED, AND A TABLE NOTHING USES IS REACHED BY NOBODY** (migration 536, POST-A-299's fourth tranche, the write side the ledger item named as the urgent part, 22-10-2026). Four client tables carried a firm policy and nothing else, so any member of the firm, an unassigned Reviewer included, read and wrote them over PostgREST. `party_credit_balances` (SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE) is the figure `party_credit_service.apply_credit` trusts and then raises a document's `paid_paise` and status with no journal behind it, so a balance of 100 paise raised to 99,999,999 settled invoices on paper; `party_credit_ledger` (SELECT, INSERT) is the trail behind it, writable with rows nobody signed in could then remove; `inventory_stock_ledger` (SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE) is the append-only chain every later movement is priced from (`_last_ledger_row`), whose deltas closing stock is summed from and which ties to the Inventory control account, and the same Reviewer rewrote a row's value to 999,999,999 and deleted the whole ledger; `demo_filings` (ALL, TRUNCATE, TRIGGER and REFERENCES included) is migration 087's sandbox table, which nothing reads or writes any more (the browser-side demo that wrote it is deleted and the shared filing-demo framework writes nothing). **WHAT 536 DOES, IN THE SHAPE OF 499, 513, 519 AND 532:** INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, TRUNCATE, REFERENCES and TRIGGER are revoked from `authenticated` (and everything from `anon` and PUBLIC) on the three client tables, with SELECT kept because the API reads them under the caller's own sign-in (nothing in `apps/web` reads them: the browser names them in comments only); the two RESTRICTIVE policies every client table carries (`<t>_assignment_scope`, `<t>_internal_partner_only`, once-per-query form of 525 to 532, six in all) are written for all three, so a Partner reads every row, a member reads the clients they are assigned to and the practice's rows are a Partner's, and with the writes revoked the same policies are the second lock; and `demo_filings` loses every privilege from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC, needs no scope because nobody signed in reaches it, and leaves the shrink-only catalog by itself. The catalog ceiling is 18 and three client tables remain (`fx_adjustments`, `fx_revaluations`, `client_portal_sessions`). **THE WRITERS MOVED FIRST, IN THE CHANGE BEFORE THIS ONE** (`party_credit_service._credit_db`, `inventory_service.ledger_writer`, the godown transfer; see the entry on the writers of four tables), because the old API against these revoked grants fails soft and wrong; this migration therefore merges only after that change has been on Render for at least an hour. **WHAT THE REVOKE DOES NOT CHANGE:** the reads stay on the caller's sign-in, so row security (the two policies) is the second lock on them; the stock ledger is still chained by insertion order and trusts its own last row. **NOT DONE, ON PURPOSE:** the cached stock figures on `service_catalogue` (`stock_qty_units`, `avg_cost_paise`, `stock_version`) are a copy of the ledger's last row that an assigned Manager or Executive can still PATCH over PostgREST (the column is updatable under migrations 176, 188 and 241); closing stock sums the ledger's deltas, so the books are unaffected, but a stock screen reads the cache until the next movement rewrites it, and "forgeries closed" above does not cover it (POST-A-327, closed by migration 543); no role tier on the three tables (the revoked grant is the control and the routes ask the role); `fx_adjustments`, `fx_revaluations` and `client_portal_sessions` stay on POST-A-299 (the fx rows are written in the same request as the posting that creates them, by `fx_revaluation_service`, `purchase_payment_service`, `receipt_service` and one route of `routers/purchase_payments.py`, and a portal contact is a principal that is not staff). Tests: `tests/test_536_the_party_credit_tables_the_stock_ledger_and_the_demo_filings_are_written_by_the_api_only_pg.py` (58 cases, real Postgres: the two policies of each table, who sees what, the internal practice and a firm without one, the scope holding with the firm policy widened, a principal with no member row, the effective privileges at every level and from a database that granted everything first, eight forgeries refused by the privilege as six different people with the rows untouched (six of the eight work without the migration; deleting a credit-ledger row and truncating the stock ledger were never granted and are asserted so that a later grant fails), `demo_filings` closed to every signed-in role, the service role writing all three, the closing-stock function still answering, the policies as the second lock with the writes granted back, the rollback bringing the forgery back, a re-apply ending it and a second apply harmless, the plan asking the caller's clients once).
- **THE INTERNAL PRACTICE CANNOT BE ASSIGNED BY A SIGNED-IN SESSION AND IS A PARTNER'S IN NINE MORE TABLES, AN ENGAGEMENT LETTER TAKES THE TIER ITS ROUTES ASK, AND ONLY A PARTNER CLEARS THE CLIENT OF A ROW THAT IS THEN OPEN TO THE FIRM** (migration 537, POST-A-306 and POST-A-309, found by the independent reviews of migrations 529 and 530 and checked against a database built from the migrations on 22-10-2026). THREE HOLES, EACH SMALL AND EACH A DEFENCE IN DEPTH RATHER THAN A LIVE LEAK. (1) The internal practice is a Partner's alone (G1). `routers/assignments.py` refuses to assign it (400) and migration 478 made the assignment table Partner-writable, yet the table's insert and update policies asked only that the member and the client belong to the firm, so a Partner's direct PostgREST insert could assign the practice to a Manager, who then read its rows in every table whose assignment scope admits an assigned client and whose per-table guard (`<table>_internal_partner_only`) was never added. Production held no such row when the table was read on 22-10-2026. (2) The ledger counted NINE tables without the guard (the parents of the twenty children of 529: `debit_notes`, `purchase_credit_notes`, `sales_debit_notes`, `onboarding_workflows`, `tasks`, `recurring_journal_templates`, `recurring_purchase_bill_templates`, `workflow_instances`, `itr_filings`). **THE COUNT WAS THE FAMILY'S AND NOT THE DATABASE'S: 119 of the 177 tables that carry an `_assignment_scope` policy have no policy that asks the practice's id, under any name** (counted from `pg_policy` on 22-10-2026; migration 074 listed the tables of its day and nothing has asserted since that a table scoped later joined it). (3) `engagements` and `engagement_events` carried a firm policy and the assignment scope and no role tier, while every route that writes them asks `client:write`, a Manager's by default, so a Reviewer assigned to a client could update or delete that client's letter over PostgREST and a member assigned to nothing could delete a lead's letter; and a row whose client may be NULL (a letter against a lead, a property, a Tally job, a payment event with no payment) is open to the whole firm, so a member assigned to a client could set the column to NULL on that client's row and show it to everyone. **WHAT 537 DOES.** (a) Nine `<table>_internal_partner_only` policies, RESTRICTIVE FOR ALL, in the form of 527 and 528 (the role and the practice's id asked once per query; the WITH CHECK is the USING), with `client_id IS NULL OR` only on `workflow_instances`, the one of the nine whose client is nullable, and a test that reads the column's nullability so the form follows it. The twenty children follow their parents, so the 529 test's `GUARDED_PARENTS` now lists all of them and it checks that a member assigned to the practice reads none of the practice's rows in any of its twenty-one tables. (b) `partners_insert_assignments` and `partners_update_assignments` (as 530 left them) gain `client_id IS DISTINCT FROM (SELECT my_internal_client_id())` in the WITH CHECK, which is the source: with it no signed-in session creates the row the other 110 tables would have to answer, and those tables are NOT given nine more policies each (a policy that answers a row that cannot be made is cost with no reader); the delete policy is untouched so a stale row can be removed, and the migration warns with a count if any exists. The tests of the guards assign the practice as the database owner, as before. (c) Six RESTRICTIVE per-command policies on `engagements` and `engagement_events`, `my_permission('client', 'write', 'Manager')` (the pair every letter route asks, in the form of 488 and 489, so a grid grant to an Executive passes and a grid denial to a Manager does not); reads are unchanged. (d) One trigger function, `refuse_a_signed_in_member_clearing_the_parent_of_a_row(column)`, on `engagements.client_id`, `customer_payment_events.payment_id`, `properties.client_id` and `tally_migration_jobs.client_id`: a signed-in session (`current_setting('role')` is `authenticated` or `anon`, the test of migration 498) whose member is not a Partner may not change the column from a value to NULL, with SQLSTATE 42501 and a sentence. NULL to a value stays allowed (`lifecycle._link_signed_letter` stamps a lead's letter with the client just made, as the caller, and that is a write a Manager makes), every update that does not clear stays allowed, a Partner may clear it, and the privileged client and the database owner are not judged (the trigger reads the role setting, which a foreign-key action does not change: the `SET NULL` of a client's deletion passes when it runs as the owner, as the firm purge's does, and would be refused if it ran inside a signed-in non-Partner session, which is harmless because no signed-in path deletes a client, the API soft-deletes and a Partner passes). **WHAT WAS CHECKED BEFORE ADDING THE TIER AND THE TRIGGER, BECAUSE EITHER COULD HAVE BROKEN A ROUTE:** the only writers of `engagements` are the letter routes (all `client:write`), the public signing routes (the privileged client), the lead conversion (`client:write`) and no job; the only writer of `engagement_events` is `_log_engagement_event` from those same routes; the browser touches none of the four cleared tables; no route clears `client_id` or `payment_id`. The webhook that writes payment events uses `request_db()`, which the code does not show to be the privileged client, so the signed-in write on `customer_payment_events` is NOT revoked here (moving that writer first, as 535 and 536 do, would be its own change); the trigger covers the one move the ledger named. **DELIBERATELY LEFT, SAID HERE SO NOBODY REDISCOVERS IT.** The per-table guard on the other ~110 tables (see (b)); a change to `my_assigned_client_ids()` and `can_access_client()` that stopped counting the practice for a non-Partner would close even a row the privileged client inserted, at the price of changing what every scoped read returns, and is not made; the same clearing move on the other tables whose client may be NULL, filed as POST-A-326 with the list read from a migration-built database (eleven are updatable by the signed-in role); a letter or event inserted by a non-Partner with no client (a lead's letter) is still allowed, because the tier is what says who may raise one; and the trigger stops MOVING a row to the part open to the whole firm and not COPYING it, so a member who reads a payment event or a lead's letter can insert a copy with no parent (that insert is allowed, as just said) and delete the original within the write tier, which closes the in-place clear and not that. Tests: `tests/test_the_internal_practice_guard_reaches_nine_more_parents_and_the_letters_take_a_write_tier_pg.py` (70 cases, real Postgres, the roles production uses: the nine policies' shape, who reads what with a Manager assigned to both the client and the practice, a member assigned to the practice refused every write for it and allowed every write for their own client, a Partner not judged, the assignment table refusing the practice on insert and update and still taking a client of the firm, the tier by role including the two grid cases, a lead's letter, the lead-conversion link, the trigger on all four columns for a Manager, a granted Executive, a Partner, the privileged client and the owner, the foreign-key cascade, an idempotent second run and the rollback restoring the earlier behaviour exactly and a re-apply closing it again, and the six tier policies asking the helper once per query), with 32 mutants of the migration and its rollback all failed by at least one test (dropping the nine guards' WITH CHECK changes no behaviour, because Postgres applies a FOR ALL policy's USING as its check, so only the policy-shape cases fail it). **WHAT THE WHOLE-SUITE RUN CAUGHT:** the six tier policies were first written with `my_permission(...)` bare, as 488 and 489 had before 525 wrapped them, so the helper would have been evaluated once per row; `test_a_policy_asks_who_is_calling_once_per_query_and_not_once_per_row_pg` refuses that and the tier is written `(SELECT public.my_permission(...))`. A migration that adds a policy is only as tested as the whole real-Postgres suite, which a PR's own files do not run.
- **NOTHING IN THE API HARD-DELETES A CLIENT, SO NO API PATH TURNS A TALLY JOB INTO A FIRM-LEVEL ONE, THAT PREMISE IS A TEST, AND A PARTNER'S OWN TOKEN CAN STILL DO IT OVER POSTGREST** (POST-A-303, 22-10-2026). `tally_migration_jobs.client_id` is ON DELETE SET NULL and a job with no client is open to the whole firm by design (migration 528), so a client row that was hard-deleted would turn its Tally job, and the parsed ledgers, customers and vendors under it, into a job every member of the firm reads. The item was filed as a defect of "deleting a client"; it is not reachable through the API. The API's client delete is a SOFT delete (`client_repo.soft_delete` sets `deleted_at` and `is_deleted` and leaves `client_id` on every row that hangs from the client, the job included), a client with historical records cannot be deleted at all (`_check_delete_blockers`), and the one statement that removes client rows, the `platform_purge_firm` function, removes the whole firm with them and clears every firm-scoped table, the Tally jobs and their items among them, first. So nothing was built for the job, and the severity rested on "nothing hard-deletes a client", which is stated as a test (the rule of `test_an_unreachable_premise_fails_when_it_becomes_reachable.py`, whose two probes are a route and a symbol and cover no table verb): `test_nothing_hard_deletes_a_client_but_the_firm_purge.py` fails on a `delete` chained off `.table("clients")`, on `DELETE FROM clients` in a string, on a client delete route that stops being a soft delete, and on any migration other than the three definitions of the firm purge that deletes from `clients`; `test_a_firm_purge_removes_a_clients_tally_jobs_with_it_pg.py` counts rows after a purge (no job and no item of the purged firm, none of another firm touched) and after a soft delete (the job stays pinned to its client and stays hidden from a member who is not assigned to it). The day something in the API deletes a client row, decide in the same change whether its Tally job is kept Partner-only or deleted with it. Blind spot, written down: a client query kept in a variable and deleted later is not seen. **ONE PATH REMAINS, FOUND BY THE INDEPENDENT REVIEW OF THIS CHANGE, AND THE ITEM STAYS OPEN FOR IT:** the signed-in role holds DELETE on `clients` (migrations 010 and 041, a Partner-tier policy since 260, which the role-by-table matrix reviews), so a Partner's own token can delete a client row that has no posted ledger straight over PostgREST, and the job then becomes firm-level; nothing in `apps/web` does it, which is why a guard over the API's code does not see it. POST-A-303 is kept with that remainder (revoke DELETE on `clients` from the signed-in role, or make the job go with the client).
- **TABLES NO SIGNED-IN ROUTE WRITES ARE CLOSED TO THE SIGNED-IN ROLE, THE TABLES ONLY A MIGRATION-BUILT DATABASE HAS ARE DROPPED, AND THE STAFF INVITE'S SECRET IS SHOWN TO BE UNREADABLE TO THE FIRM, WHICH IS NOW A TEST** (migration 538, 22-10-2026). **THE FINDING:** `authenticated` held every privilege on sixteen firm-level tables behind a firm policy (a per-user one on `permission_grants`, one that follows its parent on `recurring_journal_template_lines`) and no role tier, so any member of the firm, a read-only Reviewer included, could insert, change or delete their rows over PostgREST whatever the API's checks say. Production's ACLs were read: `authenticated` held INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, TRUNCATE, REFERENCES and TRIGGER (and MAINTAIN) on each, and `anon` held TRUNCATE, REFERENCES and TRIGGER. The source was searched with the table-use scanner the earlier writer guards share (`tests/_table_uses.py`, then a grep for every spelling in `apps/api`, `apps/web`, `supabase/`, `scripts/` and the workflows), by one read-only agent, again by hand for the tables the change closes, and a third time by an independent reviewer who also looked for PostgREST embeds, SQL functions, triggers, views and every client built from the anon key: **no signed-in route writes any of them.** TEN ARE USED BY NOTHING: `ai_trigger_registry`, `entity_addresses`, `entity_documents`, `lead_activities`, `lead_contacts`, `pending_invites` (an e-mail, a role and an invite token), `permission_grants`, `retainer_logs`, `team_members` (the retired staff table; three comments still mention it) and `xbrl_tag_mappings` (in production and in no migration). ONE IS USED ONLY THROUGH THE PRIVILEGED CLIENT: `recurring_journal_template_lines` (read and written only by `recurring_journal_service` through `_db`, which is `service_db`, and no route hands that service a client of its own). FOUR ARE WRITTEN ONLY BY THE PRIVILEGED CLIENT AND READ BY THE SIGNED-IN ROLE: `email_templates`, `firm_branding`, `invoice_settings` (the bank details printed on every invoice: a Reviewer could change the account a customer is told to pay into, with no route, no audit row and no trace but the row) and `invoice_templates` (all through `branding_repository._svc`, behind `branding:write`, a Partner's). ONE HAS WRITE METHODS NOBODY CALLS: `escalation_rules` (the API only reads it, `escalation_service` and the escalation route under the caller's own sign-in; the repository's write methods are DELETED, so the inherited `create` and `update` raise NotImplementedError and a future caller fails loudly instead of reaching a database that would refuse it). **WHAT 538 DOES:** the eleven the signed-in role neither reads nor writes lose every privilege from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC; the five it only reads lose them too and `authenticated` is given SELECT back (the invoice PDF, the settings loaders, the escalation route), their policies unchanged; the service role's four privileges are granted explicitly so nothing above can have removed them through PUBLIC; the four `ri_*` tables (047: `ri_entities`, `ri_cross_client_signals`, `ri_entity_relationships`, `ri_client_entity_links`) are dropped where they exist, and production has none, so a migration-built database matches it; a DO block fails the migration unless the effective privilege (table, column, through PUBLIC) is as stated. Each table is closed only where it exists, because `xbrl_tag_mappings` is a production-only table (its ACL was read: every grant on it was made by `postgres`, the role the runner connects as, so the REVOKE removes what it names). Nothing is dropped in production (a drop there needs the production fixtures refreshed, docs/schema-drift.md) and the rollback gives back exactly what the migrations granted. **WHAT IT DOES NOT DO:** no role tier on the tables the request's client DOES write (filed as POST-A-329) and none on the nine document line tables (POST-A-328, the larger of the two, closed by migration 539 below); `entities` stays firm-wide on purpose (the note at the top of `routers/relationships.py`: that sharing is what makes cross-client match detection possible). **A PREMISE THAT WAS WRONG, AND THE TEST THAT KEEPS IT RIGHT:** the first reading was that `users.invite_token` (a staff invite's secret, stored in the clear) was readable by every member of the firm, and a hash in the employee-portal style was built. It was not needed: a signed-in session reads only its OWN `users` row (`users_own_row_select`) and a pending invitee has no auth id yet, so nobody signed in reads the row, and `accept_invite` also checks the mailbox. The change was reverted before it was committed and a test now asserts that a Partner, a Manager and a Reviewer read no row and no clear token of a pending invitee, so a policy that ever widens `users` fails there; hashing the token remains the answer if it does. **THE LESSON IS THE ORDER:** the column list said "exposed" and the policy said "not"; a finding about what a column holds is a finding about the policy that guards it only once the policy has been read. Tests: `tests/test_538_tables_no_signed_in_route_writes_are_closed_to_the_signed_in_role_pg.py` (102 cases, 6 of which skip on a migration-built database because they name `xbrl_tag_mappings`, real Postgres: the eleven tables hold nothing for `authenticated` or `anon` at any level and a Partner, a Manager and a Reviewer are refused a read and a write; the five keep SELECT only, are read without error and refused every write; a Reviewer's UPDATE of the invoice settings is refused and with the migration rolled back the same statement is accepted, the negative control; the service role keeps its privileges and still writes; the `ri_*` tables are absent; the rollback and a re-apply; a second run changes nothing; the pending invitee's row and token are read by no member; and the worst case, a database that granted everything to PUBLIC, `anon` and `authenticated` first and took the service role's own grant away, which is what production's platform defaults look like and what a database built from the migrations does not) and `tests/test_the_tables_closed_by_migration_538_have_no_signed_in_writer.py` (9 cases, no database: the ten are named by nothing in the API, the only functions that write each of the others are the named ones, the branding repository writes through `_svc` and the recurring-journal service through `_db`, both `db_provider.service_db`, that table is named by that one module and no caller of any of its functions hands it a client, `escalation_rules` has no write method, the browser reaches none of the sixteen tables by `.from()` or by an embed in a select string (a response key of the same name is not a table), and the scanner is shown each spelling a write can take); with 15 mutants of the migration, its rollback and the writers, all failed by at least one test after one round of strengthening (the first run left three survivors, `anon` and PUBLIC left out of a revoke and the service role's explicit grant dropped, all invisible on a database built from the migrations because it has no platform defaults to take away, so the worst-case test was added). **The mirror image was found on 28-10-2026** (POST-A-323): twelve tables (GSTR-4 annual's four, GSTR-9C's three, the e-commerce operator's two, `rcm_documents`, the two stock-count tables) were created with SELECT for the signed-in role and everything for the service role and nothing else, so the fear that a member could write them directly was already answered by the grants (read from production: no INSERT, UPDATE or DELETE at table or column level, each with one write policy only the service role reaches, all twelve empty). What was wrong was the other direction: their five services wrote with the caller's own session, which under `USE_USER_JWT` (on in production since 15-08-2026) is the signed-in role, so every save, withdrawal, issue and count would have been refused with 42501 the first time a practice used those screens, while every double accepted any caller. All 29 writes are now made on `privileged_for(db)` and each names the firm and the client (the updates that were keyed by id and firm gained the client); `tests/test_the_return_workpaper_tables_are_written_with_the_privileged_client_only.py` holds the writers, the receiver, the firm and client on every statement and the behaviour through a request client that refuses these tables the way the signed-in role is refused, and `tests/test_the_return_workpaper_tables_grant_no_write_to_a_signed_in_role_pg.py` holds the grants against the migration-built database. **The rest of the class is now a rule** (POST-A-354): `tests/test_a_write_of_a_table_the_signed_in_role_cannot_write_is_made_on_a_client_that_can.py` takes the 157 tables production's signed-in role cannot write (a frozen list read on 28-10-2026) and classifies every write of one of them by where its client comes from: a `privileged_for`, `service_db` or `get_service_supabase` call is privileged, a `get_supabase` or `request_db` call is the request's, a name is judged by what it was bound to (a helper such as `_lock_table_db(db)` by what it returns, `service_db_or_none() or db` as privileged because the first operand is empty only where no database is configured), and a parameter by what every caller in the source passes for it, up to five calls up, through calls it can resolve. A write on the request's client, or on one it cannot trace, fails unless it is on a reviewed list that only shrinks: three entries, the dead System B repository (POST-A-322) and the posting kernel's branch for a database double with no `rpc()`. Run on the tree before the workpaper fix it finds exactly the 21 functions of the five services, so it would have caught them; a name collision (`create`, `update`, `post` are everywhere) made a first, name-only version report writers that were not (it shares the call graph of the named-row rule, `tests/_call_graph.py`). `tests/test_the_tables_the_signed_in_role_cannot_write_are_closed_in_the_migration_built_database_pg.py` holds the premise against a migration-built database and found that two reference tables, `hsn_master` and `tds_section_limits`, are closed in production and open there (POST-A-355). It cannot see a client reached through an attribute, a dictionary or a callback, and it skips `scripts/`, which run with a service key. **THE TWO TABLES PRODUCTION CLOSED AND THE MIGRATIONS DID NOT ARE CLOSED BY A MIGRATION (POST-A-355, migration 558, 28-10-2026).** `hsn_master` and `tds_section_limits` held no INSERT, UPDATE or DELETE for the signed-in roles in production (read 28-10-2026) because of a statement no migration carried, so a database rebuilt from the migrations let a signed-in member write them. Migration 558 revokes the four verbs from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC on both, checks the effective privilege at table and column level and that `service_role` kept its write, and the exception list in the premise test is gone with the two names. ON PRODUCTION it changes one thing, which is that both signed-in roles also held TRUNCATE on both (a platform default PostgREST does not expose) and no longer do; the rollback gives TRUNCATE back and not the write verbs.
- **THE LINES OF A DOCUMENT TAKE THE ACCOUNTING WRITE TIER, A SIGNED-IN SESSION EDITS THE LINES OF A DRAFT AND ON NO OTHER DOCUMENT BUT A UNIT OR A CATALOGUE LINK, AND A FOREIGN KEY'S SET NULL IS AN UPDATE OF THE LINE.** (POST-A-328, migration 539, 23-10-2026.) **WHAT WAS WRONG:** eight line tables (`client_sales_invoice_lines`, `purchase_bill_lines`, `credit_note_lines`, `debit_note_lines`, `purchase_credit_note_lines`, `sales_debit_note_lines`, `recurring_invoice_template_lines`, `recurring_purchase_bill_template_lines`) carried the parent's READ scope (a policy asking the parent's firm and, through the parent, the client) and nothing else: no role tier and no rule about the document's state, `authenticated` held INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE and no trigger existed on any (production read on 23-10-2026: one permissive policy each, no user trigger). So a Reviewer assigned to a client, or an Executive, could change the quantity, rate, HSN or tax of a line of an ISSUED invoice, bill or note over PostgREST without touching the header or the journal: the document's PDF and any return built from its lines moved while the ledger did not. The API was right and the database was silent: the routes that write a line ask `accounting:write` (the billing run asks `billing:write`, a Partner's, and its header insert already needs `accounting:write`), a note's PATCH is draft-only (422 after issue), an invoice or bill PATCH replaces its lines only while draft, and the one post-issue line edit is a `unit` correction. The parents have had their tier since migration 487; the lines were never named in it. **WHAT 539 DOES:** (1) a RESTRICTIVE per-command policy for INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on each of the eight, `(SELECT public.my_permission('accounting', 'write', 'Manager'))` (the pair every route that writes a line asks, in the once-per-query form of 525, so a per-person grant or denial in the access grid counts on a line as it does on the header: 24 policies, each `CREATE POLICY` written out with its name as a literal because the production-drift guard finds a policy a migration adds by that text); the recurring-template lines are in the tier and have no state rule, because a template has no draft. (2) One trigger function, `refuse_a_signed_in_edit_of_lines_of_a_non_draft_document()` (56 characters: Postgres keeps 63, and the first draft's 72-character name was stored cut), on the six line tables whose documents HAVE a draft, as the trigger `a_document_that_is_not_a_draft_keeps_its_lines`: a signed-in session (`current_setting('role')` is `authenticated` or `anon`, the test of 498) may insert, update or delete a line only while its document's status is `draft`. Two exceptions, each an UPDATE that changes nothing else (the whole row is compared with the named columns and `updated_at` taken out, so a line cannot be moved to another document under cover of either): a change of `unit` on an invoice or bill line of a document that is not cancelled (the correction the API itself allows), and clearing `service_catalogue_id` or `purchase_order_line_id` on a line of a CANCELLED document, which is what the foreign key's ON DELETE SET NULL does when a product or service preset is deleted. The code tests the SHAPE of that change and not its cause, so a Manager can also clear either link directly on a cancelled document's line; that cuts a reference and changes no amount (the purchase cycle already leaves cancelled bills out of what an order has billed), and only the catalogue link can reach a signed-in session as a foreign-key action today. A MOVE of a line from one document to another is judged against both. The privileged client, the database owner and the firm purge (replica mode) are not judged, and a document that cannot be found is not judged either: when a document's own delete cascades to its lines the document row is already gone when the line is reached (the cascade runs under the deleting person's role setting, so it is the missing parent, not the role, that lets it through). The parent's status is read FOR SHARE, so a line write that races the issuing of its document waits for the issue to commit and is then judged on the new status. The function reads the parent as its owner so row security cannot hide the status, and EXECUTE on it is revoked from the signed-in roles. (3) The policies and the triggers are created inside ONE `DO` block with every statement written out literally, and the rollback drops them in one block too: a table-locking statement at the top level costs a round trip while its lock is held, and the second review measured the earlier shape (about seventy top-level statements) through a proxy adding a quarter of a second to each round trip, an assumed figure and not the runner's: the invoice-lines table was locked about 15 seconds, past the 8 seconds a signed-in request may run in production (`authenticated` has `statement_timeout=8s`, read on 23-10-2026), and a reader holding a lock on a later table made the apply fail on its own 15 second lock timeout; inside one block the same DDL took about 3 seconds end to end and no reader waited more than half a second. A test keeps every `CREATE` or `DROP` of a policy or trigger out of the top level of both files. A closing `DO` check fails the migration unless the 24 policies and 6 triggers exist and no signed-in role can execute the function. **THE FIRST REVIEW (THREE READERS) FOUND, AND IT WAS NOT SMALL:** one hunting a route the rule would break, one trying to get round it, one on the migration and the tests. All three found, and one reproduced as a Partner and as a Manager, that **deleting a product or service preset used only on a cancelled invoice or bill failed 42501 for everybody**: `delete_service` deliberately lets a preset go when every document that carries it is a draft or cancelled, and the foreign key's SET NULL is an UPDATE of the line made under the deleting person's role setting, so the new trigger judged it.
