The first draft's claim that no code edits a line of an issued document except the unit correction was false because nobody had listed the DATABASE'S OWN actions as writers; the foreign-key actions that can touch these tables are now listed (SET NULL on the two reference columns, SET NULL on a template line's `expense_account_id` and `service_catalogue_id` (migration 379), where there is no state rule, and the CASCADE from the document), and the purchase order's re-pricing, which deletes order lines, runs on the privileged client. The second finding would have turned the required real-Postgres check red: the 24 policies were created inside `format()`, which the production-drift guard cannot read, so it saw 24 restrictive policies production does not have (migration 537 says in a comment why every `CREATE POLICY` is literal). The third was the race with issuing. The rest were the function name, a refusal sentence that pasted a plural table name into "a %" and gave the same advice for a cancelled document, and claims in the migration header, the test docstring and this entry that were stronger than what was asserted (six people where the matrix has seven; a second-run test that compared a count; an owner/service test that ran an UPDATE only). **THE SECOND REVIEW (TWO READERS, ONE ON LOCKING AND COST, ONE ON BYPASS AND THE RECORDS) FOUND:** the lock hold above (fixed by the one block); that the trigger name said the opposite of the rule (`a_draft_keeps_its_lines_unless_it_is_a_draft`, now `a_document_that_is_not_a_draft_keeps_its_lines`); that the header, this entry and the ledger said things the code does not: the foreign-key exception is a shape and not an action, the cascade passes because the parent is gone and not because it "runs as the owner", the list of foreign-key actions was incomplete, "the parents had their tier since 487 to 489" is true of 487 only, the headline said "nothing else" beside two exceptions, and POST-A-330 omitted the three shipping-bill fields the invoice PATCH must keep editable after issue (customs issues the bill later), mis-stated which roles may update a bill and which tables carry an audit trigger, and called a hole not reproduced that the reviewer reproduced in minutes; and two costs of the rule, recorded here and not fixed. **THE COSTS OF THE RULE:** reading the status FOR SHARE waits for any open transaction that has updated the document's header (nothing in the code holds one longer than a statement), and an UPDATE or DELETE of a line locks the LINE row before the trigger runs while a DELETE of the header locks the header and then reaches its lines through the cascade, so deleting a draft while one of its lines is being edited can deadlock (40P01): one request fails after about a second and a retry works, nothing is corrupted, and the review reproduced it with a loop of one deleter and four editors on one document and not with anything an ordinary session does; the lock cannot be weaker, because only a lock that conflicts with the issuing UPDATE closes the race with issuing. And the API replaces a draft's lines as a delete and then an insert, two requests: if another member issues the document between them, the insert is now refused and the issued document is left without lines, where before 539 the insert would have succeeded and left lines that differ from the journal (POST-A-332: one write, header locked first, because a function that writes lines first and the header after deadlocks against a concurrent one). **WHAT IT DOES NOT DO, ON PURPOSE:** the recurring-template PARENTS (and `recurring_journal_templates`) carry no tier in 487 to 489 (POST-A-329), and the HEADERS of the six documents carry no state rule, so a member with the tier can still delete an issued document with its lines or alter its frozen columns and move it back to `draft`, after which its lines are editable again (POST-A-330, closed by migration 541, which refuses all of that except the received bill whose journal failed; what stays open is POST-A-331, the status and settlement columns that about a dozen legitimate paths write under the caller's role); the second review reproduced the move back and the delete as an assigned Manager and as an assigned Executive, and a Reviewer was refused. This migration is a floor under the lines, not a seal. The signed-in roles also keep the platform's TRUNCATE, TRIGGER, REFERENCES and MAINTAIN privileges on the eight tables (read in production; PostgREST issues none of them). Not compared: whether production's cascade order on a client delete matches a migration-built database's (here the invoices cascade before the catalogue SET NULL, so a Partner's delete passes); nothing in the API hard-deletes a client and POST-A-303 revokes the privilege. Nothing is rewritten, so existing issued documents keep their lines. **THE LESSON IS A LIST:** a rule on a table has to name every writer of it, and the database's own actions (a cascade, a SET NULL, a trigger's update) are writers that no route scan finds; an equivalent trap is a migration that builds a policy name at run time, which a guard reading the text cannot see; and a migration that takes table locks takes them in one statement, because the runner's round trips are inside the lock. Tests: `tests/test_539_the_document_line_tables_take_the_accounting_write_tier_and_a_signed_in_session_edits_the_lines_of_a_draft_only_pg.py` (205 cases, real Postgres: the tier for each of the eight tables and seven people, and that the people it refuses still read; every write to the lines of a document that is not a draft refused by status and by table, and the same writes passing on a draft; the unit correction and each way it must fail; the preset delete for a Manager and a Partner on a draft, a cancelled and an issued document, and the link exception changing nothing else; the anonymous role made reachable in a worst case; the race with two sessions; the owner and the service role inserting, updating and deleting; a Partner's delete of a draft and of an issued document cascading without the trigger refusing; a document built in steps; the policies asked once per query; the lock-taking statements inside one block in both files; the trigger function closed to a signed-in role even when the platform granted it first; the rollback bringing the forgery back and a re-apply ending it; and a second run leaving every policy, trigger and the function as they were). Mutation: 42 mutants of the migration and its rollback (the tier pair, a dropped command, a bare policy, the anonymous role, the draft test, each exception widened or narrowed, the lock, the cascade case, the EXECUTE revoke, a policy written by `format()`, a lock-taking statement moved out of the block or the block split in two, a rollback that leaves a trigger, a policy, the function or a top-level drop) were each run against this file in a separate copy, and all 42 are caught after two rounds of strengthening: the first run left survivors for the bare (per-row) policy shape, the anonymous role not being judged, the function left executable by a signed-in role and the advice sentence for a cancelled document, and the second (after the one-block change) added the shape test and a rollback test that reads the catalogue afterwards; two further mutants of the first round were equivalent (an UPDATE check that restates its USING, and a clause the foreign key makes redundant) and the redundant clause was removed.
- **A SIGNED-IN MEMBER WHO IS NOT A PARTNER CANNOT CLEAR THE CLIENT OF ELEVEN MORE TABLES OR MOVE A SHARED ACCOUNT, THE SAME FUNCTION GUARDS THE WORKFLOW OF AN ONBOARDING TASK, AND NOBODY SIGNED IN CAN DELETE A CLIENT ROW.** (POST-A-326 and POST-A-303, migration 540, 23-10-2026.) **WHAT WAS WRONG:** migration 537 closed one move on four tables, a member assigned to a client setting that client's row to NO client (`UPDATE ... SET client_id = NULL`) so that the assignment scope, which admits a client-less row to every member of the firm, shows it to everybody. The same move was open on every other table whose client may be NULL and whose signed-in role holds UPDATE on it: `activity_logs`, `ai_evidence`, `ai_signals`, `chart_of_accounts`, `client_lifecycle_events`, `knowledge_articles`, `mca_filings`, `proposals`, `reminders`, `scheduled_reports`, `task_recurring_configs` and `time_entries`, plus the child column `onboarding_tasks.workflow_id` (production read on 23-10-2026: `authenticated` held UPDATE on all twelve, among other verbs, and none carried a trigger of that kind). `chart_of_accounts` is the one where the harm is more than a read, and in both directions: a row with no client is a firm-level account that any of the firm's entries may use (migration 360), so clearing a client's account puts it in every other client's chart, and setting a shared account's client to one client takes it from every other client, whose postings to it are then refused by 360's own trigger until a Partner moves it back (production's chart is almost all firm-level: 133 accounts, 2 with a client, per 360's header; no flow fills the column on an existing account). `knowledge_articles` was found by the review of this migration, not by the writer readers: its scope policy is called `_assignment` and not `_assignment_scope`, a Manager reads every article already, and clearing the client of one shows it to the Executives and Reviewers who are not assigned to it. Separately the signed-in role held DELETE on `clients` (migrations 010 and 041, a Partner-tier policy since 260, the per-person grid since 415): the API soft-deletes and the browser never deletes, so nothing used it, but a Partner's own token could delete a client that has no row in any NO ACTION table over PostgREST, cascading into about 139 child tables, among them tables the signed-in role cannot delete from (a foreign-key cascade is not a privilege check), and turning `tally_migration_jobs` and `engagements` into firm-wide rows through their ON DELETE SET NULL. **WHAT 540 DOES:** (1) the trigger function of 537, `refuse_a_signed_in_member_clearing_the_parent_of_a_row('<column>')`, unchanged, is attached as BEFORE UPDATE OF <column> to twelve columns (every one above except the chart's; a statement that does not name the column never runs it): a signed-in session whose member is not a Partner may not change the column from a value to NULL; filling a NULL, leaving a NULL null and sending the value it already has (the mark-sent route of `reminders` re-sends the whole row it read) are untouched; a Partner, the privileged client, the owner and the firm purge are not judged. (2) A new function, `refuse_a_signed_in_member_changing_the_client_of_a_row('client_id')`, on `chart_of_accounts` alone refuses ANY change of the column (a value to NULL, NULL to a value, one client to another) by the same people and spares the same ones. The thirteen triggers are created inside ONE `DO` block, as 539 learned. (3) `public.clients`: `REVOKE ALL` from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC, then SELECT, INSERT and UPDATE back to `authenticated` and the service role's four explicitly (the shape of 538), with a closing check of the effective table and column privileges; the policies, `clients_role_delete` included, are not touched, so a privilege handed back later is still refused to all but a Partner. **HOW IT WAS CHECKED BEFORE IT WAS ADDED:** four read-only readers went through the writers table by table (the table-use scanner, the browser's `.from()` sites and API wrappers, every SQL function and trigger, every foreign-key action) and none found a path that clears one of the columns as a signed-in session: three of the tables have no writer at all, `chart_of_accounts` is written through the privileged client with no UPDATE naming `client_id`, `client_lifecycle_events` is insert-only, `knowledge_articles` is inserted with its client and updated for its version and `is_archived` only, `mca_filings` and `proposals` are updated for status and notes only, `reminders` goes back with the value it had, `scheduled_reports` is toggled for `is_active`, the recurring-task PUT drops null fields, the time-entry update model has no client field and both onboarding inserts set the workflow. For `clients` the scanner found 12 writes, all inserts or updates, the application code has 29 `.from("clients")` sites in 27 files (they select, insert at two sites and update at one), and the firm purge is SECURITY DEFINER and executable by the service role only (a test runs it after the revoke). The production ACL of `clients` was read: every grant, and no column privilege, was made by `postgres`, the role the runner connects as, so the REVOKE removes what it names. **WHAT IT DOES NOT DO, ON PURPOSE:** the copy-and-delete variant (insert a client-less copy of a row the member reads, then delete the original) is stopped by no trigger and stays open wherever a tier admits a client-less insert; a Partner can still clear the column; `task_recurring_configs` can still be moved from one client to another by a member who may write both; the signed-in roles keep the platform's TRUNCATE, REFERENCES and TRIGGER on the eleven tables (PostgREST issues none), and closing the write grants of `activity_logs`, `ai_evidence` and `ai_signals`, which nothing writes, as 505 and 538 did for others, would be the cleaner end state and is left as a follow-up because a trigger leaves the platform's grants as they are. **THE MATRIX NO LONGER MEASURES `clients_role_delete`:** with the privilege gone every actor's delete cell reads `x` in `rls_expected_access.json`, so the role-by-table matrix cannot see that policy move; the second-lock file is wired to the tables 499 and 515 revoke whole and was not extended for one verb, and `test_540` measures the policy directly with the privilege handed back, on a client ASSIGNED to the Manager, the Executive and the Reviewer (the first version deleted an unassigned client, which the assignment scope hides whether or not the policy exists, so the test could not fail; the reviewer proved it by dropping the policy and watching it stay green), with a negative control that neutralises the policy and shows the Manager's delete passing. The test of 498 that deletes a client as a signed-in Partner now hands the privilege back first. **WHAT THE INDEPENDENT REVIEW FOUND (two readers, on the first version):** the three things above that were wrong in it (a second-lock test that could not fail; `knowledge_articles` missed because the catalogue rule keyed on a policy NAME; the chart's opposite move), a catalogue rule blind to a column-only UPDATE grant (this repo grants columns to the signed-in role), a closing check that counted trigger names and would have passed a trigger attached to the wrong table, column or argument (it compares table, name, `BEFORE UPDATE OF` exactly its column, function and argument now) and could not see MAINTAIN on a PG16 CI database (it reads the ACL itself now), no function-privilege worst case for the new function (the mutation run found it too: a revoke that forgot `authenticated` survived because a migration-built database never grants it), and statements stronger than the code (the lock_timeout of migrations 533 to 535 does not exist, the review of 539 did not 'measure' the runner's round trip, the browser's 29 sites and not 44, 138 foreign keys and not 'about 139'). **THE LESSON** is the one 537 wrote and the catalogue now holds: a rule about a column has to be asked of every table that has the column, so the test derives the family from the catalogue (every public table with a nullable `client_id` and an UPDATE grant on that column, table or column level, whatever its policies are called) and fails a table added later that carries no guard or no named reason (`notifications` is read by its recipient and not by the client's assignees); the tables that are read-only to the signed-in role (`ai_recommendations`, `workflow_instances`, `demo_filings`) are found by their grant and not by a list. Tests: `tests/test_540_a_member_who_is_not_a_partner_cannot_clear_the_client_of_eleven_more_tables_and_nobody_signed_in_can_delete_a_client_pg.py` (98 cases, real Postgres: each of the twelve columns refused to a Manager and cleared by a Partner, the privileged client and the owner, the untouched writes, the catalogue rule and its sensitivity to a handed-back grant or a removed guard, the effective privileges on `clients` including the worst case where the platform's defaults granted everything first, every kind of signed-in person refused a delete, the product's reads, inserts and updates of a client still working, the policy as the second lock, the one-block shape of both files, a second run changing nothing, and the rollback bringing the old behaviour back and a re-apply ending it). Mutation: 31 mutants of the migration and its rollback (a trigger missing on four tables, on the knowledge articles and on the chart; a trigger watching another column or passing another argument; the revoke forgetting `anon`, PUBLIC or `authenticated` or taking only DELETE; each of `authenticated`'s three verbs and the service role's DELETE or its explicit grant dropped; a lock-taking statement outside the block or the block split in two; a trigger firing on every update; the chart function refusing only a clear, refusing a Partner, judging the privileged client or executable by `authenticated`; the client delete policy dropped; a rollback that leaves a trigger, the function or the old DELETE) were each run against this file in a separate copy, and all 31 are caught; the first run of the set left one survivor, the chart function executable by `authenticated`, because a migration-built database never grants it, and the worst-case test above was added and now fails it.
- **THE WRITERS OF THE TWO FX RECORD TABLES MOVE TO THE PRIVILEGED CLIENT BEFORE THE GRANT IS TAKEN AWAY, AND THE PORTAL SESSION LOG ALREADY WAS.** (POST-A-299's last tranche, first half, 23-10-2026; Python only, no migration.) **WHAT WAS WRONG:** `fx_adjustments` (the realised gain or loss of a foreign receipt or vendor payment and the unrealised one of a revaluation, which the FX reports and the vendor statement read back), `fx_revaluations` (the rows the next revaluation diffs against, so a forged row changes the next journal) and `client_portal_sessions` (the log the health score reads a client's login recency from) carry a firm policy and no assignment scope and no role tier, and `authenticated` holds INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on all three (migrations 156 and 194), so any member of the firm, an unassigned Reviewer included, writes them over PostgREST (reproduced on a throwaway database on 21-10-2026 for the session log). **WHAT THIS DOES:** the five writes of the two FX tables go through `domain/currency/fx_writer.fx_writer(db)`: the privileged client where there is one, the caller's own where there is none (mock mode, a test double), `None` staying `None`, the shape of `_credit_db` and `ledger_writer` of 536's first half. The writes are the foreign receipt (`receipt_service.create_foreign_receipt`), the two foreign vendor payment paths (`purchase_payment_service.create_foreign_payment_core` and `routers/purchase_payments._create_foreign_payment`, which duplicates it) and the revaluation (`fx_revaluation_service.revalue`, the run row and the adjustment). Every one is an INSERT that already names the firm and the client, because the privileged client carries no row security, and every route that reaches one has checked the caller's access to the client; reads (the FX reports, the vendor statement, the reversal's `_payment_ap_relief`, the revaluation's own `_prior_runs`) stay on the caller's sign-in, so row security is the second lock there. `client_portal_sessions` needed no move: its one writer (`core/portal_auth._touch_portal_session`, a best-effort touch that must never fail a portal request) already writes with `get_service_supabase()`, and the guard now holds that. **WHY IT SHIPS ALONE:** the old API against revoked grants fails soft and wrong (a foreign receipt would post its journal and then fail to record the exchange difference that the FX report and a reversal read), so the migration that takes the writes away from `authenticated` and writes the two scope policies for the three tables follows at least an hour after this has been on Render, as 536 followed its writers. **NOT DONE, ON PURPOSE:** the grants are not revoked here and the three tables are still on the shrink-only list of `assignment_scope_exceptions.json`; the FX rows are still written in requests of their own after the posting that creates them (they never shared its transaction), and the compensation paths of the receipt and the payment delete nothing from these tables. Tests: `tests/test_the_fx_record_tables_and_the_portal_session_log_are_written_with_the_privileged_client_only.py` (14 cases, mock mode: the only writers of each of the three tables, derived from the AST of every module of the API with `scripts/` included, with the shapes a write can hide in shown to the detector; each FX write goes through the helper and never the `db` it was handed; the functions that write them name no privileged accessor and the helper is the only one that does; the privileged receiver is used only to write the two FX tables; nothing in the browser touches the tables; the helper's three cases; and, driven through a request client that REFUSES the two tables' writes as `authenticated` will, a foreign receipt, a single-bill and a multi-bill foreign vendor payment and a revaluation still succeed with every FX row on the privileged client carrying the firm and the client). Six of the fourteen fail against the previous code (verified with the four source files reverted).

- **THE HEADER OF AN ISSUED INVOICE, BILL OR NOTE KEEPS ITS PARTICULARS, A SIGNED-IN SESSION CANNOT DELETE ONE OR INSERT ONE ISSUED, AND THE DATABASE'S OWN CASCADE IS TOLD APART FROM A STATEMENT BY THE TRIGGER DEPTH.** (POST-A-330, migration 541, 23-10-2026.) **WHAT WAS WRONG:** migration 539 put the accounting tier and a draft-only rule on the LINES of a document, and the HEADERS of the six documents (`client_sales_invoices`, `purchase_bills`, `credit_notes`, `debit_notes`, `purchase_credit_notes`, `sales_debit_notes`) carried a role tier since 487 and nothing that asks what state the row is in, so a member who holds the tier (INSERT and DELETE of any of the six need `accounting:write`, a Manager, and so does UPDATE of the four notes; UPDATE of an invoice also opens to an Executive through `banking:write` or `invoice:write`, and UPDATE of a bill through `banking:write`) could, over PostgREST and while the API refuses every one of these, change an issued document's amounts, tax, party, number, dates or supply fields (the PDF, the return built from it and the ledger then disagree), delete an issued document with its lines (reproduced as a Manager on 23-10-2026), insert a document already issued (for the four note tables that went round the draft the numbering function creates), soft-delete an issued document and hide it from every report, or move an issued document back to `draft`, which opens its lines again and so undoes 539. **WHAT 541 DOES:** one SECURITY DEFINER trigger function, `refuse_a_signed_in_edit_of_a_document_that_is_not_a_draft(allowed, opening, label)`, on the six header tables, BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE OR DELETE, judging only a signed-in session (`current_setting('role')` is `authenticated` or `anon`, the test of 498 and 539; the privileged client, the owner and the firm purge pass untouched): an INSERT must be a draft except an opening document (`is_opening`, which the API creates `issued` (an invoice) or `received` (a bill) with no tax, no TDS, no ineligible credit, no discount, no round-off, no payment and no journal entry, so the exemption is held to exactly that shape, its total being the balance carried over, which the member states as through the API, the status coming from the trigger's second argument, `opening:issued` or `opening:received`; the four note tables have none); a DELETE only of a draft, except when `pg_trigger_depth()` is above one, which is how a foreign-key cascade (the delete of a customer, vendor, client or firm reaching its documents) is told from a statement and why a Partner's delete of a customer still works (the API's own dependency guards decide whether a party may be deleted at all); an UPDATE of a draft is not judged (the API decides what a draft may change, and issuing is an update of a draft); an UPDATE of a document that is not a draft may change only the columns listed for its table, and anything else is refused in words naming up to six of them. The lists are the union of what the legitimate writers change, read from the code and from the first review: soft fields (references, notes, due date, credit days, the three shipping-bill fields on an invoice, the attached document and the Form 15CA/15CB references on a bill), the settlement and status columns the receipt, payment, bank, party-credit and note-application paths write, the collections metadata and `landed_cost_basis`; everything that says WHAT was supplied, to WHOM, for HOW MUCH and ON WHICH DAY, the number, the currency and the tax is frozen. the journal link of a document that is not a draft cannot be changed once it is made, even when it is wrong or points at another firm's entry (the receive, the repost and the issue only ever set one where there is none; correcting one is the privileged client's); a move of a non-draft back to `draft` is refused except the one the API makes, a received bill that is not an opening bill, was received in the last ten minutes (`received_at` is stamped by the receive and the rollback runs in the same request; without the window the three amount columns, which stay writable, could be zeroed first and a bill received days ago with no journal link walked back to a draft), has no journal link yet and nothing paid, debited or credited against it, whose `received_at` is cleared in the same statement and nothing else changed; `deleted_at` may change only on an opening document with nothing paid against it (the API's own 409); the two generated outstanding columns are ignored by name because a BEFORE trigger's `NEW` does not carry their recomputed value; an UPDATE made by a foreign-key action (ON DELETE SET NULL of the journal link, the billing schedule or the recurring template) runs one level deeper and is not judged. The six triggers are created inside ONE `DO` block (539's lesson) that first takes the six tables' lock in `SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE` mode with `NOWAIT` and, when one is busy, gives the attempt up holding nothing, waits half a second and tries again for fifteen seconds before failing in its own words (a plain request would queue every later write behind it, and six taken one after another would hold the earlier tables while waiting for the later; the triggers are made with `CREATE OR REPLACE TRIGGER`, which takes that mode whether or not the trigger exists, measured on 16, because a `DROP TRIGGER` of an existing one takes `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`, which on a re-apply queues behind every reader and can deadlock a transaction that reads and then writes, as the review of the neighbouring migration 543 reproduced, so this migration drops nothing), the rollback takes the same shape, EXECUTE is revoked from the signed-in roles, and a closing check compares each trigger's table, event, function, arguments and tgtype. **THE SAME CHANGE** closes two neighbours the work found: the customer's permanent-delete guard did not name `sales_debit_notes`, whose `customer_id` cascades, so a customer whose only record was an issued §34(3) debit note could be hard-deleted and the document would go with it, and the vendor's did not name `recurring_purchase_bill_templates`; both are on their lists, with the labels in the two delete dialogs, and `tests/test_the_party_delete_guard_names_every_table_that_cascades_from_a_party_pg.py` asks the catalogue which tables cascade from `customers` and `vendors` and requires each to be on the router's list or on a shrink-only list that says why not (six tables are, POST-A-334). 539's test of a Partner's delete of an issued document now deletes the customer or vendor (the way it still can), and for the two note tables whose vendor has no foreign key, takes the header's own rule out of the way so that what is under test stays the line trigger's. **WHAT IT DOES NOT DO, ON PURPOSE:** `status`, `paid_paise`, `paid_txn`, `credited_paise`, `debit_note_paise`, `debited_paise`, `credit_note_paise`, `cancelled_at` and `journal_entry_id` (where it is NULL) stay writable by a member who holds the tier, because about a dozen API paths write them under the caller's role, so such a member can still mark an invoice paid, cancel it or re-issue a cancelled one, and an UPDATE of a DRAFT is not judged, so a document can still be ISSUED over PostgREST in two statements (edit the draft, then set its status), carrying whatever particulars the draft was given and none of the API's issue checks (POST-A-331: the writers move to the privileged client first, then the columns close; the first draft of this entry and of the migration's header said a document could not be 'born issued', which was true of the INSERT only, and the review reproduced the two-statement route); a cancelled document's soft fields stay editable as they are in the API; a TRUNCATE is not seen by a row trigger and PostgREST has no verb for it; nothing is rewritten. **WHAT THE INDEPENDENT REVIEW FOUND (three readers, on three versions):** an opening-document exemption that let a Manager create an issued, paid or cancelled document with real tax in one statement (held to the API's shape now, and to the status the trigger's argument names, not a column a note table might gain); a rewrite of a paid bill through the way back to a draft (clear the journal link, move the bill back to received, then to a draft with `received_at` cleared), and an opening bill that went back to a draft in one statement (the link is permanent, the exception is for a bill that is not an opening bill); the second-statement issue of a draft, which the first draft of this entry had said could not happen; the zero-the-amounts-first walk-back of a bill received days earlier (the ten minutes); an opening screen that left `tds_rate_bps`, the TDS cess, the ineligible credit, the discount and the round-off unread; six table locks taken one after another holding the earlier ones while waiting for the later (the NOWAIT loop); `to_jsonb` of both rows six times a row (once, and only for a document that is not a draft); a depth-rule test whose docstring said a function writes at depth two when only a write from inside another trigger does; an allowed-column test that a database refusal or a policy that updated nothing would have passed (it asserts one row written); and header statements stronger than the facts. Tests: `tests/test_541_the_header_of_an_issued_document_keeps_its_particulars_and_is_not_deleted_or_born_issued_from_a_signed_in_session_pg.py` (383 cases, real Postgres: every column of each header classified once as allowed, frozen or generated, from the catalogue; each frozen column refused to a Manager on a non-draft and each allowed column passing; a draft changed freely and issued, then locked; the move back to draft refused and the one exception and the ways it must fail; the soft delete of an opening document; inserts refused issued and passing as a draft or an opening document; deletes refused unless draft and passing as a cascade from a customer or vendor; the foreign-key SET NULL not judged; the privileged client and the owner changing, deleting and inserting anything; the anonymous role judged as well; the function closed to a signed-in role even when the platform granted it first; the one-block shape of both files; a second run changing nothing; and the rollback bringing the forgery back and a re-apply ending it). Mutation: 100 mutants of the migration and its rollback, run on the version before the last two changes, caught 96; the four survivors are a gap that got a test (a bill in any status but `received` has no way back, whatever else looks like a fresh receive) and three that are redundant by construction (the opening exemption's table argument is backed by the status check beside it; the soft delete reading the flag off `NEW` rather than `OLD` is backed by `is_opening` being frozen; the received-bill exception's table-name test is backed by the `received` status and by `received_at` existing only on bills). The 35 mutants that the later changes touched (the triggers made with `CREATE OR REPLACE`, the rollback, the lock loop, the ten-minute window, the opening screen, the bill status) were all caught on the final version.

- **THE TWO FX RECORD TABLES AND THE PORTAL SESSION LOG ARE WRITTEN BY THE API ONLY AND READ BY THE ASSIGNED, AND THE SHRINK-ONLY LIST OF CLIENT TABLES WITH NO SCOPE HAS NO PENDING ENTRY LEFT.** (migration 542, POST-A-299's last tranche, 23-10-2026.) **WHAT WAS WRONG:** `fx_adjustments`, `fx_revaluations` and `client_portal_sessions` carried a NOT NULL client and a firm policy and nothing else, and `authenticated` held INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on all three (migrations 149, 156 and 194; production read on 23-10-2026: `arwdDxtm` for the signed-in role and `Dxtm` for `anon`, every grant made by `postgres`, one policy and no trigger each), so any member of the firm, an unassigned Reviewer included, read and wrote them over PostgREST. `fx_adjustments` is what the FX reports and the vendor statement read the realised and unrealised gain or loss from and what a reversal reads to undo a payment's AP relief; the rows of `fx_revaluations` are what the next revaluation diffs against (`_prior_runs`), so a forged row changes the next journal; `client_portal_sessions` is the log the client-health score reads login recency from (a Reviewer forged a row on a throwaway database on 21-10-2026). **WHAT 542 DOES, IN THE SHAPE OF 536:** INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, TRUNCATE, REFERENCES and TRIGGER (and MAINTAIN where the server has it) are revoked from `authenticated`, everything from `anon` and PUBLIC, SELECT is kept for `authenticated` because the API reads the tables under the caller's own sign-in, the service role's four privileges are granted explicitly, and the two RESTRICTIVE policies every client table carries (`<t>_assignment_scope` and `<t>_internal_partner_only`, in the once-per-query form of 525 to 536, six in all) are written, so a member reads the clients they are assigned to, a Partner reads every row of the firm and the practice's rows are a Partner's; with the writes revoked the same policies are the second lock on the write side. No role tier is added: the revoked grant is the control, and the routes that write these tables ask the role themselves. **THE WRITERS MOVED FIRST, IN THE CHANGE BEFORE THIS ONE** (`domain/currency/fx_writer.fx_writer` for the foreign receipt, the two foreign vendor payment paths and the revaluation; the portal touch has always used the service client), because the old API against revoked grants fails soft and wrong (a foreign receipt posts its journal and then cannot record the exchange difference); this migration therefore merges only after that change has been on Render for at least an hour. **WHAT THE LESSON OF 539 CHANGED:** the policy, grant and `ALTER TABLE` statements are inside ONE `DO` block with every statement literal (the production-drift guard finds a policy by its `CREATE POLICY` text), and a test keeps every `CREATE`, `DROP`, `GRANT`, `REVOKE`, `LOCK` and `ALTER` out of the top level of both files, because a lock taken at the top level is held across the runner's round trips (`DROP POLICY` and `CREATE POLICY` take `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`, measured; `GRANT` and `REVOKE` take no table lock and sit there so that the file is one unit). **THE BLOCK ASKS FOR ITS LOCK WITHOUT QUEUEING** (the independent review of this change, after the same finding on 541 and 543): all three tables in `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` mode with `NOWAIT`, retried every half second for fifteen seconds and then failing in its own words, because a plain request would queue every later read and write behind it and three taken one after another would hold the earlier tables while waiting for the later; the rollback has the same shape. The block also turns row security ON for the three tables (a no-op where it is on) and the closing check fails when it is off: with it off the six policies decide nothing and the check, which counted policies, stayed green. The closing check reads the ACL itself (a grant through PUBLIC, and PostgreSQL 17's MAINTAIN, which the PG16 CI database lacks, are seen) and is tested by running it alone after every verb at every level for the signed-in, anonymous and PUBLIC grantees, each of the service role's four revoked, the signed-in role's read revoked, a policy dropped and row security off. Firm separation rests on the firm policy each table already carries; the two restrictive policies narrow it and carry no firm clause of their own, as in 528 and 536. **WHAT IT DOES NOT CHANGE:** the two SECURITY DEFINER readers of these tables (`fx_dated_adjustments`, `party_opening_position`) ask who is calling themselves (firm, client, internal practice), so they are untouched; the signed-in roles keep nothing but SELECT, so the 18-table ceiling of the shrink-only list is now 15 and its `pending` kind is empty. Tests: `tests/test_542_the_fx_record_tables_and_the_portal_session_log_are_written_by_the_api_only_and_read_by_the_assigned_pg.py` (99 cases, real Postgres: the privileges at every level and from the worst case, the forgeries refused by the privilege as six different people, the policies, who sees what, the internal practice and a firm without one, the scope holding with the firm policy widened, a principal with no member row, the policies as the second lock with the writes granted back, the service role writing all three, the one-block shape of both files, the closing check run alone, the rollback bringing the forgery back, a re-apply ending it and a second apply harmless, the plan asking the caller's clients once). Mutation: 57 mutants of the migration and its rollback (the revokes narrowed or missing at each level for each grantee, the service role's grants cut, row security not enabled, each policy missing, permissive, narrowed to one command or written with the Partner branch dropped or with the row's client asked per row, the lock loop without its wait or its give-up, a statement outside the block, the closing check's reads of privileges, column privileges, TRUNCATE, row security and policies, and the rollback leaving a policy behind) were each run against the test file: 56 were caught at once, and the one survivor (a closing check that never read the kind of the two policies) got three spoilings (a policy made permissive, narrowed to one command, dropped) and is caught.

- **THE ITEM'S CACHED STOCK FIGURES ARE WRITTEN BY THE API ONLY, AN ITEM WITH STOCK HISTORY IS NOT DELETED FROM A SIGNED-IN SESSION, AND THREE TABLES NOTHING USES ARE CLOSED TO THE SIGNED-IN ROLE.** (POST-A-327 and POST-A-333, migration 543, 23-10-2026.) **WHAT WAS WRONG:** `service_catalogue.stock_qty_units`, `avg_cost_paise` and `stock_version` are a copy of the stock ledger's last row, and the signed-in role held UPDATE on the table with only the assignment scope between a member and the row (production read on 23-10-2026: `arwdDxtm`, every grant made by `postgres`, no trigger), so a member assigned to the client could set the copy to any figure over PostgREST; the stock register, the reorder check and the next movement's cost read the LEDGER, so the books were never affected, and what reads the copy is the catalogue's Stock column, the product form's "stock already started" gate, the item block of the item ledger and the reconciliation. The review of this change found the worse hole beside it: the same member could `DELETE` the item, and the stock ledger's foreign key is `ON DELETE CASCADE`, so the item's stock history went with it, with no reversal of the Inventory control journal that posted it (reproduced; the API route refuses to delete an item with a stock movement, the database did not). `activity_logs`, `ai_evidence` and `ai_signals` have no reader and no writer anywhere (the scanner finds a retention-position tuple for the first and nothing for the other two, apps/web never names them, production holds no row in any), yet the signed-in role held every write verb on all three (`arwdDxtm`, anon `Dxtm`), which migration 540 had guarded with a trigger because a trigger leaves the grants as they are. **WHAT 543 DOES:** (1) `refuse_a_signed_in_edit_of_the_items_cached_stock()`, `BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE OF` the three columns on `service_catalogue`, judging only a signed-in session (`current_setting('role')` `authenticated` or `anon`): an UPDATE that changes one of them is refused (a statement that names a column and sends the value the row holds is not a change, and one that names none never runs the trigger), and an INSERT carrying any figure other than the column default, a negative one included, is refused too (an item born with a forged copy is the same forgery; the upsert's insert candidate is judged as well); (2) `refuse_a_signed_in_delete_of_an_item_with_stock_history()`, `BEFORE DELETE`, refusing a signed-in delete of an item that has a stock ledger row unless the delete is made by a foreign-key action (one trigger level deeper than a statement; the firm purge runs with triggers off), which is the API's own rule made the database's; an item with no stock history is still deleted as before; (3) `REVOKE ALL` on the three unused tables from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC, the service role's four granted explicitly, migration 540's triggers left on them as a second lock (its test hands the privilege back in its throwaway copy and measures them there). Both functions are `SECURITY DEFINER` with a pinned search path, executable by nobody signed in, and the closing check compares each trigger's table, event, function, enabled state, `tgtype` and column list and reads the effective privileges of the three tables at every level. **THE WRITERS MOVED FIRST** (the change before this one: `_insert_and_cache`'s compare-and-set, its restore after a refused ledger insert and the opening-stock batch's upsert are on the privileged client and name the firm and the client), and this migration merges only after that has been on Render for an hour, because the old API against the trigger fails soft and wrong: a movement's claim is refused with 42501, which is not a version conflict, so it propagates before any ledger row or stock journal is written and the callers swallow stock failures by design, so the movement is LOST silently. **THE LOCK:** the triggers are made with `CREATE OR REPLACE TRIGGER` inside one `DO` block that first takes the table lock in `SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE` mode with `NOWAIT`, retried every half second for fifteen seconds and then failing in its own words (the shape of 541); the review reproduced that a `DROP TRIGGER` followed by `CREATE TRIGGER` on a re-apply takes `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`, queues behind every reader and can deadlock a transaction that reads and then writes, so the migration drops nothing (and 541 was changed to match). **WHAT THE INDEPENDENT REVIEW FOUND (on the first version, together with the Python change):** the lint ratchet red on both changes; the re-apply lock upgrade above; two statements stronger than the code (what reads the copy, and what happens when the order is wrong); two mutants of the Python change that survived; and a combined mutant of the migration that passed all its tests (an INSERT check of `> 0` that accepts a negative forged quantity, the service role's explicit grant removed, the search path unpinned) together with a purge test that never called the purge, each of which has a test now. **NOT DONE, ON PURPOSE:** an item with no stock history can still be deleted by a signed-in member although a document line may name it (the lines' foreign keys are `ON DELETE SET NULL`, so they keep their text and lose only the link; the API refuses that too, and it is the smaller harm); a privileged writer that sets the cache for an item that is not its client's is not stopped by the database (the three writers name the firm and the client, and a guard holds that). Tests: `tests/test_543_the_items_cached_stock_figures_are_written_by_the_api_only_and_three_tables_nothing_uses_are_closed_to_the_signed_in_role_pg.py` (99 cases, real Postgres: each column refused to a Manager and a Partner with the row left as it was, the privileged client and the owner free, an unchanged value and an edit of anything else passing, inserts and upserts with a forged, negative or fractional figure refused and with the defaults passing, the anonymous role judged in a worst case, both functions closed to a signed-in role and pinned, the delete of an item with history refused and of one without passing, a cascade not judged, the three tables holding nothing for the signed-in and anonymous roles at every level even from a database that granted everything, the service role's four granted explicitly, the firm purge called, the closing check run alone after each of fourteen spoilings, a busy table waited for without queueing and a re-apply not waiting for a reader, a table that stays busy failing the file after fifteen seconds, the one-block shape of both files, a second run changing nothing, and the rollback bringing both forgeries back with a re-apply ending them). Mutation: 58 mutants of the migration and its rollback (each function's role test, insert and update branches, delete rule, search path and revokes; each trigger missing, on the wrong event, column list or timing; the lock loop without `NOWAIT`, on the wrong table, giving up at once or never, with no wait, or preceded by a drop; each of the three tables left open, each revoke narrowed, the service role's grant cut; the closing check's trigger, column, table, column-privilege and service-role reads; the rollback leaving a trigger, a function or a table) were each run against the test file: 54 were caught by the first version of the tests, and the four survivors (the delete function left executable by a signed-in role, a lock loop that never gives up, and the closing check's comparison of a trigger's definition and of its column list) got tests and are caught.

- **A DOCUMENT HEADER'S SETTLEMENT AND STATUS COLUMNS ARE WRITTEN THROUGH ONE DOOR, AND THE LIST OF WRITERS STILL OUTSIDE IT ONLY SHRINKS.** (POST-A-331, first four slices, no migration, 24-10-2026.) **WHAT WAS WRONG:** the headers of an invoice, a bill and the four notes carry the columns that say what has been settled against a document (`paid_paise`, `credited_paise`, `debited_paise`, `debit_note_paise`, `credit_note_paise`), what state it is in (`status`) and which journal entry it posted, and about a dozen API paths write them as the CALLER, through the request's own client, so no database rule that asks who is writing can tell the API's settlement from a member's direct PostgREST call: a member holding the accounting tier can mark an invoice paid, cancel it or re-issue a cancelled one over PostgREST today (migration 541 refuses the other changes and leaves these columns writable on purpose). **THE ORDER IS THE ONE MIGRATIONS 535 AND 536 USED:** the writers move to the privileged client first, behind the checks their routes already make, and a later migration refuses a signed-in session's change of the columns (the old API against a refusing database fails soft and wrong). **THE DOOR:** `services/document_header_writer.update_header(db, table, doc_id, values, *, firm_id, client_id, actor, expect=, before=, reason=)` runs the UPDATE on the privileged client where there is one and on the caller's own where there is none (mock mode, a test double); the privileged client carries no row security, so the statement names the document, its firm AND its client itself, a missing one is a ValueError before anything is sent, and an unknown table is refused; `expect` becomes the compare-and-set filter; and it writes the audit row the database does not, because migration 111's capture trigger records a write only when `auth.uid()` is present and a privileged write has none (the row-level record of who changed a document's settled amount or status, from what, would otherwise vanish with the move). The audit row is written only when the statement changed a row (a compare-and-set that lost its race writes nothing), carries the signed-in person's auth id and email, the values the statement replaced and the values it wrote, and a reason when the write is a rollback; `log_event` is non-fatal by design, so a failed audit write never undoes a settlement that has happened. **WHAT MOVED:** `issue_credit_note`, `issue_debit_note`, `issue_purchase_credit_note` and `issue_sales_debit_note`: the compare-and-set on the invoice or bill the note settles against, its two rollbacks (the note's application could not be recorded; the note's journal could not be posted) and the note's own header update, which now names its client as well as its firm; and, in the second slice, `issue_invoice` (the move to issued with its issue time and journal link), `cancel_invoice`, `repost_journal` (the journal link), `receive_purchase_bill` (the move to received, its rollback to a draft when the bill's journal cannot be posted, which is the one place the code moves a document back to a draft, and the journal link) and `cancel_purchase_bill`; and, in the third slice, the money paths: the compare-and-set claim of a bill by a vendor payment, its rollback when the payment cannot be recorded and the reconciliation of the bill's paid amount (`routers/purchase_payments`), the foreign payment's settlement of its bill, the re-allocation of a receipt (`routers/receipts._adjust_invoice_paid`) and of a vendor payment, the settlement of an invoice by a foreign receipt and by the receipt fallback that a database without the atomic function takes, the settlement of bills by a domestic and a foreign payment across several bills, and the two rollbacks the reversal of a receipt and of a payment make (`services/reversal_service`); and, in the fourth slice, the three writers that build their table name in a variable because one body serves an invoice and a bill: the settlement a bank posting makes of its invoice or bill and the undo that gives it back (`services/bank_posting_service`, which now carries the Supabase auth id down the chain from `post`, from `undo` and from the approval of a draft, because the internal user id it also carries is a different kind of id and a job names no person) and the application of a party's credit (`services/party_credit_service.apply_credit`). Those three are the only calls allowed to hand the door a table that is not a literal, and the door refuses a table that is not one of the six. Every one of them is a compare-and-set on `paid_paise`, so the door's `expect` carries it; the helpers that other code calls (the claim, its rollback, the reconciliation, the two re-allocations, the two reversal rollbacks, `reverse_receipt` and `reverse_payment`) take the signed-in person by name, and a second static check holds that every caller outside the tests passes it. **THE GUARD** (`tests/test_the_document_header_writers_use_the_privileged_client_and_name_the_firm_and_client.py`, 59 cases, and one more in the post-dated cheque file) walks every module of the API and holds three exact sets, so a new writer on the caller's client is a red test and a writer that moved while staying listed is one too: the settlement-column UPDATEs of the six header tables that are still on the caller's client (none since the third slice), the UPDATEs whose payload the scan cannot read, each with the reason none is a settlement write (draft edits and the stamps a billing run or a recurring template puts on a draft it just made), and the writers that reach a header through a table the scan cannot read (a variable, a constant, a split chain), none now, which a second scan holds by finding every literal payload that names a money column written through such a table; it is also driven through the four real routes on the e2e double with a request client that REFUSES every write to the six tables the way the signed-in role will once the migration lands and a privileged client that records the statement it is handed (every write on the privileged client naming the document, firm and client, the compare-and-set filter on the column it moves, both audit rows with the actor and the before and after, the rollback of a failed posting and of an application that could not be recorded, each with its reason). **THE AUDIT ROW IS WEAKER THAN THE TRIGGER'S, AND SAYS SO** (the independent review of this change): the database trigger wrote the whole row, read from the database, in the same transaction as the update; the explicit row carries the columns written, `old_data` is what the WRITER read earlier (its belief, not the database's OLD: under a concurrent change to `paid_paise` or `status` the compare-and-set guards only the note column, so it can differ), and it is a second request after the write, so a crash or a network failure between the two leaves a settlement with no row (`log_event` logs the failure and never undoes the settlement). It adds up to two round trips to an issue. The entity is `sales_invoice` (the name the invoice routes' own events use), where the trigger recorded `client_sales_invoice`; nothing reads the old name. Where `USE_USER_JWT` is off the trigger recorded nothing and the rows are an addition. **THE INDEPENDENT REVIEW OF THE THIRD SLICE** found no behaviour regression and five things to close: the post-dated cheque conversion's docstring and its router comment said every write the engine makes stays on the request's client, which the settled amount of the bill an issued cheque pays no longer does (reworded, and a test holds that the bill's paid amount is written by the privileged client with an audit row); the audit assertions checked only that the replaced paid amount was present, so a wrong replaced value passed on five of the twelve calls (they compare the replaced paid amount, status and foreign amount with what the row held, and the reason texts exactly); the exact-list scan could not see a writer whose table is not a literal (a second scan finds every literal payload naming a money column written through a table it cannot read, and it must equal the three writers named by hand); the actor check accepted `actor={}` (it must be a name now); and two call sites no test reached (a payment that settled several bills, reversed). **TWO OLDER TESTS ASSUMED THE CALLER'S CLIENT WROTE THE SETTLEMENT AND WERE ADJUSTED:** the receipt re-allocation test seeded an invoice and a receipt with no client, which a settlement now refuses (every real row has one), and the compare-and-set retry test wraps the client the receipt engine was handed to simulate a concurrent writer, so it now wraps the privileged client the settlement is written with too, or the simulation never reached the write it is about. **NOT DONE, ON PURPOSE:** the opening documents (the INSERT of an issued document with a fixed shape and its soft delete, which migration 541 already confines) and the receipt and journal functions the database runs as their owner (`settle_receipt_atomic` writes an invoice's paid amount and status inside the database as the caller's role, which a trigger on the columns would judge as a direct write; moving it is the ledger-door item, POST-A-317, which also has to carry the actor across), the allocation tables the three purchase-side and sales-debit routes still write with the caller's client (a separate item), and the trigger that takes the signed-in role's write away, which waits until the list is empty. The collections flags (`is_overdue`, `days_overdue`, `aging_bucket`, the reminder and follow-up counters) and `landed_cost_basis` are written on the caller's client on purpose: they are soft columns that migration 541 lets a signed-in session change, the closing trigger will not judge them, and the guard treats an UPDATE whose literal columns are all soft as no settlement write at all. Negative control: against the four note routes as they were, 16 of the first slice's 31 cases fail and the other 15 pass; against the invoice and bill routes as they were, 10 of the 37 fail (the four static cases and the six that drive the routes) and the other 27 pass; against the receipt, payment and reversal sources as they were, 14 of the 47 cases then in the file fail and the other 33 pass; against the bank and party-credit sources as they were, 9 of the 57 fail and the other 48 pass. Mutation: 100 mutants of the door and of the four routes (each call's actor, replaced values and reason dropped; the guard filter dropped; a rollback restoring the new status; the forward write carrying a wrong status; a rollback recording the wrong status as replaced; the note header recording no applied amount or no journal link; the first call naming another client; the door writing on the caller's client, a table's chain lost from the dispatch, not naming the client, the firm or the document of each of the six chains, ignoring the guard, auditing when nothing changed, under the wrong entity or without the replaced values, the written values, the actor id, the actor email or the reason; the door accepting an unknown table or a missing document, firm or client; a table dropped from the list) were each run against the file: all 100 were caught; the second slice's 37 mutants (each of its eight calls with the actor, the replaced values or the reason dropped, the client or the firm another, a wrong status or no journal link written) were all caught as well; the third slice's 104 mutants (each of its twelve calls with the actor, the guard, the replaced paid amount, status or foreign amount dropped or wrong, the client or the firm another, a reason text swapped, and each caller that passes the signed-in person to a helper no longer passing it) are all caught; the first 73 of them left twelve survivors that showed four paths with no test (the receipt fallback, a domestic payment across several bills, the reconciliation of a bill's paid amount, and the actor on the claim's rollback), which got tests, and the review's mutants of the replaced values and of the reason texts were added and caught; the fourth slice's 30 mutants (its three calls with the actor, the replaced values, the client or the firm dropped or wrong, and each hand-over of the auth id along the chain from `post`, from `undo` and from the approval of a draft down to the door) were all caught, two of them after tests were added for the bill's branch and for the approval of a legacy draft; and the firm-scope reader's budget of places it cannot read falls from 40 to 37, because those three writers named their table in a variable and now go through a door whose statements are literal. (The review found one class the first 65 did not contain, a rollback that restores the wrong status, which the first 30 cases passed; the cases now build a note that increases what is owed on a document that is already paid in full, so that the move to partly paid and the way back to paid are both seen.)

- **THE SETTLEMENT, STATUS AND JOURNAL LINK OF A DOCUMENT ARE WRITTEN BY THE API ONLY, AN UPDATE IS JUDGED BY `current_user` AND AN INSERT BY THE `role` SETTING, AND THE DATABASE'S OWN CASCADE NEEDS NO EXEMPTION.** (POST-A-331, second step, migration 544, 24-10-2026.) **WHAT WAS WRONG:** migration 541 froze the particulars of an issued invoice, bill or note and left the columns that say what has been settled against it (`paid_paise`, `paid_txn`, `credited_paise`, `debited_paise`, `debit_note_paise`, `credit_note_paise`, `applied_paise`), what state it is in (`status`, `issued_at`, `received_at`, `cancelled_at`) and which journal entry it posted writable by a member who holds the tier, because about thirty API paths wrote them as the caller. A member could mark an invoice paid, set its paid amount to anything, cancel an invoice, issue a draft with none of the API's issue checks, or point a document at another journal entry, over PostgREST and while the API refuses all of it. The first step (the writers moved to the privileged client through `services/document_header_writer.update_header`, which names the document, its firm and its client and writes the audit row the capture trigger skips for a write with no `auth.uid()`) merged and ran for an hour first; this migration is the second half. **WHAT IT DOES:** one trigger function, `refuse_a_signed_in_change_of_a_documents_settlement()`, and one trigger per table (`a_documents_settlement_is_written_by_the_api_only`, BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE OF the table's own settlement columns, tgtype 23). An UPDATE that changes one of the columns is refused with 42501 and a sentence naming exactly the columns changed, on a draft as well as on an issued document (a draft is the one place migration 541 does not look, and issuing it is a status change); a statement that sends the value the row holds is not a change; an INSERT carrying any of them other than at its default (zero or NULL, a negative figure included) is refused too, `status` excepted because 541 already judges it. The soft columns migration 541 allows (notes, references, due date, the collections counters, `landed_cost_basis`) stay writable, and a test reads 541's allowed list from the catalogue so that a column added to it later has to be decided here. **WHY AN UPDATE IS JUDGED BY `current_user` AND AN INSERT BY THE `role` SETTING:** the API's plain rupee receipt calls `settle_receipt_atomic`, which writes an invoice's paid amount and status inside the database. It is SECURITY DEFINER (migration 217) and runs as its owner, so inside it `current_user` is the owner and `current_setting('role')` is still `authenticated`: a trigger judging the setting would refuse every ordinary receipt, and one judging `current_user` passes it and refuses a direct PATCH. The function is therefore SECURITY INVOKER (a definer would see its owner on every call and pass every statement; the closing check reads that, and a test shows the difference in two functions that are not the product's). An INSERT is the other way round, and the independent review of this migration found why: `numbered_document_atomic` is SECURITY DEFINER too and inserts the four note tables from whatever header it is handed, so a `current_user` test let a Manager create a note already carrying a paid amount or a journal link through the RPC. No function a signed-in session legitimately calls inserts a document with a settlement on it, so an INSERT is judged by the setting, which stays the caller's inside a definer function. **NO EXEMPTION BY TRIGGER DEPTH:** 541 exempts a write made one trigger level down (a foreign-key action) because it judges the setting. Here a foreign-key action runs as the owner of the table it updates, so it passes without a rule, and a depth exemption would only have excused an invoker trigger on another table that wrote a settlement as the caller; the migration has none, a test makes one and sees it refused, and another reads every function source (a name scan and the dynamic-SQL scan, because `numbered_document_atomic__unguarded` names its table in a variable) so that a writer cannot appear unseen. **A RENAME FAILS CLOSED:** a watched name that is not a column of the row makes the trigger refuse the write in its own words, because a renamed column would otherwise read as NULL on both sides of an UPDATE and stop being judged without a sound while the `UPDATE OF` list, stored by column number, kept firing. **THE ORDER:** the writers first (the old API against this trigger fails soft and wrong: a receipt's settlement is refused with 42501, which its callers do not treat as a version conflict), so this migration merges only after the first step has been on Render for at least an hour, the order of 535 and 536. **NOT DONE, ON PURPOSE:** `settle_receipt_atomic` and `post_journal_atomic` still run as their owner and still take what they are handed from a signed-in caller (a member holding the tier can call the first over PostgREST with an invoice id of the firm), which is the ledger-door item (POST-A-317) and also has to carry the actor across; a DRAFT's particulars and flags stay editable over PostgREST and the API's issue posts from the stored row, so a draft flagged `is_opening` and then issued is left out of the returns it should be in (POST-A-335); the rows a settlement is derived from (receipts and their allocations, vendor payments and theirs) are written by the caller's own client (POST-A-336). **HOW IT IS HELD:** `tests/test_544_…_pg.py` (436 cases on a real database, as each person) pins the list from four sides (the trigger's arguments and its `UPDATE OF` list from the catalogue, the Python guard's own `SETTLEMENT` set, and 541's allowed list from the catalogue), refuses every column on a draft and on a document that is not for a Manager and a Partner, reads back the writes the trigger lets through, takes the real `settle_receipt_atomic` and the real `numbered_document_atomic` as a Manager, judges the anonymous role in a worst case on every column, and runs the closing check alone after 96 spoilings across all six tables. The lock tests read the runner's own locks and the busy-table holder is found in `pg_locks` in its own database only: clones of one template share relation numbers, so another file's holder on the same table (541's) could satisfy the wait. The 541 test switches the later trigger off in the copy its cases run on, because several of its cases change the settlement columns as a Manager to show what 541 alone leaves open, and the row-security matrix's insert probe copies a seeded row with its journal stamps emptied for the same reason. **THE SEVEN ROWS A SETTLEMENT IS DERIVED FROM ARE CLOSED TOO (migration 559, POST-A-336, 28-10-2026).** `receipts`, `receipt_allocations`, `purchase_payments`, `purchase_payment_allocations`, `debit_note_allocations`, `sales_debit_note_allocations` and `purchase_credit_note_allocations` were written with the caller's own session while production still granted `authenticated` INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on all seven (`credit_note_allocations` was closed by 513), so a member who holds the accounting tier could move an allocation or date a payment inside the fifteen days over PostgREST and change §43B(h)'s answer although the bill's header could not be touched. The writers of the five routers and three services involved (`receipts`, `purchase_payments`, `debit_notes`, `sales_debit_notes`, `purchase_credit_notes`, `receipt_service`, `purchase_payment_service`, `reversal_service`) write through `core.db_provider.privileged_for`, after the route's own checks and naming the firm and the client on every statement; the migration then revokes the three verbs and TRUNCATE from `authenticated`, `anon` and PUBLIC and checks the effective privilege. The browser never wrote any of them (the two allocation modals save through the API), the receipt's settlement is made inside `settle_receipt_atomic` (SECURITY DEFINER) and is unaffected, and the frozen list of closed tables grew from 157 to 164. `tests/test_558_…_pg.py` (56 cases, real Postgres) proves the privileges, the refusal of each verb on each table as an assigned Manager (a statement that matches no row, so a refusal is only the privilege), the same statements passing as the service role, the worst case (grants to PUBLIC, anon, authenticated and a column), the closing check failing on a grant the owner cannot take back, idempotence and the rollback as the negative control; the header-writers test refuses all seven on the request's own client. **Still open:** the six document headers and their lines (POST-A-335 part 2).

- **A ROUTE THAT ASSERTS THE CLIENT THE CALLER NAMED ACTS ONLY ON A ROW OF THAT CLIENT, A MISSING ROW IS NEVER A REASON TO SKIP THE SCOPE CHECK, AND A FIRM-WIDE BATCH IS REFUSED TO A CALLER WHO IS NOT FIRM-WIDE BY EVERY DOOR THAT STARTS IT.** (24-10-2026, found by mapping every route that reaches the ledger for POST-A-317; Python only, no migration.) **WHAT WAS WRONG:** the plan to take the direct database door to the ledger away makes the route's own check the only one, so three read-only readers (sales and purchase, banking and payroll and assets, manual journals and imports and jobs) listed every route that posts and what it proves about the client before it posts. None of the posting routes was open, and the search for the shape they exposed, which an independent review of the first version then widened, found thirty-one routes that were not safe. **(1) Twenty-eight routes** take the client from the request (a body field, a query parameter or the path), call `assert_client_access` on the CLIENT THE CALLER NAMED, and then read or write a row by firm and id alone through the privileged client, which carries no row security: eleven payroll employee handlers (the leaver settlement preview and record, the declaration upsert and verify, perquisites, salary revisions and loans, the bonus disqualification), the payroll remittance link and retract, the Bill of Entry PATCH, POST .../post and DELETE, six sales-cycle routes (quotation lines and PATCH, order position and PATCH, challan read and PATCH) and six purchase-cycle routes (order lines, position and PATCH, receipt lines and PATCH, bill match). Access to client A says nothing about client B's row, so a member assigned to A alone could name A beside B's id and read B's employee's leaver settlement, revisions and loans, record B's employee's settlement (a journal in A's books out of B's salary data, B's employee marked resigned), amend B's declaration, perquisites and bonus disqualification, POST B's Bill of Entry (a journal in B's books), amend B's challan (stopping its s.143 clock) and goods receipt (moving the MSMED s.43B(h) fifteen days), and link or retract B's statutory remittance. **(2) Three guards could be skipped by a missing row:** the journal reverse route guarded with `if orig and not can_access_client(...)`, which passes when the read finds nothing and held only because the kernel's own read found nothing too; the bank account PATCH read `or [{}]` and asserted access to the client of an empty row (None, which passes), then answered success for an account that was not there; and `POST /api/scheduler/run` asked `team:write` alone where its sibling route refuses a caller who is not firm-wide, although the batch it starts runs privileged across every client of the firm and a per-person override can give `team:write` to anyone. **WHAT IT DOES:** each shape has one tie, in the words a missing row gets. Payroll: `routers/payroll._employee_of_client(db, current_user, employee_id, client_id)` reads by firm AND client AND id (404 "Employee not found" for a missing employee and for one of another client alike) and `verify_declaration` ties the declaration in its own read. Bill of Entry: `bill_of_entry_service.get` and `post` take `client_id` as a required keyword (no default, filtered on) and the statements that write a Bill name the client too. Sales and purchase cycle: `row_of_client`, `order_of_client`, `receipt_of_client` and `bill_of_client` read the document and compare its client with the named one before the service is called, comparing the two client ids without regard to case (an extra read on routes that are not on a hot path, so no service signature and none of their forty test calls changed). Remittances: `statutory_remittance_service.link_payment` and `retract` take `client_id` as a required keyword and filter on it, and a payment can be linked only to a live journal entry of that client (422 otherwise, checked before anything is written); `retract` also wrote a bare DATE into the `timestamptz` column `deleted_at` (the test double's production-type check found it) and now writes the instant. The reverse route answers 404 when the original is missing, the bank PATCH answers 404 "Bank account not found." and words the scope check the same, and `core.authz.assert_firmwide_job` is the one guard both manual batch routes call (the tasks router's helper keeps its name and delegates). **HOW IT IS HELD:** `tests/test_a_payroll_handler_acts_on_an_employee_of_the_client_it_named.py` drives each of the eleven with a FakeDB holding a second client's employee and asserts the 404 in the words a missing employee gets and a snapshot of every table unchanged, with positive controls for the client's own employee and a rule over the router's own source (every handler that names an employee CALLS a helper that establishes whose it is, or is on a list of twelve with the reason it needs none, counted by AST so a comment does not satisfy it; the exemptions are checked for staleness and the rule for vacuity); `tests/test_a_cycle_document_or_remittance_is_reached_only_through_its_own_client.py` does the same for the fourteen cycle and remittance routes and holds the same rule over the two cycle routers; `tests/test_a_route_that_asserts_the_client_it_was_given_acts_only_on_a_row_of_that_client.py` covers the Bill of Entry routes (the post negative reaches the kernel without the tie, so the journal assertion is what catches it), the reverse route, the bank PATCH and the scheduler route and holds that the service's `client_id` has no default. Each route also has a wiring test (a spy that records what the route hands its helper, because a helper handed the wrong client ties nothing), and every one of 78 mutants made in turn in a throwaway copy of the tree (a helper call removed, handed the wrong client, or comparing existence only; a comparison dropped or made case-sensitive; a statement no longer filtered by client; a service default restored; the retract written as a bare date; a deleted entry accepted) fails a test: 77 of them these tests, and the last the existing wording rule, which pins the words the bank PATCH's scope check carries. **NOT DONE, ON PURPOSE:** a tree-wide guard for the shape (an AST scan of every router flagged thirty-six handlers and could not see a read inside a service function, which is where the fourteen the review added were; a precise rule needs the schema's list of client-owned tables and has to follow a call into a service, POST-A-337, which also lists the lower-tier handlers that read with the request's own client and so still have row security as a second lock); the rows a document NAMES in its body (a Bill of Entry's vendor and purchase bill, a challan's or receipt's order, a fixed asset's bill), which nothing ties to the document's client (POST-A-338). The bank's trusted-rule sweep, which was on this list because it posted as the person who trusted the rule without asking at run time whether they still reach the client, asks since POST-A-339 (see the banking record).
 **FOLLOW-UP, 28-10-2026 (POST-A-337; Python only, no migration).** The sixteen handlers the review reported and left are tied, and the tree-wide guard now exists. **Real, and fixed:** the eight soft deletes of the GSTR-4 annual entries (four), the GSTR-9C lines (two) and the e-commerce operator's supplies (two) withdrew a row by firm and id after asserting the client the request SENT, so a member assigned to client A could withdraw client B's entry by naming both: each service takes the client as a required argument and filters on it (404 "Entry not found."); the two GSTR-9C line creators attached a line to whatever reconciliation id the body named, and B's statement, which lists a reconciliation's lines by its id, would have carried A's line, so a line is created only under a reconciliation of its own client; the AIS working (`save_working`) rewrote any client's line (`client_id` is a required keyword now; "Record not found"); the three reverse-charge document routes read the bill, payment or both by id and then built the document for the ROW's client (`issue` took `client_id = parent["client_id"]`), so a document could be issued for a sibling's bill; and `POST /api/einvoice/records` stored a record against any client's invoice and its 409 sentence named another client's live record, because `_invoice_treatment` deliberately answers None for an invoice it cannot find under the client. **THE RULE:** `tests/test_a_route_that_asserts_a_client_does_not_write_a_row_by_the_firm_alone.py` takes every `@router` handler that calls `assert_client_access*`, follows a call into `services/`, `domain/` and `repositories/` by function name three calls deep, and flags a WRITE (`update`, `delete`, `upsert`) on a table that carries `client_id` (the production snapshot plus any table a migration creates with the column) filtered on `id` and `firm_id` with no client filter, unless the function compares a row's own `client_id` or asserts access on a client taken from a row. Run against the code of 27-10-2026 it fires on exactly the eight deletes; the twenty-eight handlers it still fires on are each read and listed with the reason the row is already tied (the update of a row a client-scoped read just returned, a rollback of a row the request created, a `row_of_client` or `_employee_of_client` call first), and the list can only shrink: a stale entry fails. Behaviour is held by `tests/test_a_route_that_names_a_client_acts_only_on_a_row_of_that_client.py` (22 tests: each route with the sibling's row named under the wrong client is refused in the words a missing row gets and the row is left as it was, and the same request under the row's own client works, so the refusal is the client and nothing else). 17 of 18 hand-made faults (each fix removed in turn, and the rule's service-following removed) are caught; the survivor, `issue` taking the client from the row again, is an equivalent change because the bill and payment lookups already compare the row's client with the named one. **NOT DONE:** a READ by id with the firm alone (information, not tampering) is not asked by the rule; the reads the scan found (`ais_service._get_record` for the compliance roll-ups, the GST return builders' customer and vendor lookups, the cycle `_one_order` family) were not judged one by one, and the rule does not see a chain built across several statements or a write through a variable table name. Ledger POST-A-337 is closed and the reads are POST-A-352. **THE READS ARE ASKED THE SAME QUESTION, ALONG THE PATH AND NOT AT THE HANDLER ALONE (POST-A-352, 28-10-2026).** `tests/test_a_route_that_asserts_a_client_does_not_read_a_row_by_the_firm_alone.py` is the write rule's twin for `select`: a handler that asserts the client the caller named must not reach a read of a client-owned table by `id` and `firm_id` with no client filter, unless a step on the way to the read ties the row to the client. A tie is a compare of the row's own `client_id`, an assertion on a client taken from a row, a call to a helper whose name says it ties (`..._of_client`, `..._of_the_client`, `..._is_the_clients`, `..._are_the_clients`) or a `client_id` filter on the read itself, and it counts ANYWHERE on the path from the handler to the read; the write rule asked only the handler, so its first read-run flagged 29 handlers and the path-sensitive one 20.
