One was a real gap and is fixed: `itc_register_service.record_reclaim` read the reversal a reclaim releases by id and firm, so a reclaim could be registered against another client's reversal and counted against that client's credit; `outstanding_for` takes the client now (no default) and answers `Reversal not found.` for another client's row. The other nineteen read an id that was TAKEN FROM A ROW OF THIS CLIENT (a customer or vendor on its own invoice or bill, a parent invoice on its own note, an employee on its own payroll row, the vendor on its own cheque) and are on a reviewed list with the reason, which only shrinks. What it does not see: a read whose table is named by a variable, a chain built across several statements, and a tie made by a helper whose name does not end in one of the four suffixes.
- **A POSTING THE SERVER MAKES FOR A PERSON IS STILL AUDITED AS THAT PERSON: THE TWO LEDGER FUNCTIONS CARRY THE PERSON'S AUTH ID FOR THE LENGTH OF THEIR OWN TRANSACTION, BOTH AUDIT TRIGGER FUNCTIONS READ IT ONLY WHEN NOBODY IS SIGNED IN, AND A ROW WRITTEN FOR A CARRIED PERSON SAYS SO.** (POST-A-317 step 1, migration 545, 24-10-2026.) **WHAT WAS WRONG:** the ledger-door plan (POST-A-317) makes the API call `post_journal_atomic` and `settle_receipt_atomic` with its privileged client and then takes EXECUTE away from signed-in users. The privileged client has no `auth.uid()`, and the row-level edit log (Companies (Accounts) Rules 2014 Rule 3(1)) is written by triggers that record a write only when `auth.uid()` is present: production held 14,479 journal-entry creates written by the trigger against 15 written by the application on 23-10-2026, so the move would have emptied the edit log of every posting. **TWO THINGS THE FIRST DESIGN MISSED, FOUND BY AN INDEPENDENT REVIEW BEFORE ANYTHING SHIPPED:** the lines of an entry are audited by a different trigger function (`audit_capture_journal_line`, migration 266), which has its own `auth.uid()` test, so carrying the person into `audit_capture` alone left every posting with an entry row and no line rows (reproduced: one `journal_entry` and two `journal_line` rows for a signed-in Manager, one and none for the service role); and the person was to come from `created_by`, which most of the `journal_for_*` helpers do not pass, so most postings would have carried nobody. **WHAT 545 DOES:** `carry_audit_actor(audit_actor, created_by, firm)` (SECURITY INVOKER, closed to every signed-in role, called only from inside the two wrappers) does nothing when `auth.uid()` is set; otherwise it first clears the transaction-local setting `app.audit_actor` (a second posting in one transaction never inherits the first one's person), then resolves the person: `audit_actor` is an AUTH id, which the API reads off the request's own bearer token, and it must name a member of the entry's own firm (anything else is no actor and is not replaced by `created_by`, which would hide a wrong value); when it is absent a job's person is found from the internal user id in `created_by`, again only inside the entry's own firm. `carried_audit_actor()` is the one reader of the setting (a value that is not a uuid is no actor). `audit_capture` (migration 336's body) and `audit_capture_journal_line` (migration 266's) each gain one block: when `auth.uid()` is NULL they read the carried actor, and the row's metadata says `actor_source: carried`, because the person did not make the write with their own token and a reader of the log should be able to tell. The two wrappers (migration 533's bodies) gain one call after the caller check and remove the reserved payload key `audit_actor` before the unguarded body, which builds its INSERT from the payload's keys and would refuse it. Each function is the whole definition restated from the migration that last defined it, derived by script (`gen545`), and the test reconstructs the ancestor by scanning the migration directory and asserts the new body is the ancestor plus exactly the added lines. **WHAT IT DOES NOT DO:** it does not change who may call either function, the kernel, or any table; a signed-in session's actor is never replaced (the setting is read only when `auth.uid()` is NULL, whatever the session sets); a job that already posts with the service role and names a person in `created_by` is now audited as that person; the audit of a request with no token and no `created_by` (a job) is nobody, which is what it always was. **HOW IT IS HELD:** `tests/test_545_the_ledger_functions_carry_the_posting_persons_audit_actor_for_a_privileged_caller_pg.py` (real Postgres, as each person: a privileged journal and receipt audited as the named person with the line rows, `audit_actor` winning over `created_by` and a wrong one not replaced by it, every (entity, action) a signed-in posting audits also audited for a privileged one, a signed-in session never replaced however it sets the key or the setting, the label, the setting gone with the transaction and not inherited inside it, garbage as no actor, the shape of all four functions against their ancestors, the closing check run alone after each spoiling, the rollback and a re-apply). Mutation: 62 mutants of the migration and its rollback (the carrier's skip for a signed-in caller, its clearing and setting scope, the firm and the lookup for each of the two ways a person is named, which of them wins, the unreadable-value handlers, the shape and grants of the carrier and the reader; each audit trigger function's read, label and precedence; each wrapper's carry call, key, firm and the removal of the reserved key; every read of the closing check; each statement of the rollback): 53 caught by the tests, 6 refused by the migration's own closing check when applied (reported apart), and 3 equivalent (the clearing made session-wide leaves an empty value that reads as no actor; a null firm matches no member either way; the carry made before the caller check cannot be seen because a refused caller aborts the transaction). The first run left nine alive, six of which were real gaps that got tests: a signed-in posting that names someone must leave no carried actor behind, the carrier's and the reader's search path is pinned, both stay closed where the platform grants every new function to the signed-in by default (this test database has no such default, so the migration's revoke had never been seen to matter), and the closing check's clause for the carrier call had been hidden behind the clause for the key removal.

- **THE POSTING KERNEL AND THE PLAIN RECEIPT CALL THE TWO LEDGER FUNCTIONS THROUGH THE PRIVILEGED CLIENT, FROM ONE PLACE, CARRYING THE PERSON WHO IS POSTING, AHEAD OF THE MIGRATION THAT TAKES THE SIGNED-IN GRANT AWAY.** (POST-A-317 step 2, no migration, 24-10-2026.) **WHAT WAS WRONG:** `_create_journal` called `post_journal_atomic` and `receipt_service._settle_receipt_via_atomic_rpc` called `settle_receipt_atomic` with the REQUEST's own client, so the database function was reachable over PostgREST by any signed-in member who held one posting permission, and the route's own checks were the only thing that had been asked of the API's own callers and not of that member. **WHAT IT DOES:** `services/ledger_door.ledger_rpc(db, fn, params, *, firm_id, client_id)` is the one place either function is called from (its two `.rpc("<name>")` calls are the only literals of the names that call a client): the privileged client where a database is configured (the caller's own client otherwise, so the in-memory doubles are unchanged), firm and client required keyword arguments with no default and an empty one refused before the privileged client is touched (`can_access_client` answers True for no client), only the two ledger names accepted, and the payload must name the same firm and client the door was told (the scope a route proved is the scope that is posted; the door does not change the dictionaries it is handed). The reserved key is the DOOR'S: any `audit_actor` a payload carried is removed whether or not a person is bound, and only the verified person is put back, so a payload can never name its own actor (the independent review found the door added the key only when a person was bound; no caller built an entry from a user-supplied dictionary, so there was no vector, and it is closed). The two call sites are one `ledger_rpc(...)` each. **WHO POSTED:** the audit triggers record a write only when `auth.uid()` is set, which the privileged client never has, so the person travels in the payload as the reserved key `audit_actor`: the auth id of the person the request is, as `core.auth.get_current_user` verified it and recorded in `core/request_actor.py` (a small mutable holder begun by the request middleware, shared with the worker thread's copy of the context, the same shape as the firm in `core/request_context`; a job has no request and so no holder and sends none). It is not read off the token again: a first version decoded the token's claim without verifying it, and the rule that holds there is exactly one token decode in the API (`tests/test_a_token_must_be_issued_for_this_product.py`) failed it, rightly; and it does not ride on `created_by`, because most of the `journal_for_*` helpers pass none (the independent review found that, and that the line rows were not audited either; both are in migration 545). The label is not a credential: the database checks the person is a member of the entry's own firm before it believes it. **ORDER:** shipped after migration 545 (the functions must be able to carry the person before anything depends on it; against a database without 545 every posting from a request with a verified person fails, loudly, because the unguarded body builds its column list from the entry's keys) and at least an hour before migration 546, because the previous API against a revoked grant fails soft and wrong, with every posting refused 42501. **WHAT 546 STILL HAS TO ADD, AND THE WINDOW UNTIL IT DOES:** the signed-in caller's `assert_caller_acts_for` asks that the client in the payload belongs to the firm in the payload; it does not apply to the service role, so from this deploy until 546 the database no longer asks that of an API posting (this module asks that the payload names the firm and client the route proved, and the route's own checks are the lock), and 546 is to ask it of a privileged caller as well as revoke EXECUTE from `authenticated` and `anon`. **A MANUAL RUN OF THE DAILY BATCH** (`POST /api/scheduler/run`) happens inside the request of the person who pressed it, so its postings are audited as that person (`actor_source` `carried`); the nightly run has no request and is audited as the person in `created_by`, or nobody; migration 546 is not merged until a production posting has been seen to go through this door (an audit row with `actor_source` `carried`) or the owner has confirmed the deploy. **HOW A DEPLOYMENT IS SHOWN TO BE RUNNING IT:** `/health` carries `ledger_door`, `server` once a database is configured and the privileged client does the calling, `caller` otherwise (a fact about the process's configuration, no network), because production had no posting for the audit log to show the door by (the last journal entry was written on 29-09-2026) and migration 546 must not be merged on an assumption that Render has deployed. **HOW IT IS HELD:** `tests/test_the_two_ledger_functions_are_called_through_the_privileged_client_from_one_place.py` (45 cases: a request client that refuses either function and a privileged client that records the call, so a journal and a plain receipt post through the privileged client with the scope in the payload; the verified person rides on a posting that names no `created_by`; nobody, a blank id or a non-text id adds nothing; no privileged client leaves the payload exactly as built; a payload naming another scope is refused for both functions; the holder is shared with a copied context; `get_current_user` records the person it verified; the REAL application, with throwaway routes behind the real middleware and the real authentication dependency, shows a sync and an async handler the verified person and a request with no token nobody (the textual check that the middleware begins and ends the holder stays beside it, and no longer stands alone: ending the holder before the route runs passed it and every other test of the file); a payload's own `audit_actor` is removed with and without a person bound; the in-memory ledger functions remove the reserved key as the real wrappers do; the rule over the API's own source: the only `.rpc("<ledger function>")` literals are the door's two, a string equal to either name is code only in the door and the two call sites, and each call site names its firm and its client), the closed list of ledger writers in `tests/test_the_hero_figures_are_counted_facts.py` (it now names the door where it named the two services, and says why), and a change to `tests/test_concurrency_idempotency.py`, whose privileged client now has the database functions the real one has (the test double had none, which is how the compare-and-set simulation reached the ledger call). Mutation: 41 mutants of the door, the holder, the two call sites, the authentication dependency, the middleware, the in-memory mirrors and the health word (the function picked, the scope compared or not, the person added, to which payload and under which key, a caller-supplied actor kept or kept when nobody is bound, the caller's dictionaries written to, a blank or non-text id recorded, a fresh holder per read, the dependency recording nothing or the wrong value, the middleware never beginning the holder or ending it before the route runs, each call site going straight to the caller's client, swapping firm and client, naming no client or calling the other function, a mirror keeping the reserved key): all 41 caught. An earlier run over the first design (22 mutants) left two alive: the caller's dictionaries written to in place (it got a test) and firm and client swapped at the kernel's call (the door then used its arguments only to refuse an empty one, so it now compares them with the payload and the swap is refused); the independent review of 24-10-2026 then found that the middleware's order was held by source text only (ending the holder before the route runs passed every test, now a behavioural test), that the door added the key only when a person was bound, and that the in-memory ledger functions kept the key the real ones remove, and all three are closed. The whole mock-mode suite on this tree: 27,091 passed, 5,370 skipped, 3 failed (the same three container-only failures as on main).

- **THE TWO LEDGER FUNCTIONS ARE THE SERVER'S ALONE, AND A PRIVILEGED CALL MUST NAME A CLIENT OF ITS FIRM.** (POST-A-317 last step, migration 546, 24-10-2026.) **WHAT WAS WRONG:** `post_journal_atomic` (the posting kernel) and `settle_receipt_atomic` (a plain rupee customer receipt) were SECURITY DEFINER and executable by every signed-in user, admitted by a union of the role pairs the posting routes ask (migration 533), so a member who held ANY ONE of them (banking:write at Executive is enough for either) could post, over PostgREST with their own token and for any client they reach, a balanced entry with any content: any `source_type`, `status`, `created_by`, `posted_by` or narration, in an open year, with none of the questions the application's routes ask before they post. Migration 533 closed the closed-year hole in both; this closes the door they were reached through. And for a caller with no `auth.uid()` (the service role) `assert_caller_acts_for` returned at once and the unguarded body asks the client's firm only for a signed-in caller, so once the API posts through its privileged client nothing in the database asked whether the client in the payload belongs to the firm in the payload. **WHAT 546 DOES:** EXECUTE on both wrappers is revoked from PUBLIC, anon and authenticated and kept for service_role (the two unguarded bodies were never executable by a signed-in user); both wrappers keep every check they have for a signed-in caller (so a grant restored by hand meets the same checks as before, no fewer) and gain one for a caller with no `auth.uid()`: the client in the payload must be a client of the firm in the payload, and a NULL firm or client names no such client (SQLSTATE 42501, `post_journal_atomic: client % is not a client of firm %`). Each wrapper is migration 545's definition plus exactly that block, derived by script; the closing check reads the grants and that each wrapper still asks the client, asks who is calling, carries the audit actor and removes the reserved key, and is run alone after each spoiling in the test. **ORDER:** the API has called both functions through its privileged client since the door change, and migration 545 lets them carry the person; this migration must not apply before that API is the one serving, because the previous API calls them with the caller's own token and every posting would be refused 42501. It is merged behind a gate: production's `/health` must answer `ledger_door: server` (it did, read on 24-10-2026 at 11:37 UTC, ten minutes after the door merged) and an hour must have passed since the door's deploy. No production posting exists to show the door by an audit row (the last journal entry is of 29-09-2026), so the `/health` word is the proof. **WHAT IT DOES NOT DO:** it does not touch `edit_posted_journal` (the manual journal's edit, which asks who is calling since migration 486 and is limited to a manual journal in an open period), `numbered_document_atomic` or any table, and it does not change what the routes check before they post. One check moves from the database to the application for a posting: that only a Partner may act on the firm's internal practice, which `assert_caller_acts_for` asked of a signed-in caller. It now rests on the routes and on there being no non-Partner assigned to the internal practice (none in production on 24-10-2026, and the database refuses a signed-in session creating one). **HOW IT IS HELD:** `tests/test_546_the_two_ledger_functions_are_executable_by_the_server_alone_and_its_call_must_name_a_client_of_its_firm_pg.py` (real Postgres, as each role: a Partner, an Executive with banking:write, another firm's Partner and anon refused by the privilege and nothing written; the server still posting a journal and a receipt; a client of another firm, one that does not exist, no client, no firm and another firm's id with this firm's client refused by both functions for the service role and a connection with no JWT, with nothing written and SQLSTATE 42501; with the grant handed back inside the throwaway database the signed-in checks are migration 533's; the shape against 545; the closing check run alone after each spoiling; the rollback restoring 545's definitions and the grant and a re-apply taking it away) and edits to the older real-Postgres tests that drove the two functions as a signed-in user (they hand the grant back inside their throwaway databases, read the real grants from the migrated template, and know the migrations that redefine the wrappers after 533 and 545). Mutation: 37 mutants of the migration and its rollback (each wrapper's client check removed, half-compared, inverted, given another SQLSTATE or message, asked of a signed-in caller only or of nobody; the grants: authenticated keeping either wrapper, the service role losing either, an unguarded body or anon given EXECUTE; each read of the closing check; the rollback's grants and definitions): 26 caught by the tests and 11 refused by the migration's own closing check when applied (reported apart), none survived. The first run (36 mutants, before the closing check also read that the reserved key is removed) left four alive: two were not mutants at all (a REVOKE from the service role followed by the GRANT that gives it back) and were rebuilt to take the grant away too, and two were real gaps, the closing check's clauses for the caller check and for the carried audit actor, which had no spoil that removed only them and now have one each, as does the clause for the reserved key.

- **THE BROWSER REACHES NO PAYROLL, BANK OR DOCUMENT TABLE AND NO STORAGE, AND THE GUARD'S LIST OF WHAT IT STILL REACHES IS EMPTY** (POST-A-010, 25-10-2026, four steps: bank accounts #891, documents and Storage #892, the staff payroll screens, the employee portal; no migration). **WHAT WAS WRONG:** about three hundred `.from("...")` calls in `apps/web` read and wrote tables over PostgREST with the signed-in person's token, so the role, the per-person grid a Partner sets on the Team screen, the client assignment, MFA and the upload bounds had to be said a second time as database policy, and for payroll, documents and the bank accounts they were not: a Partner who unticked Banking for a Manager stopped the server answering that Manager and left the same list readable from the browser. Two defects hid in the reads: a PostgREST response is cut at about a thousand rows without a word (the Documents list, the payroll roster and run list, the Leave tab's year of attendance, all read once), and the Leave tab added the days taken in the browser over that cut read, so the balance left came out too high. **WHAT IT IS NOW:** `tests/test_a_sensitive_table_is_not_read_from_the_browser.py` defines the three groups by RULE (every `payroll_*` and `bank_*` table, the leave, attendance and salary tables, the document tables, and every use of Supabase Storage) and scans `apps/web` for a literal `.from("<table>")`, for `deleteRowAndConfirm(client, "<table>", id)` and for any `.storage`; a table named by an interpolated template is refused outright. Its `REMAINING` list was how the item moved in steps (a new reach failed as "new", a moved one failed as "stale" until its line was deleted) and is EMPTY; it is kept with its checks so that the day a screen reaches one of these tables again somebody must move it behind the API or write down why. **WHAT THE SERVER GAINED:** `GET /api/banking/accounts` already existed; `/api/client-documents` (list, upload, link, delete; Partner-only delete that first asks whether a later version, a Form 26AS upload or a tax claim's evidence still points at the record); `DELETE /api/portal/document-requests/{id}`; `GET /api/accounting/shared-reports/{id}/download-url`; `POST/GET /api/income-tax/notices/{id}/attachment[-url]`; `GET /api/payroll/attendance-board` and `/leave-ledger`; and, on the employee principal alone, `GET /api/portal/employee/access`, `/me`, `/payslips`, `/leave-balances` and `GET/PUT /tax-declaration`. The existing document, roster and run lists now read every row by keyset pages. **THE RULES THIS TAUGHT, EACH HELD BY A TEST:** (1) A file this request stored and whose record could not be written is taken out again through the SERVER's client and an empty answer from the removal is reported, because migration 469 lets only a Partner delete from the bucket and Storage answers a denied removal with an empty list and no error: a clean-up through the caller's own token leaves an Executive's file in place without a word. (2) A stored key is signed or removed only when it sits under the row's own firm and client (or `shared_reports/{client}/` for a shared report), because the columns that hold a key are writable over PostgREST by an Executive. (3) A signed link for a list is made in batches of 200 with a per-object fallback, because Storage's batch call raises for the whole batch when one object has no link. (4) A delete asks whether anything points at the record BEFORE it touches the file, fails closed when it cannot ask, and says in its own words (never the database's) what happened when the record is refused after the file went. (5) The upload path scanner in `test_a_session_and_a_stored_file_are_scoped_in_the_database.py` skips a whole function that names the server's client, so a clean-up that needs it lives in a function of its own. (6) The employee routes read and write with the SERVER's client (`routers.payroll._db` is the privileged one), so the principal's ids are the only scope: every query carries the employee and the firm, and the tests seed a colleague's and another firm's rows FIRST so a missing filter returns theirs. **THE EMPLOYEE'S DECLARATION IS THE ONE WRITE THE EMPLOYEE API HAS.** `tests/test_an_employee_can_see_their_own_withholding.py` held the API read-only on an owner decision of 14-09-2026 ("an employee write path is a separate decision"); the declaration was already an employee write (migration 297 let the employee file it from the browser) and moving it is the point of this item, so the guard now names exactly that one route. The body has no verified amount, no status, no proof and no person: the employee, client and firm come from the principal. A declaration the CA has finished verifying is read-only (409), and that is decided IN THE WRITE: an existing declaration is changed by an update that carries `proofs_verified = false`, as migration 297's update policy carried it, so a CA who finishes verifying between the request's read and its write is not overwritten (an independent review found the first version read the flag and then wrote unconditionally). Re-filing resets what the CA had checked, as the staff door does, because the claim is no longer the one that was checked. The staff door's validation now applies to the employee in their own words (a landlord's PAN above the Rule 26C threshold, a lender's PAN against §24(b) interest, a head payroll cannot give effect to), where before nothing refused them: a deliberate tightening. **(SUPERSEDED BY POST-A-340, 26-10-2026: the filing is now ONE database function in one transaction under a row lock, `file_it_declaration`, migration 548; the read-back, the put-back and the update-that-carries-`proofs_verified = false` described next are gone, and the function refuses a verified declaration inside the same transaction. See the payroll record.)** The three steps were not one transaction (no migration): the old lines are read in full, the new lines go in before anything is removed, whatever else the declaration then holds is removed, the result is READ BACK and must be exactly the new lines (a delete a policy refused comes back as an empty answer and no error), and a failure at any step puts back what it replaced: the new lines come out, the old lines that are gone are written again with their own ids and figures, the header goes back to what it was (only while it is still unverified, so a put-back never undoes a verification that landed meanwhile) or is removed if it did not exist, and a put-back that itself fails is reported. A first filing made from two windows at once is a 409 in words (the unique index decided). Two re-filings by one employee at the same instant could interleave, and the answer was POST-A-340, one database function. A refusal names the box ("Landlord's PAN must be ...", "The landlord's name must be at most 200 characters.") and the tab's boxes stop at those lengths. **THE LEAVE YEAR:** the portal asked for the April-to-March year's first calendar year, which from January to March is the PREVIOUS year's row, while the staff side records an allotment "for a calendar year" and the attendance rows it sums carry the calendar year; the server asks for the current calendar year in IST. **WHAT IT DOES NOT DO, STATED SO NOBODY ASSUMES IT:** it revokes no grant and drops no policy. `authenticated` still holds privileges on every table the screens stopped using, so a person with their own sign-in can still call PostgREST by hand and reach exactly what the policies allow; the real-Postgres access matrix keeps trying every actor against each moved table in `ALSO_COVERED` for that reason. That is POST-A-339, and its first step is to move every read or write the API makes with the request's own client to the privileged client in a change that ships before the migration. It also leaves two things as they were: an employee's declaration replaces its lines wholesale, which drops any proof reference or attachment a verifier had put on a line (the browser's save did the same), and the payroll staff pages lose the saving the direct reads gave on a cold server, one fewer hop, which is the price of asking the permission once. **HOW IT IS HELD:** the guard above; `tests/test_a_denial_says_what_it_reaches.py`, which now parses the screens the Team notice names as read directly (none today) and has a negative control; `tests/test_the_payroll_staff_screens_ask_the_server_for_their_lists.py` (24), `test_an_employee_reads_their_own_record_through_the_server.py` (17), `test_an_employee_files_their_own_declaration_through_the_server.py` (38), the three document files (105) and `apps/web/scripts/a-payroll-screen-is-withheld-from-a-person-without-payroll-access.test.ts`, whose list of employee-portal readers is empty. Hand-made faults in the new routes (25 in the document routes, 16 in the payroll lists, 34 in the employee routes, re-run after the review below) were each caught by a test. Both of the last two steps were reviewed independently before they were merged: the payroll review found two failed-load screens that kept the old "timed out" sentence for a refusal in words and a month in the URL that stranded the page, and the employee-portal review found the lock check non-atomic, a put-back that could not restore old lines, unlabelled refusals and a raw "Missing or invalid Authorization header" for a signed-out visit; all are fixed.

- **A PLAIN TABLE WRITE THE SERVER MAKES FOR A PERSON IS AUDITED AS THAT PERSON, BECAUSE THE ROW-LEVEL EDIT LOG IS WRITTEN BY A TRIGGER THAT ONLY SEES A SIGNED-IN WRITER** (migration 547, POST-A-339 step 0, 26-10-2026). **WHAT IS AT STAKE:** `audit_capture` (migration 111, redacted by 336, with the carried actor of 545) writes the row-level edit log only when `auth.uid()` is present, and its own comment says why: a service-role write "is audited in-app by `log_event`". Production holds 492 bank-line creates and 789 bank-line updates written by that trigger in 120 days against 58 written by the application (`bank_post`, `bank_undo`, `bank_match`), and every client-document create and delete (22 and 22) comes from the trigger alone. POST-A-339 takes the signed-in role's grants on the payroll, bank and document tables away, which makes the API write them with its privileged client, so without this step each of those writes would leave the edit log with no row, no person, no before and after, and nothing to say so. **WHAT 547 DOES:** migration 545 had already given both trigger functions one reader, `carried_audit_actor()`, for the ledger functions' transaction-local setting; a plain PostgREST write cannot set a setting, so 547 teaches the same reader one more place to look: the request header `x-acting-auth-user`, which PostgREST exposes as `current_setting('request.headers')`, and ONLY when the request runs as `service_role` (`current_setting('role')`, which PostgREST sets from the verified key and a caller cannot choose). A value that is not a uuid, or names nobody in `users`, is no actor; a readable setting of 545 wins when both are present; a garbage (non-empty, not a uuid) setting is still no actor and the header is not a fallback for it, but an EMPTY setting, which is what 545's carrier writes for "no actor" and what a pooled connection leaves behind, is read as no setting, so the header is read (decided, because the two cannot be told apart and the header is the verified person of the request); a signed-in person is never replaced (auth.uid() wins) or impersonated (their role is not `service_role`). A row written for a header-carried person says so in its metadata (`actor_source: carried`), as 545's does. `audit_actor_probe()` (read-only, the service role alone) returns the role and header value the database saw, so `/health` can say whether the hosted API passes the header through (the Python that reports it ships after this, because the schema guard flips `/health` to 503 for a function the code names and the database lacks). **WHY IT SHIPS ALONE AND FIRST:** nothing sends the header yet, so it changes nothing when it applies; the Python that sends it (`core.db_provider.privileged_for`) ships after this is applied, and every revoke after that. **WHAT IT DOES NOT DO:** it does not check that the person belongs to the row's firm or is still active (the reader cannot see the row; the header is set only by the server, from the person its own authentication verified, on routes that already keep that person in their firm; a person of another firm is accepted today and a test pins that, so a hardening, which would have to live in the two trigger functions where the row's firm is known, flips it knowingly), a write the server makes for nobody (a job) carries no header and writes no row, as today, and the `firms` table's own audit trigger does not read the carried actor, so the server's writes to `firms` stay unaudited. **THE GATE IS THE DATABASE ROLE:** a signed-in session cannot become `service_role`, because nothing the API or PostgREST exposes runs a caller's SQL; on a real Supabase connection the authenticator role is a member of every API role, so `SET ROLE authenticated; SET ROLE service_role` succeeds there, and a future function that ran caller-supplied SQL would turn the header into impersonation and must be refused on that ground. **VERIFIED:** 52 real-Postgres cases (30 written first, then 22 more after an independent read of the migration) (`tests/test_547_..._pg.py`): the server's request audited as the named person on an ordinary table and on the document tables, no header or a label that names nobody writing no row and breaking nothing, a signed-in Manager sending a Partner's id audited as themselves and not marked carried, the owner connection not believed, the setting beating the header, an empty setting reading the header, `anon`, `authenticated` without a subject and a token that merely claims role `service_role` all not believed (each with a proof that the UPDATE touched the row), a service request that also carries a subject audited as the subject, request headers of every other shape and a uuid in upper case, without hyphens, in braces or wrapped in spaces, a real ledger posting made on a header-carrying request, the seventeen audited sensitive tables still carrying the trigger switched on for insert, update and delete, the reader still invoker with a pinned search path and closed to every signed-in role, its body 545's plus exactly the added lines, the closing check failing when each claim is spoiled, the rollback restoring 545's definition and a re-apply bringing it back; seven hand-made faults (any role believed, any role but the owner believed, an unknown person believed, the header never read, the header beating the setting, an empty setting treated as garbage, a header trimmed before it is read) were each caught. **NOT VERIFIED:** that the hosted API forwards a custom header to PostgREST; the documented behaviour says it does and the probe is how production will say so.

- **THE API READS AND WRITES THE PAYROLL, BANK AND DOCUMENT TABLES WITH ITS PRIVILEGED CLIENT, NAMING THE FIRM, AHEAD OF THE MIGRATIONS THAT TAKE THE SIGNED-IN ROLE'S GRANTS OFF THEM, AND EVERY STATEMENT IT MAKES FOR A PERSON NAMES THAT PERSON** (POST-A-339 step 1, 26-10-2026; no migration; needs 547 applied). **WHAT WAS WRONG:** POST-A-010 took the browser off these tables, but `authenticated` still holds the grants, and the API itself, under `USE_USER_JWT`, read and wrote most of them with the REQUEST'S own client (the caller's token), so a revoke would have made those queries fail SOFT and wrong (an empty list, a lookup that says not found, a posting skipped), the order the four earlier writers were moved in. **WHAT IT DOES:** `core/db_provider.privileged_for(db)` (the sixth function of the one door) answers the privileged client where a database is configured, the caller's own `db` where none is (mock mode and the test doubles), and the privileged client wrapped by `core/acting_client.ActingClient` where a person is in flight, which puts `x-acting-auth-user: <the auth id the authentication dependency verified>` on every statement it builds (migration 547 believes it only on a `service_role` request), so the audit triggers keep naming the person. 211 call sites in 42 modules (the bank router and every `bank_*` service, `banking_service`, the document repository and the three document routers, the portal and employee-portal services, the payslip, ECR, ESI and bonus readers, the FX services, the post-dated cheque and reconciliation services, the trusted-rule job) now start from it. `routers/payroll.py` was already wholly privileged and is exempt BY SHAPE, tested. The document repository and the risk engine take the firm and read NOTHING without it (`_NO_FIRM`): the privileged client has no row security, so a call that names no firm is a call that finds no rows, and the four repository methods that were on the frozen list of unscoped chains left it. **WHAT THE PRIVILEGED CLIENT LACKS IS THE ROUTE'S TO ASSERT:** the firm, the client assignment and the role tier that the signed-in role's policies applied are now `rbac()`, `assert_client_access`, `filter_by_client` and a `firm_id` on every query; `tests/test_every_query_on_a_firm_table_carries_its_firm_scope.py` holds the last of those and the new `tests/test_a_payroll_bank_or_document_table_is_queried_with_the_privileged_client.py` holds WHICH client each of these queries is made with, as a rule over the source and not a list of files (a parameter is never privileged, whatever its caller passes; the table named at run time is checked where a sensitive one can reach it; one module exempt by its shape, which is itself tested). **THE LABEL IS PROVEN FROM BOTH ENDS:** Python proves it leaves (a real supabase client given a recording transport sends the header on all five verbs, `single`, `maybe_single`, `from_` and `rpc`, and two requests never share one); migration 547's tests prove the database believes it; the one fact neither can prove is that the HOSTED API forwards a custom header to PostgREST, so the API asks `audit_actor_probe()` once at boot and `/health` carries the word (`audit_actor`: `carried`, `not_seen`, `wrong_role`, `failed`, `unchecked`), which a person reads with `curl .../health`. **UNTIL THE WORD IS `carried` IN PRODUCTION NO REVOKE MAY FOLLOW**, because a moved write the database cannot name leaves the edit log; the fallback is an explicit `log_event` at each moved write. **NOT DONE:** Supabase Storage (its own change: the bucket's policies for a signed-in session), the revoke migrations, the dead employee-own-record policies, and the Team screen's notice; the grants are untouched, so nothing a signed-in person could reach before is closed yet. **FOUND BY AN INDEPENDENT READ OF THIS DIFF AND FIXED BEFORE IT SHIPPED** (the rule test and the existing scope tests had all passed): (1) `GET /api/banking/register` named a bank account, left `client_id` optional and asked the client only `if client_id:`, so a caller assigned to client A who named client B's account and no client read B's whole register; the route now asks the account row's own client, and `tests/test_a_scoped_caller_is_narrowed_in_the_statement_and_a_named_account_is_tied_to_its_client.py` sweeps the route table for the shape (a banking route that names an account or statement with an optional client must ask the row or be a narrowed list); (2) attaching a document to a bank line asked the document's firm and not its client; (3) five lists (statements, transactions, reconciliations, column-mapping layouts, documents) were read for the whole firm and narrowed to the caller's clients afterwards, so the thousand-row cap or a `limit` applied to the firm first and `has_more` described the firm; `core.authz.scope_query_to_clients` puts the caller's clients into the statement (an empty set names a client nobody has, never "no filter") and the filter after it stays as the second line; (4) `replace_bank_transaction_splits` is a definer executable by the SERVICE ROLE ALONE (migration 256; production's ACL read on 26-10-2026) and the splits route was calling it on the caller's own sign-in, so the database refused it whoever asked (the transfer-pairing functions had the same defect and were fixed earlier); it is called with the privileged client now and is a `SENSITIVE_RPCS` entry; (5) a statement's delete check read its lines unpaged, so a posted line past the thousandth was never seen and the statement and all its lines went; it pages now; (6) the audit-header probe ran once at boot only if the schema verdict was healthy at that moment, so a deploy that booted before its migration kept saying `unchecked`; the watcher runs it when the verdict turns healthy. **ACCEPTED, AND WRITTEN HERE SO THAT IT IS A DECISION:** a member suspended or demoted keeps the access they had for up to 30 seconds on these tables, because the user lookup in `core/auth.py` is cached for 30 seconds with no invalidation and the database's policy used to deny them at once (`routers/payroll.py` already lived with that window); a statement the scheduler's manual run (`POST /api/scheduler/run`) makes inline is labelled with the Partner who pressed the button, while the nightly sweep labels nothing; the payroll router's writes use the plain service client and stay unlabelled, as they were unaudited before; the rule test does not see a module-level constant passed to `.table(...)` or a `name=` keyword (no instance exists) and the payroll tables outside its list (`bonus_declarations`, `client_payroll_settings`, `client_pt_registrations`, `epfo_ecr_filings`, `firm_pt_slabs`) are the revoke migration's inventory to settle; `POST /statements/inspect` returns the saved column layout of any account of the firm, which is the designed cross-client reuse of a bank's layout and nothing else. Two older defects of the same files went onto the ledger (POST-A-341, POST-A-342). **VERIFIED:** the full mock-mode suite on this tree, rebased onto main with migrations 547 and 548: 27,444 passed, 5,506 skipped (the real-Postgres files, which skip without a database), 2 failed, both in `tests/test_the_server_does_not_fetch_wherever_it_is_pointed.py`, which resolves `example.com` and fails the same way on unmodified main in a container with no outbound name resolution. A first run of the same tree had a third failure, `test_attaching_another_firms_document_is_a_404`: its fixture built the document with no client, from before the client check on a bank line's attachment; it is corrected and now also drives the same firm's document of another client (it fails without the client filter). Real Postgres: the targeted set on this tree (migration apply, rerunnable, atomic, the 547 and 548 files, backend tables, schema and guard drift against production, the definer rule, the schema guard's function list, frontend columns, assignment scope, the role-by-table matrix, RLS coverage) passed, 204; the full suite is the required CI job (a full local run of this change before it was rebased onto 548 passed: 5,447 passed, 10 skipped). Lint ratchet 0 new, 0 stale. Negative control: 11 hand-made faults in the new rules and the labelling were each caught. **NOT VERIFIED:** that the hosted API forwards a custom header to PostgREST; the documented behaviour says it does and the probe is how production will say so.
- **EVERY FILE THE API STORES, SIGNS OR REMOVES GOES THROUGH ONE DOOR, THE SERVER'S CLIENT BOUND TO THE FOLDER THE ROUTE ASKED ABOUT, AHEAD OF THE MIGRATION THAT DROPS THE STORAGE POLICIES** (POST-A-339, the Storage step, 26-10-2026; no migration). **WHAT WAS WRONG:** nine routers reached Supabase Storage with the REQUEST's own client (`get_supabase().storage`, which under `USE_USER_JWT` carries the caller's token), so the policies of migrations 005 and 469 on `storage.objects` decided who could open which folder (the first folder the caller's own firm, the second a client they are assigned to, and a tier for the verb: a Partner to delete), and a signed-in person with their own token could call Storage by hand and open whatever those policies allowed, when nothing legitimate needs that to work at all. A second place for a rule about people to live, in SQL, for a thing the route had just decided in Python. **WHAT IT DOES:** `core/storage_door.storage_folder(bucket, *folders)` answers an object that stores, signs (one link or a batch), removes and gives a public link for keys UNDER THE FOLDER IT WAS BOUND TO and refuses any other before a request is made (another firm's, another client's, a climb out with `..` or `.` as a whole path component, a doubled, leading or trailing separator, a backslash, a control or bidirectional character, `?` or `#` and a percent-encoded dot or separator, which the Python client would put into the request's URL unencoded, the folder itself, a value that is not a string), using the SERVER's client. `..` inside a name (`invoice..pdf`) is a name and opens: an independent read found the first version refused it as a substring, which would have made a bill attached under such a name unopenable. The route has already asked who the caller is and which client they may touch (`rbac()`, `assert_client_access`); the door is what makes the server's key unable to be used for anything outside the folder the route asserted. Every router that stored, signed or removed a file now opens one: documents, the client vault, tax-notice attachments (bound to THAT notice's folder, `{firm}/{client}/notices/{notice}/`), debit and credit note attachments, the purchase bill link, the invoice photo, the year-end packs (`{firm}/{client}/{year}/`), shared reports (`shared_reports/{client}/`), the firm's logo (`logos/{firm}/`) and the portal download. **A KEY IN A ROW IS NOT TRUSTED:** the columns that hold a key (`documents.storage_path`, the notes' and bills' `document_url`, the notices' two paths, a year-end export's `storage_path`) are writable over PostgREST by an Executive, so a row could be made to name somebody else's object and have it signed on the strength of a key that opens everything; each download link is now signed through the door bound to the firm and client the route PROVED (the row read under the caller's firm and checked against their assignment; for a year-end pack the ENGAGEMENT's client and year, and not what the export row says about itself, which an independent read found and a test now holds), plus the notice for a notice, and a key outside it is the same fixed 404 as a missing one. **THE RULES THAT HOLD IT:** `tests/test_storage_is_reached_only_through_the_door.py` walks every module of `apps/api` and fails any read of `.storage` outside the door (whatever the client was called), fails a door opened with fewer than two folders (so `storage_folder("Documents", firm_id)`, every client of the firm, cannot be written), holds the door's folder-name pattern equal to `core/uploads`' own (a value the upload code let into a key is one the door accepts), and runs the door against a recording server client on every foreign key above; `tests/test_a_session_and_a_stored_file_are_scoped_in_the_database.py` now reads the firm-then-client shape off the door's folders where it used to read it off `.upload(` calls. **THE SHARED SERVICE CLIENT'S STORAGE SESSION IS ON HTTP/1.1 (found by an independent read, fixed before this ships):** storage3 0.9 builds its own httpx session with `http2=True` and supabase-py keeps ONE Storage client per client, so once every file went through the cached service-role client, the whole threadpool would have driven one HTTP/2 connection, the situation `_force_http1` records for PostgREST (`LocalProtocolError: Received pseudo-header in trailer`, load-dependent, never seen by a test). Before, under `USE_USER_JWT`, each request built its own client and its own Storage session. `core/supabase_client._force_http1_storage` replaces both attributes the library keeps the session in (`session`, and `_client`, which `from_` hands to each bucket) when the service client is built; four tests hold it, including a canary that fails the day storage3 stops forcing HTTP/2. **THREE BEHAVIOUR CHANGES, DELIBERATE:** (1) the invoice-photo upload put the caller's raw file name in the key (only `/` replaced), which the door would refuse if it held `..`, a backslash or a control character, and a refusal there is swallowed because the attachment is best-effort; the name is now the ASCII one `core/uploads` makes, so a name such as `a..b.pdf` still stores; (2) a document is deleted from Storage by the route's own tier (`rbac("document", "delete")`), where the old policy asked for a Partner whatever the route said, so a per-person grant of that action to a non-Partner now actually removes the file (the route's rule is the one the grid shows, and it is the one that was meant); (3) a tax-notice attachment is stored on the route's `income_tax:compute` write and not on the policy's `document`, `accounting` or `year_end` write, which differ only for a member with a per-person override (the role defaults are identical). **WHAT IT DOES NOT DO:** the policies are still in the database; the migration that drops them (and revokes the document tables) follows once this has been live, because the old API against dropped policies fails soft and wrong (a file that does not open). **VERIFIED:** the full mock-mode suite on this tree passed except the two tests that resolve `example.com` and fail the same way on unmodified `main` in a container with no outbound name resolution; thirty hand-made faults in the door, the client factory and the call sites (each check removed in turn, including the component, control, bidirectional, `?`, `#` and encoded-separator limbs, the request's client in place of the server's, an upsert always sent, a slash allowed in a folder, a door with no folder, a link signed for the wrong client, a notice door bound to the client's folder only, a year-end link bound to the export row's client or year, the raw file name back in the key, the Storage session left on HTTP/2 or replaced in only one of its two attributes) were each caught by a test that failed against the fault.

- **A SIGNED-IN SESSION READS AND WRITES NONE OF THE PAYROLL TABLES OR THE BANK TABLES, RUNS NEITHER BANK REPORT AND READS NO FOREIGN BANK BALANCE, AND THE ROW POLICIES STAY AS THE SECOND LOCK** (POST-A-339 step 2, migrations 549 and 550, 26-10-2026). **WHAT WAS WRONG:** POST-A-010 took the browser off these tables and POST-A-339 step 1 took the API's own queries off the caller's token, but `authenticated` still held SELECT on all twenty-three payroll tables and the `salary_slips` view, INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on the tax declaration, its lines and the firm's professional-tax slabs, and every verb on the eight bank tables (with the platform's TRUNCATE on `authenticated` and `anon`), so a person with their own sign-in could call PostgREST by hand and read, or write, whatever the row policies let them: payslips, salary history, loans, PF and ESI remittances, bank account numbers, narrations that name strangers to the engagement, the matches that settle invoices and the rules that post to the ledger on their own. `bank_register` and `bank_reconciling_items` are SECURITY INVOKER reports over those tables and were executable by PUBLIC, `anon` and `authenticated`; and `fx_foreign_bank_balances` (migration 281) is a SECURITY DEFINER function that returns every foreign-currency bank account of a client with its bank name, account number and balances, gated on the caller's firm and the client's assignment and never on the banking tier, so a Partner's block on banking did not reach it (an independent read of this change found it; the Team screen's sentence would otherwise have been false). **WHAT THEY DO:** 549 revokes SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE on the twenty-three payroll tables (and SELECT on the view) from PUBLIC, `authenticated` and `anon`; 550 does the same on the eight bank tables and revokes EXECUTE on the two reports and on `fx_foreign_bank_balances` (whose one caller now asks through the privileged client, falling back to its own privileged reconciliation if refused) from PUBLIC, `anon` and `authenticated`, granting it to the service role EXPLICITLY (production's ACL for `bank_reconciling_items` held no service-role entry: the role reached it through PUBLIC, which the revoke would have taken). REFERENCES and TRIGGER, which are not privileges on rows, are left as 499 left them. Each ends in a closing check that asks the EFFECTIVE privilege (`has_table_privilege`, `has_any_column_privilege`, `has_function_privilege`: a privilege that reaches the role through PUBLIC, another role or a column survives a look at the two role names) and rolls the whole migration back if a signed-in role still holds anything, or if the service role would be left without a privilege the API needs. **THE POLICIES STAY, AND THE LEDGER'S PROPOSAL TO DROP SOME WAS NOT TAKEN:** the employee-own-record policies of 262, 296 and 297 become unreachable with the grants, and the ledger line proposed dropping them in the same migration; they are kept, because a later migration that grants a list of tables again (041 and 055 did) undoes a revoke and would not undo a policy, they cost nothing, and a policy removed here would be one less reason a regrant is harmless. **THE SECOND LOCK IS STILL MEASURED:** a signed-in role cannot be tried against a table it cannot touch, so each test module that measures a policy or a trigger as a signed-in role gives the privilege back in its throwaway database from the migration's own rollback file (`tests/_signed_in_payroll_writes.give_the_signed_in_payroll_access_back` and `tests/_signed_in_bank_access.give_the_signed_in_bank_access_back`, which cannot drift from what the migration took), the access matrix's main file reads "no privilege" in every cell of the tables these migrations closed and `rls_expected_second_lock.json` holds what the policies decide with the privilege given back (it gained `bank_accounts` and `bank_transactions`; the other six bank tables are held by their own test files), and the matrix's controls that put a policy hole back apply the rollbacks first and are measured against that file. The list of client tables with no assignment scope lost the seven payroll and one bank entries that left the signed-in role's reach (its ceiling fell from 15 to 7), and the deny-list of tables the API's user token does not read gained the twenty-three and the eight. **VERIFIED:** each migration has its own real-Postgres file (the group is a RULE found in the migrations and not a list of files; no signed-in privilege survives on any table, effective; a PostgREST-shaped call as `authenticated` and as `anon` is refused on every verb; the service role keeps what it needs and runs both reports; no policy or view of another table reads a closed table; apply and rollback leave every row policy byte for byte as it was; the rollback restores exactly what the earlier migrations granted and the migration can be applied again; the closing check refuses a privilege another role handed out, at table or at column level, a report still executable by a signed-in user, and a service role left without a privilege; the privileges the hosting platform adds by default (TRUNCATE, a grant to PUBLIC) are put into the throwaway database first, because a database built from the migrations alone never holds them). Twenty hand-made faults in the two migrations were each caught by a test that failed against the fault, and the first run left four alive (TRUNCATE and PUBLIC not revoked, the column limb and the service-role SELECT limb of the closing check), each of which now has a test. An independent read of the change found `fx_foreign_bank_balances`, closed in 550 above. 215 existing real-Postgres tests in twenty-four files failed against the first version of 549 and every one of them measures a policy as a signed-in role; they were adapted as above and not weakened, and the full suite is the required CI job. **NOT DONE, AND SAID SO:** the document tables (`documents`, `client_documents`, `document_requests`, `document_extractions`, `document_risks`) and the object policies on Supabase Storage, which follow in migration 551 now that every Storage call goes through `core/storage_door` (merged and running). The payroll router's own writes still use the plain service client and stay unlabelled in the edit log, as they were. **WHAT A SIGNED-IN SESSION SEES NOW:** "permission denied for table <name>" (SQLSTATE 42501) on any of these tables and "permission denied for function" on either report, instead of a row-security answer; a foreign-key cascade is not a privilege check, so deleting a client still cascades.

- **A SIGNED-IN SESSION READS AND WRITES NONE OF THE DOCUMENT TABLES AND OPENS NO STORED FILE, AND THE TABLE POLICIES STAY AS THE SECOND LOCK WHILE THE OBJECT POLICIES WERE THE ONLY LOCK** (POST-A-339 step 3, migration 551, 26-10-2026). **WHAT WAS WRONG:** POST-A-010 took the browser off the five document tables (`documents`, `client_documents`, `document_requests`, `document_extractions`, `document_risks`) and off Supabase Storage, and the Storage step (`core/storage_door`, merged and running before this) made every file the API stores, signs or removes go through the server's client bound to the folder the route asked about; but `authenticated` still held SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on the five tables (and the platform's TRUNCATE on `authenticated` and `anon`), so a person with their own sign-in could call PostgREST by hand and read or write what the row policies let them (the register of every document a client's papers are filed under, what a model read out of them, the risks it raised), and the object policies of migrations 005, 426 and 469 on `storage.objects` still let a signed-in session open, store and (a Partner) remove a file under `{firm}/{client}/...` in the buckets `Documents` and `year-end-exports`, with no route to ask whether the per-person grid allowed it. **WHAT IT DOES:** the migration revokes SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE on the five tables from PUBLIC, `authenticated` and `anon` (REFERENCES and TRIGGER, which are not privileges on rows, are left as 499, 549 and 550 left them), DROPS the six signed-in object policies (`documents_storage_select`, `_insert`, `_delete`, `year_end_exports_storage_select`, `_insert`, and `_delete`, which neither production nor a migrated database has but a database that kept it would not keep), and closes with checks that ask the EFFECTIVE privilege (table and column, and, for Storage, that row security is ON for `storage.objects`, because a table with it off and no policy is open to every role that holds a privilege on it, and that every policy left there asks for the service role and nobody else: a policy that does not name it, or names it beside an OR, is a way in for a signed-in session, whoever wrote it and whichever bucket it names, so the migration refuses and rolls back; an independent read of this change found the first and the OR limb missing). The compat bootstrap of a throwaway database now turns row security on for `storage.objects`, as hosted Supabase has it. `service_role` keeps every privilege, and so do the six service-role object policies of 204 and 426 that the API's own key needs; the three helper grants to `supabase_storage_admin` that 469 made are left in place (harmless without a policy that calls them, and one thing fewer for the rollback to remember). **TWO LOCKS ON THE TABLES, ONE ON THE FILES:** the table policies stay as the SECOND lock, as 499, 549 and 550 left theirs (a later migration that grants a list of tables again would undo a revoke and not a policy), and the modules that measure them give the privilege back in a throwaway database through `tests/_signed_in_document_access.py`, which applies 551's own rollback file so it cannot drift from what the migration took away. For Storage THERE IS NO SECOND LOCK: the object policies were the lock, so dropping them is the closure, with row security still on for `storage.objects` (a table with row security on and no policy is how this platform says nobody), and the modules that measured those policies (the suspended-member and per-person-grid tests) now measure them as the ROLLBACK restores them, which holds that the rollback leaves a database whose files open for the people the policies name; `test_551_*_pg.py` and the access matrix measure the live state. The matrix keeps `documents`, `client_documents` and `document_requests` and the two `storage/` rows: the main file reads no privilege in every cell of the first three and nothing for every signed-in actor on the storage rows, and `rls_expected_second_lock.json` holds what the policies decided before (the cells the main file held, unchanged). **TESTS THAT WERE WRITTEN BECAUSE A MUTATION RUN LEFT THEM ALIVE:** the platform's default privileges (TRUNCATE for `anon` and `authenticated`, a grant to PUBLIC) are put into the throwaway database before the migration is applied, because a database built from the migrations alone never holds them and the TRUNCATE and PUBLIC limbs of the revoke would otherwise be untested; and a column-level privilege another role handed to `authenticated` (which no table-level REVOKE removes) proves the column limb of the closing check, as it does in 549 and 550. **THE TEAM SCREEN'S SENTENCE:** it used to end "The one other read it reaches is a stored year-end export file", the one read the grid reached in Storage; that policy is gone, so it says that the files a practice stores for its clients (documents, attachments, reports shared to the portal and year-end exports) are opened only through the server and that the database lets no signed-in session open or store one, and that the firm's logo, which sits in the public bucket `firm-assets` and was never behind a policy, is read by its address. **WHAT A SIGNED-IN SESSION NOW SEES:** any read or write of one of the five tables is "permission denied for table" (42501); any Storage call it makes on `Documents` or `year-end-exports` finds no object and stores none; a foreign-key cascade is not a privilege check, so deleting a client still cascades. **VERIFIED:** the migration has its own real-Postgres file (the group is a RULE found in the migrations; no signed-in privilege survives on any of the five tables, effective; a PostgREST-shaped call as `authenticated` and as `anon` is refused on every verb; the service role keeps what it needs; no policy or view of another table reads a closed table; apply and rollback leave every table policy byte for byte as it was; a signed-in Partner reads, stores and removes in Storage before the migration and does none of it after; every policy left on `storage.objects` asks for the service role and nobody else; the rollback re-creates the object policies exactly as 469 wrote them and restores exactly the grants the earlier migrations gave; the migration can be applied again; the closing check refuses a privilege another role handed out at table or column level, a policy that does not ask for the service role or asks for it beside an OR, row security switched off for `storage.objects`, and a service role left without a privilege). Eighteen hand-made faults in the migration (a table left out, TRUNCATE or PUBLIC not revoked, the service role revoked too, each of the five signed-in object policies kept, none dropped, each limb of the closing check removed) were each caught by a test that failed against the fault, and the three limbs added after the independent read have their own. The existing real-Postgres modules that measured the table policies, or the Storage policies, as a signed-in role (the engagement-letter children, the per-person grid, the suspended-member and storage tests, the access matrix) give the privilege and the policies back in their throwaway database through the rollback and keep measuring what they measured. **Not done, and said so:** `document_extractions` and `document_risks` are not tables the access matrix observes (their own test files hold them); the six service-role object policies stay, and the migration refuses if any other policy appears; `firm-assets` is a public bucket whose objects are read by URL, so no object policy ever governed it.

- **A DOCUMENT NAMES ONLY ROWS OF ITS OWN CLIENT, A PERMANENT DELETE COUNTS WHAT IT WOULD CASCADE, AND A FAILED PERMISSION READ IS NOT NOTHING** (POST-A-338, POST-A-342, POST-A-219, POST-A-334, 28-10-2026). Four small defects of one family: a check that answers "nothing" or "yes" because it could not look. **Named rows.** A Bill of Entry carries a vendor and a purchase bill, a fixed asset the bill it was bought on, a delivery challan an order, a customer, a vendor and an invoice, a purchase or sales order its party; the API reads and writes by firm and id through the privileged client and the foreign key on each column is not client-aware, so an id of a SIBLING client of the same firm was accepted. The worst case: client A's Bill of Entry naming client B's purchase bill attached A's customs duty to B's bill and so to B's stock cost. `services/named_row_ownership` asks once, before anything is written, under the firm AND the client, one literal-table function each (vendor, customer, purchase bill, sales invoice; a table read through a variable is invisible to the column scan), and answers 404 in the words a missing row gets; the purchase and sales cycles use their own `order_of_client` / `row_of_client`. Asked at the Bill of Entry create and correct doors (the ids a correction SENDS), `create_asset` and `correct_asset` (vendor, bill, bank account), a cost on work in progress, both cycles' create doors (and the quotation a sales order converts, and the order line a receipt or challan names, which is refused when the document names no order), the one id the challan update door can still re-point, the sales invoice edit, the four note kinds at create and edit (the party) and the input-credit reversal; the landed-cost carry-over reads the bill by client as well, so a row written before the check cannot charge a sibling's bill, and `add_landed_cost` and the basis override refuse a body client that is not the bill's. `match_bill` asks the order the bill names. Production was read on 28-10-2026 for rows already crossed: none of fifteen relationships crosses clients, and the Bill of Entry, goods-receipt, order, challan and landed-cost tables hold no row that names another at all. **The first version of this covered fewer doors than it claimed**: an independent review reproduced five it had missed (a sales order converting a sibling's quotation, a fixed-asset correction, a draft invoice edit, the four note kinds' party, an order line named without an order), all now closed, and the test that lists the doors checks that each asks BEFORE it writes; it is a list and cannot find a new door, so the derived rule is on the ledger. **A count that cannot be read.** `delete_rule` counted the lines a rule posted inside `except Exception: posted = 0`, and the count decides whether an Executive may delete it, so a failed read let the deletion through exactly where it is refused; it now answers 503 with a way out for a caller who cannot delete a rule that posted lines, and lets a Manager or Partner (for whom the count decides nothing) proceed with the audit entry and the answer saying the count was UNKNOWN, not zero. The two foreign-bank reads (revaluation, FX report) answered an empty list on any error on the strength of a column that has existed for a long time, so a year-end ran without the foreign accounts and said nothing; they raise. The permanent delete of a customer or vendor did the same (`counts[label] = 0` under a comment saying a locked table "must never mask a dependency"): an unreadable table now stops the delete (503, in words) and the dependency read the dialog opens with says it could not check instead of listing nothing. **What a party's delete counts.** The catalogue lists six more tables that cascade from a party. Decided on the three tests: `post_dated_cheques` (both sides: a memorandum of money held or issued) and `tds_lower_deduction_certificates` (the section 197 certificate the withholding reads) now block, UNDATED, so the refusal claims no statutory clock for a document nobody has said is a book of account; the link to a client, the draft interest figure (derived, recomputable, and guarded once invoiced) and the statement mail log go with the party as they always did. The mail log is the one whose answer needs a retention reading nobody has made, so it is on the owner's list (S3) and unchanged. **The declaration guards.** Migration 554: the two triggers that stop an employee verifying their own declaration still asked `my_role_at_least('Manager')` for staff after 519 moved the policies to `my_permission('payroll','write','Manager')`, so a Manager a Partner had blocked from payroll who is also the linked principal of an employee row skipped the guard on their own row; both now ask the grid, the floor is unchanged, the service role is not staff by either question and passes at the next test, and the rollback is the negative control. **Verified:** 61 hand-made faults across the routes, the services and the screen's dialog were each caught by a test that failed against the fault; the 554 trigger proof runs as the real signed-in roles on a real database (a blocked Manager refused on the header and on a line, the floor unchanged, the employee still refused, the service role let through, the rollback restoring the hole and the migration applying again). **Not done, and said so:** the receipt, purchase payment, sales invoice and purchase bill create doors, the cheques and the challan returns were read by the review and already ask for their own party (or their linked document) under the client; the derived rule that would find a door nobody listed, `rcm_documents.issue_document`'s body client, and a non-UUID id answering 500 are on the ledger; a writer that does not come through these routes (an import that builds rows itself, a SQL function) is not asked. **The doors are now FOUND, not listed** (POST-A-350): `tests/test_a_request_that_names_a_row_asks_whose_it_is.py` reads every Pydantic request model of a writing route for a field that names a vendor, customer, purchase bill, sales invoice, bank account, order or quotation, follows the route five calls down (imports, singletons and methods resolved) and fails unless a `*_is_the_clients` function, the cycles' `order_of_client` / `row_of_client`, the reconciliation's `_validate_bank_account`, a read of the table filtered by `client_id` or a read compared with the client's id is reached; a door it cannot find a question for is on a reviewed list that only shrinks (one entry: the read-only near-duplicate probe). Its first run found eleven real doors, closed in the same change: a recurring invoice or purchase-bill template naming another client's customer or vendor, an e-way bill naming another client's invoice, a receipt or vendor payment naming another client's bank account (the posting resolves the account by firm alone and the ledger trigger refused it later as a 500), and the linked bill or invoice of the four note kinds at create and at correction (it was stored as sent and refused only at issue). It does not see a body taken as a plain dict, an id nested in a list, a query or form parameter, a column named another way or a database function, and a malformed id still answers 500 where the rule promises 404: POST-A-353.

- **SEVENTEEN FIRM-LEVEL TABLES ARE WRITTEN BY THE API ONLY, BECAUSE THE BROWSER NEVER WRITES THEM AND A TIER WOULD HAVE LEFT THE TIER'S HOLDERS A HAND-MADE REQUEST** (POST-A-329, migration 562, 29-10-2026). The writer map of 22-10-2026 found `account_group_mappings`, `firm_hsn_library`, `firm_hsn_rate_history`, `assignment_rules`, `entities`, `entity_relationships`, `entity_to_entity_relationships`, `leads`, `dsc_records`, `automation_rules`, `automation_executions`, `reminder_settings`, `engagement_templates`, the three recurring-template parents and `task_escalations` written on the caller's own session, and production granted `authenticated` INSERT and UPDATE on all seventeen (DELETE on sixteen; `firm_hsn_rate_history` never had it, so its delete route was refused in production and now works). The ledger offered a tier per table (`my_permission(resource, action, floor)` as in 487 to 489). The writers were moved instead and the grants taken away (the 558/559 shape), for two reasons: `apps/web` has no `.from("<table>")` for any of the seventeen, so nothing but the API writes them for a person; and a tier leaves everyone who holds it able to rewrite a firm-wide master or a document template from a hand-made request, skipping the route's own validation. About forty-five writers across twelve modules now use `privileged_for(db)`, each after the route's own `rbac()` and ownership checks and each naming the firm (a repository method such as `assignment_rule_repo.update` took a required `firm_id`; `automation_repo.log_execution` stamps the firm, because an execution row with none could never be read back by a member); the engagement-template seed that ran under `client:read` is privileged too. `automation_repo`'s dead `delete_by_recurring_config` was deleted. `tests/fixtures/tables_the_signed_in_role_cannot_write.txt` grows by the seventeen, so the source rule of `test_a_write_of_a_table_the_signed_in_role_cannot_write_is_made_on_a_client_that_can.py` holds every writer of them; `tests/test_562_..._pg.py` proves the effective privileges, the service role's writes, a Manager refused by the privilege on each table and the rollback as the negative control (it skips `assignment_rules`, which production has and a database built from the migrations does not). `fx_rates` is deliberately not in the list: production's own policies already admit only `source = 'manual'` rows at `settings:write` (Partner), and it has a writer client of its own. Deliberately NOT done: the two `account_group_mappings` write routes are still mounted (no screen calls them and the table holds no rows; retiring them is a separate deletion, and the statements read the table as an override), and the recurring-template LINE tables are still written on the caller's session.

- **A STRING THAT IS NOT AN ID ANSWERS AS A MISSING ROW, BECAUSE THE DATABASE'S OWN TYPE ERROR SAYS SO AND A SHAPE CHECK IN THE HELPER WOULD REFUSE THE SUITES' READABLE IDS** (POST-A-353, second half, 29-10-2026). `services/named_row_ownership` promises that a row which is not the client's is a 404 in the words a missing row gets, but a string that is not a UUID made PostgREST answer with a type error (SQLSTATE 22P02) that the route turned into a 500, so the promise failed for exactly the id a person typing by hand is likeliest to send. The five ownership lookups go through `_find`, which reads a 22P02 (by code, or by the "invalid input syntax for type uuid" text) as no row and lets every other database error propagate, so a lookup that could not run is never "not found". The test suites use readable non-UUID ids, which is why the check is the database's answer and not a pattern in the helper's first line. `tests/test_a_malformed_id_in_a_named_row_answers_as_a_missing_row.py` holds it for each lookup, with the missing row and the malformed id compared word for word. The first half of A-353 (widen what the derived rule sees: a `dict` body, an id inside a list, a query parameter, a column named another way) is still open.

- **A TABLE WITH NO CLIENT AT ALL IS ON A REVIEWED LIST, SAYS WHAT KIND OF TABLE IT IS, AND HAS EVERY WRITE IT IS GRANTED HELD BY A TIER OR BY THE PERSON'S OWN ROW** (POST-A-305, 29-10-2026). The catalog rules saw the tables that carry a `client_id` and the tables that hang from one by a foreign key; a table in neither was invisible to them, and two reviews found that shape by hand (the `ri_*` tables, then the seventeen of migration 562). `tests/test_a_table_with_no_client_is_firm_level_on_purpose_pg.py` is the THIRD population: every public base table `authenticated` can reach with no `client_id` and no path to a client table (the second population's recursion, `NAMED_LINKS` included), 45 of them today, each on `tests/fixtures/firm_level_tables.json` with a category, the write commands the signed-in role holds (`a` insert, `w` update, `d` delete) and a reason. Four things are held. A new table of the shape is a red test and a listed table that left the shape is a red test. The CATEGORY is verified against the table's own policies read from `pg_depend` (`reference`: no firm column and no policy asks a firm or reads another table; `parent`: a policy reads the row it hangs from; `scoped`: a policy asks the assignment scope; `personal`: a SELECT policy asks `auth.uid()` or `get_my_user_id`; `tiered`: a SELECT policy asks a role or a permission; `firm_wide`: a permissive SELECT policy asks the firm and nothing covering SELECT narrows it), so a reason cannot claim what the policies do not say. The WRITES recorded must equal what is granted, so a table that gains a write is a red test until the diff is read. And every granted write is HELD: a restrictive policy covering the command asks a tier, a permission or the person, or every permissive policy covering it does (permissive policies are OR-ed, so one bare firm policy among them admits the whole firm), or no permissive policy covers it (closed). The rule is stated as a pure function, so the mock-mode job tests it with planted policies, and six breakages were planted in a real database (a bare firm table, a listed table that gained a write, a wrong category, wrong writes, a stale entry, a granted write with no guard) and each turned a test red. What it cannot see, stated in the file: it reads what a policy refers to and not what it admits (a tier with the lowest floor is a tier), it checks a category against policies and not against the data, and it sees tables and not views or functions. What it found: nothing writable without a guard (the seven tables that still hold a write are `cross_client_matches`, `dtaa_treaty_rates`, `firms`, `fx_rates`, `user_notification_preferences`, `user_permissions` and `users`, each held by a permission, a tier or the person's own row), and one open question, POST-A-357: the people-and-companies register is read by every member of the firm whatever clients they are assigned to. Deliberately NOT done: no policy was changed, no migration was written.

- **THE NAMED-ROW RULE READS A QUERY PARAMETER, A MODEL INSIDE A MODEL AND A BODY TAKEN AS A DICT, AND THE STATEMENT INSPECTOR NOW ASKS WHOSE THE BANK ACCOUNT IS** (POST-A-353, first half, 29-10-2026). The rule of POST-A-350 read only the fields of the request model a route declares, by seven exact names. Four shapes were invisible to it. A model that carries another model (a receipt's allocations, a payment's bills) now names that model's rows too, followed to a fixed point. A query or form parameter that names a row counts like a body field, and a PATH parameter does not, because it is the row the route is about and not a second row it names. A body taken as a plain dict is a kind of its own, `dict_body`, because the AST cannot know its keys, so each such door is on the reviewed list with what the dict carries and a new one is a red test. The names are wider: a bare `invoice_id` or `bill_id` and the two the ledger named (`original_invoice_id`, `linked_bill_id`) are rows of the same tables.
