The run found one real gap and seven doors that are safe: `POST /api/banking/statements/inspect` took a bank account id as a form field and looked a saved column layout up by that id and the firm alone, so a person assigned to client A who named client B's account was handed B's saved layout; it asks `bank_account_is_the_clients` before it reads the file now. The two revaluation routes, the anomaly status route and the manual workflow trigger take dicts that name no row (or a client the route asserts), the payment-link route asks the invoice's own client through `can_access_client`, and the TDS preview's `exclude_bill_id` only drops one row from the vendor's own list. Four scan tests plant each shape in a throwaway tree, and a behavioural test drives the inspector with a sibling's account (it fails against the previous code). Deliberately NOT done: about sixty other model fields name a row under a name of their own (`payee_id`, `party_id`, `batch_id`, `from_godown_id`, `order_line_id`, `cost_centre_id`, the account ids and the rest), each needing its own table and asking function; they are surveyed in the ledger line, which stays open for them and for a write made through a database function.

- **THE NAMED-ROW RULE READS A STORED FILE, A LEDGER ENTRY, A PAYROLL EMPLOYEE AND THE THREE STOCK PLACES, AND THREE ROUTES THAT STORED ANOTHER CLIENT'S FILE OR ENTRY NOW ASK WHOSE IT IS** (POST-A-353, the stored-file half, 30-10-2026). Seven kinds join the seven of POST-A-350: `*document_id` (a stored file, `client_documents`), `*journal_entry_id`, `*employee_id` (`payroll_employees`), `purchase_payment_id` and `*godown_id`, `*batch_id`, `*cost_centre_id`. Run over the request models it flagged five doors, three of them real: the 26AS upload stored whatever `document_id` it was sent, the ITR workspace's deduction claim and disallowance stored whatever `evidence_document_id` they were sent, and the disallowance stored whatever `journal_entry_id` it was sent, so a person assigned to client A could attach client B's file or ledger entry to A's tax record (the foreign keys are not client-aware, and the privileged client does not narrow by client). Each now asks `client_document_is_the_clients` or `journal_entry_is_the_clients` BEFORE the `try` whose broad handler would otherwise turn the 404 into a 500; the document table is read through `privileged_for`, because no signed-in role holds a grant on it (POST-A-339). Two doors are safe and listed with the reason: removing a bank-line attachment only selects an entry of that line's own list, and the leave-balance route names no client and asks the employee's own client (`_assert_employee_scope`), which is why that helper is NOT in the rule's list of askers (a route that names a client AND an employee must tie them with `_employee_of_client`). The payroll, stock and ledger doors of the survey were already asked, and the rule now knows the helpers by name. Four behavioural tests drive each fixed door with a sibling's row (five tests fail against the previous routers), and the same request with the client's own row is not stopped. Deliberately NOT done: the remaining named fields (`payee_id`, `party_id`, `applied_to_id`, `order_line_id`, `challan_line_id`, the account ids, `enrolment_id`), and a write made through a database function.

- **TWO FIRMS DRIVE THE API AT ONCE IN A TEST, AND THE PROCESS-WIDE PARTS OF TENANCY ARE WHAT IT PROBES** (POST-A-012, 30-10-2026). Every tenant-isolation test before it called one caller at a time, and the things a second caller can break are the process-wide ones: the 30-second user-lookup cache (`core.auth._user_lookup_cache`, keyed by auth user), the per-request holders the authentication dependency binds the firm (`core.request_context.bind_firm`) and the verified person (`core.request_actor.bind`) into, and the thread pool a synchronous handler runs in. `tests/test_two_firms_calling_at_once_each_see_only_their_own.py` runs the real `get_current_user` (only the token decode is replaced) over the real user and firm lookup on the in-memory database double behind the real request-context middleware, sends 120 interleaved requests from two firms through the ASGI app at once, and has the synchronous handlers sleep in the pool so they overlap; it measures the overlap and fails if there was none. It holds that each request's user, bound firm and bound person are its own, that a customer list read through a real router holds only the caller's firm, that naming the other firm's client gets an empty list or a refusal and never that firm's rows, and that the cache keeps two users apart. With a process-wide current-user variable simulated, four of its five tests fail. `main._carry_user_token` begins the actor holder and is mirrored in the test (main's app is a process-wide object the test must not add routes to); a source assertion fails if main stops doing so. Deliberately NOT proved: the database's row security (the real-Postgres matrix) and a deployment with several workers (every cache and holder above is per process, and the in-process rate windows already say so).

- **THE WRITERS OF THE SIX DOCUMENT HEADERS AND THEIR SIX LINES TABLES ARE ON THE PRIVILEGED CLIENT, EACH HEADER STATEMENT NAMES ITS DOCUMENT, FIRM AND CLIENT, AND THE GRANT FOLLOWS IN ITS OWN CHANGE** (POST-A-335 part 2, step 1, 29-10-2026). Migration 541 froze the particulars of an ISSUED document and 544 sealed the settlement columns; a DRAFT's particulars were the part a member who holds the accounting tier could still change over PostgREST with their own token, skipping every check the API makes before it writes. The cure is the one 535, 536, 542 and 559 used: the API writes the tables as itself and the signed-in role loses the verbs. This change is the first half and takes no grant away. **WHAT MOVED:** the create, update and delete of `client_sales_invoices`, `purchase_bills`, `credit_notes`, `debit_notes`, `purchase_credit_notes` and `sales_debit_notes` in the six document routers; the invoice and bill line insert and unit edit; the draft-lines helper (`draft_document_lines.replace_draft_lines` now takes `privileged_for(db)` once, for the migration-560 function and for its two-step fallback alike); the billing, recurring-invoice and recurring-bill stamps and their compensating deletes; the collections sweep, the internal follow-up flag and the reminder counter; the opening-document create, remove and both bulk inserts; the landed-cost basis of a bill; and the late-interest draft take-back. The four note kinds are CREATED through `numbered_document_atomic`, which is SECURITY DEFINER, so a later revoke does not touch it. **THE PRIVILEGED CLIENT CARRIES NO ROW SECURITY, SO THE STATEMENT IS THE CONFINEMENT:** every header UPDATE or DELETE now ends `.eq("id", ...).eq("firm_id", ...).eq("client_id", ...)` (almost all named the firm and not the client, and a handful of the compensating deletes and the billing and collections statements named neither), `tests/test_the_document_headers_are_written_by_the_api_after_naming_their_firm_and_client.py` holds that over the source with its detector shown the shapes it must read, and the late-interest take-back that named no client was the one reviewed entry of the write rule that this closed (it left the list). The lines tables carry no firm of their own, so a line statement is confined by the parent the route read under the firm and the client first. **THE IN-FLIGHT LIST:** `tests/fixtures/tables_about_to_be_closed.txt` holds the twelve tables to the writer rule (`test_a_write_of_a_table_the_signed_in_role_cannot_write_is_made_on_a_client_that_can`, which now reads both lists) from the day their writers move, and is empty between such changes; the migration moves each name to the closed list. The rule fired on 28 functions at the start and on none at the end; the one module-level statement set (the header door's six lambdas) is reviewed with its reason. **WHY THE GRANT WAITS:** the same reason as the entry on POST-A-319 above: the old API against a revoked grant fails soft and wrong (a draft saved with its lines half replaced, an invoice created whose lines are refused), and a merge reaches Render before the migration job runs but nothing enforces it. Two tests that set a fake `SUPABASE_URL` had to stop the server's own client being built for it (`service_db_or_none` patched), the documented switch that is not a fallback. NOT DONE: the grant, the removal of the two-step fallback (migration 560 was not on production when this was written), and the older real-Postgres tests that write these tables as a signed-in session, which will need their write given back in their own setup when the grant goes.

- **THE SIGNED-IN ROLE WRITES NONE OF THE SIX DOCUMENT HEADERS OR THEIR LINES TABLES, AND A SIGNED-IN CALL TO THE DRAFT-LINES FUNCTION IS REFUSED BY THE SAME PRIVILEGE** (POST-A-335 part 2, step 2, migration 563, 29-10-2026). The grant followed the writers by the hour the rule asks: step 1 (no migration) reached production on 29-10-2026 at 13:51 UTC, this change merges at least an hour later. Migration 563 revokes INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE on `client_sales_invoices`, `purchase_bills`, `credit_notes`, `debit_notes`, `purchase_credit_notes`, `sales_debit_notes` and the six lines tables from PUBLIC, `authenticated` and `anon`, closes with a check of the effective privilege (table level, column level, through PUBLIC) that also asks that `service_role` kept its writes, and ships with a rollback; reading and the row policies and the particulars triggers (539, 541, 544) are the second lock. **A finding while testing it, written here because the migration's first header was wrong about it:** `replace_draft_document_lines` (migration 560) is SECURITY INVOKER on purpose, so it runs with the caller's privileges, and its `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` of the header needs UPDATE: after 563 only the privileged client can replace a draft's lines, which is the only caller the product has, and a hand-made call from a signed-in session is refused ("permission denied for table ..."). The four note creators go through `numbered_document_atomic`, which is SECURITY DEFINER and unaffected. The two-step fallback in `services/draft_document_lines` (a delete and then an insert, for the hour migration 560 was not on production) is deleted, since 560 has been live since 10:00 UTC the same day: a missing function is now the caller's error and the helper holds no second writer (a test reads its source). **The tests that exist to measure the second lock** (539, 541, 544, the internal-practice guard, and 560 which borrows 539's world) give the signed-in write back on their throwaway copy through `tests/_signed_in_document_writes.give_the_signed_in_writes_back`, which reads this migration's own rollback, the pattern of the payroll revokes; the access matrix gained a second-lock row for each of the six headers (what their policies decided before 563), the rollback in its "first lock lifted" test and in the 487 control, and its main expectation reads `x` in the six headers' write cells. `tests/fixtures/tables_about_to_be_closed.txt` is empty again, the twelve names moved to the closed list in this change.

- **THE PEOPLE-AND-COMPANIES REGISTER IS READ BY EVERY MEMBER OF THE FIRM ON PURPOSE, AND THE SCREEN SAYS SO** (POST-A-357, 29-10-2026). `entities` holds a PAN, a GSTIN, a date of birth, a phone number and an address, and `entity_relationships` and `entity_to_entity_relationships` hang from it; their one read policy asks the firm and nothing narrower, so a member assigned to client A can read the details of a person who stands behind client B only. The third catalog population (POST-A-305) lists the three as `firm_wide` and `test_a_table_with_no_client_is_firm_level_on_purpose_pg` checks that category against the policies. **Decided (the three tests, under the standing instructions): keep the firm-wide read and say so.** Narrowing it to "an entity is visible when a link ties it to a client in reach" would hide a person already known through ANOTHER client from the entity picker and from the cross-client match detection, which is the register's purpose, and would produce the duplicate it exists to prevent; every reader is a member of the one firm, and the signed-in role holds no write on any of the three since migration 562, so the exposure is a read by a colleague and not a change by one. The Entity Registry screen carries a notice naming the fields and the rule, and `tests/test_the_register_says_it_is_shared_across_the_practice.py` holds it to the catalog category and to the columns the table has (a Python guard, because the catalog and the policies are on that side). **If a pilot firm asks for a narrower read, the policy, the catalog category and the sentence change in one commit, and that test is what makes them.** Not done: the picker's behaviour under a narrowed read, which is the reason it was not built.

- **A SECTION 197 CERTIFICATE IS WRITTEN BY THE API ONLY, AND THE TAB SAYS IT CANNOT BE CHANGED OR REMOVED** (POST-A-359, 29-10-2026). A certificate lowers what is withheld from a real supplier, and the client's Compliance -> TDS tab wrote `tds_lower_deduction_certificates` straight from the browser behind migration 359's role-guarded policies, which were the only lock on a hand-made PostgREST call (no route asked the supplier, the section or the dates). POST-A-293 moved the only writer onto `POST /api/tds-workspace/lower-deduction-certificates` (privileged client, after the tier, the client, the supplier and the section are asked). **Migration 564** then revokes INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE on the table from PUBLIC, `authenticated` and `anon`, closes with a check of the EFFECTIVE privilege (table level, column level, through PUBLIC) that also asks `service_role` kept its writes, and ships with its rollback (INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, the verbs production held). It shipped more than an hour after the API change reached production, because the old browser bundle against revoked grants fails soft and wrong. The table joins `tests/fixtures/tables_the_signed_in_role_cannot_write.txt`, and `test_564_..._pg` proves the privileges, the Manager's refusal by the privilege for each verb, the service role's three statements, the worst case (a write through PUBLIC, a column grant and TRUNCATE), a grant the owner cannot take back failing the migration, idempotence and the rollback as the negative control. Reading and the row policies are untouched. **Not done, and said on the screen**: no endpoint ends or deletes a certificate (the policy said Manager and above), so a recorded certificate cannot be changed or removed from the product; the tab's heading now says so and to check the number, rate and period before saving. Adding the route waits until a CA asks.

- **A ROW NAMES A CLIENT OF ITS OWN FIRM AND A PARTY OF ITS OWN CLIENT, THE DATABASE ASKS ON EVERY WRITE, AND ONE INSTALLER GIVES A TABLE ITS TRIGGERS** (POST-A-304, 30-10-2026, migration 565). The independent review of migration 528 inserted, as a Partner of firm G, rows with G's `firm_id` and a client of firm F into sixteen tables, and, as an assigned Manager, a recurring template naming another client's customer: all accepted, because the Partner branch of the assignment scope short-circuits, the firm policy asks `firm_id` alone, and a foreign key bypasses row security. 565 installs, with `public.guard_client_firm(table)`, on every plain table with a uuid `client_id` and `firm_id` (209), two triggers: an AFTER INSERT statement trigger with a transition table (migration 360's reason: a statement upload writes thousands of rows in one statement, and one join costs the same whatever the count) and a BEFORE UPDATE OF client_id, firm_id row trigger with a WHEN clause (so an ordinary update pays nothing); and, where the table has a foreign key to `customers` (11 tables) or `vendors` (12), the same pair asking that the party belong to the row's own client. Six functions, all SECURITY DEFINER with a pinned search path because the check has to see the other firm's client and row security would hide it, none taking an argument; the installer is DDL and nobody signed in may run it. WHAT IT REFUSES AND WHAT IT LEAVES, on purpose: it refuses only what provably crosses a tenant (both firms known and different). A NULL `firm_id` on the row or on the client, a `client_id` that names no client (36 of these tables have no foreign key to `clients`) and a partitioned table are left alone, because refusing them would only break a write that could not be seen from here, and none of them crosses a tenant. Existing rows are not touched; the migration counts them and says so in a `RAISE WARNING 'migration 565: ...'` the runner keeps. The check is MEASURED AGAINST PRODUCTION: it was not, because this session had no read access to the production database, so the rules above were chosen so that none can refuse a write unless it names another tenant's client, customer or vendor. WHAT IT COST THE SUITE, which is the evidence it bites: 51 failures and errors in 17 real-Postgres modules, every one of them a fixture or a negative control that planted exactly the row the database now refuses (another firm's row sharing a client id, a vendor of another client on a bill, a rolled-back policy used as the old-hole control). They are fixed with `tests/_cross_tenant_rows.py`, which switches off only the six trigger names in a throwaway database (the schedule III test already did the same for migration 360's trigger), and, for two tests that roll a table back to an older column shape, drops them (Postgres refuses `ALTER COLUMN TYPE` on a column a trigger names, and a dropped column leaves the statement trigger reading a name that is gone: a later migration that changes the type of, or drops, `client_id` or `firm_id` on one of these tables must drop the triggers first). Three enumerations (the triggers on the payroll run, the triggers on the six header tables, the functions that can write a header table) now name 565's. ⚠️ THE SQLSTATE IS 42501, as migration 360's is, which is also what row security answers: a test that asserts only `err:42501` for a cross-tenant insert can now pass for the wrong reason. The internal-practice test did (its seed put a customer of one client on a document of another); it now builds its database with 565's triggers off so the refusal it measures is the guard's. Other such tests were not searched for. `tests/test_a_client_firm_table_a_migration_creates_carries_the_check.py` reads migration text and fails a table created from 566 on that has both columns and never calls the installer; `test_565_*_pg.py` holds the catalog (every table, the party keys, the privileges), the review's own case as a signed-in Partner with the same statement accepted once the trigger is off, a statement judged as a whole, the update path, the cases it leaves alone, the report, idempotence and the rollback. The member and portal-contact columns are POST-A-361.
 **THE LEDGER-ACCOUNT FAMILY IS THE SAME RULE, ONE MORE INSTALLER (POST-A-353, migration 570, 1-11-2026).** A journal LINE naming another client's account has been refused since migration 360, but the places that store a link to an account for later (a bank account's ledger account, a matching rule's suggested account, a budget, a bill of entry's two accounts, an asset's accumulated-depreciation account, a purchase bill's landed cost, an order line's expense account, a bank line's account and draft account, a split, an account's parent, the lines of the two recurring templates) were each a foreign key to `chart_of_accounts(id)`, which any account in the database satisfies, and four more had no key at all (a year-end adjustment's two accounts, a year-end mapping's account, a purchase bill line's expense account). `public.guard_ledger_account(table, column[, parent, parent_fk, firm_only])` installs, on one such column, a statement-level insert check (transition table) and a row-level update check that fires only when the column, the client or the firm changes: **the account belongs to the row's firm and is either the row's client's or a firm-level one (client_id NULL)**, 360's own rule, refusing only what provably crosses a tenant (both sides known and different). It is installed on every foreign key to the chart of a plain table with its own uuid `firm_id` and `client_id` (12 columns), through their template on the two recurring-template line tables (2), by name on the year-end adjustment's two unkeyed columns (2), through its bill on a purchase bill line's expense account (1), and, judged by the FIRM alone because a year-end mapping belongs to the firm and not to a client, on the mapping's account (1): **18 in all; `journal_lines` (360 asks), `account_period_balances` and `ledger_balances` (written only by the database's own triggers from lines 360 has checked) are the three named exceptions.** **A refusal is P0001, a business rule written for a person** (`core/exceptions` surfaces its message as it is): 565 uses 42501, which the API words to a CA as a fault on our side, and a form that kept one client's account while another client was chosen would have been told the server is misconfigured. The migration's closing check refuses to finish if an eligible column lacks its pair and counts rows that already break the rule and leaves them (a read-only count on production before writing found none in any of the 13 columns it could join, and no dangling account in the four unkeyed ones); `test_570_*_pg.py` derives the catalog from the schema, so **a foreign key to the chart added later, and a uuid column named for an account that is a key to nothing, fail there until they are guarded or named**. **Limits, written because the independent review (1-11-2026) found them:** only the row is judged, so a Partner who moves an account to another client does not re-judge the rows that already point at it, a firm-level row naming a client-level account is accepted (it matters for an account's own parent only, as the other eleven tables' client is NOT NULL), changing a template's own client does not re-judge its lines, and a column that names an account that does not exist is not refused (the four unkeyed ones have no key to say so). The review also found that `test_544`'s registry of functions that can write names every function with an EXECUTE and an INSERT or UPDATE, as 565 and 566 had to be added to; `guard_ledger_account` is added. This closes the database half of the ledger line; the API still answers a cross-client account at only some doors with the trigger's sentence rather than its own, which is courtesy and not security (nothing serves another client's account id). **Verified:** 22 real-Postgres tests (the case with the same statement accepted once the trigger is off, allowed and refused accounts, a batch as a statement, the update path and the repair of an already-bad row, a budget, an account's parent, a template's lines through the template and a move to another template, the ON DELETE SET NULL of a bank line's draft account, a year-end adjustment's two accounts, a bill line through its bill, a firm-wide mapping, the error code, a catalog derived from the schema for keys and for unkeyed columns, installer privileges and refusals, the apply report, a second run, the rollback) with four mutants of the first version (no firm check, firm-level accounts refused, an update that ignores a client move, a column skipped) each failing; the real-Postgres files that touch these tables and the registry-style files pass; not run: the other real-Postgres files (CI runs them) and the migration against production before merge. **AN INDEPENDENT REVIEW OF THE MERGED MIGRATION (1-11-2026; tests only, no migration, nothing in the migration changed): NO PRODUCTION DEFECT IN THE LOGIC, AND FIVE BEHAVIOURS NO TEST HELD.** The reviewer built the migration set into throwaway databases, ran the 22 tests, and ran mutants through them. A legitimate write is accepted (a firm-level account on a client's row, a client's own, a NULL one, an insert-select of 5,000 rows, COPY, MERGE, a data-modifying CTE, an upsert); a bad row fails its whole statement; the lookups are by primary key; the file re-runs; the warning line's counts are true; and there is no other uuid column named for an account that the guard misses. What no test held, each shown by a mutant that passed all 22: (1) the UPDATE check's firm clause (every update test re-pointed to an account that also differed by client, so the client clause refused it first; a firm-level account of another firm has no client to differ by); (2) SECURITY DEFINER, which exists because the writer cannot see another firm's account (the tests ran as a superuser, for whom nothing is hidden); (3) the parent table a recurring purchase-bill template's lines are judged through; (4) the update trigger on a purchase-bill line and on a year-end mapping, which no test updated; (5) the parent-path insert's firm clause; and four branches of the apply report (only a bank account had been planted). Five tests were added to the same file (27 pass on real Postgres): the firm clause alone refusing an update; a budget naming firm F's account written by a signed-in Partner of firm G, with the control that the account really is invisible to them (a version without SECURITY DEFINER accepts it); a recurring purchase bill's lines through their own template, with a move to another template and the firm-level account of another firm; an update of a bill line and of a mapping; and one bad row planted past the triggers in each of eight columns, which the report counts as '8 existing row(s) in 8 column(s)'. Every one of those mutants, and one per report branch, now fails a test. **Two facts for whoever next touches these columns:** (a) migration 553's rollback no longer runs on its own, because `accumulated_account_id` now carries 570's triggers and Postgres refuses to drop a column a trigger depends on; 570's rollback has to run first (a rollback in reverse order is unaffected, and the same dependency would block a `DROP COLUMN` or a type change on any of the 18 guarded columns); the commit patched the 553 test's fixture and nothing in a migration file or a document said so until now. (b) A small cost, not measured and not changed: `assert_updated_row_names_a_ledger_account_of_its_client` converts the whole new row to JSON before it tests whether the account is NULL, so each `ON DELETE SET NULL` cascade row pays a full-row conversion for nothing; fixing it is a new migration for a micro-cost and was not judged worth one. Not checked by the review: the real production catalog and data, concurrent load, and the owner of the functions on Supabase (the test cluster's `postgres` is a superuser).
- **A ROW NAMES A MEMBER OF ITS OWN FIRM, THE DATABASE REFUSES ONE THAT NAMES ANOTHER FIRM'S, AND A COLUMN ADDED LATER ASKS THE INSTALLER AGAIN** (POST-A-361, migration 566, 30-10-2026). Migration 565 made the database refuse a row naming a client of another firm; the same shape was open for the columns that name a MEMBER (`created_by`, `assigned_to`, `reviewed_by`, `user_id` and the rest, all foreign keys to `users`): a Partner of firm G, a foreign-key check that bypasses row security and a firm policy that asks `firm_id` alone stored a row of G naming a member of F. Migration 530 had closed it for the two tables that decide access and for nothing else. `public.guard_member_firm(table)` finds the table's single-column foreign keys to `users` in the catalog (`public.member_columns_of`) and gives it two triggers that carry those column names as their arguments: a STATEMENT-level AFTER INSERT over a transition table, one set-based join per column however many rows the statement carries, and a ROW-level BEFORE UPDATE OF those columns and `firm_id`, firing only when one of them changes. It refuses only what provably crosses a tenant: a NULL firm, a NULL member and a row that already breaks the rule (it can still be edited and repaired) are left alone, and `users.firm_id` is NOT NULL so a member always names a firm. The apply report counts the existing rows and changes none. The first full real-Postgres run failed 12 tests and errored 12 setups in 8 modules, and every one was a fixture or a negative control that planted exactly the row the database now refuses (a grant of access naming a member of another firm, a Tally job naming the other firm's Partner, a journal whose `created_by` is another firm's person, which the audit-actor test had expected to post). They are fixed with the same helper as 565 (`tests/_cross_tenant_rows`, now naming the member triggers too), a fixture that no longer names a foreign person, and one case moved from "still posts" to "is refused". TWO FACTS WORTH KEEPING. (1) The update trigger names its columns, so Postgres refuses to drop or retype one while the trigger stands; a migration that does so drops the table's two triggers first and calls the installer after. A `DROP COLUMN ... CASCADE` would take the update trigger and leave the insert one pointing at a dead column, so the insert function SKIPS a column that no longer exists in the catalog (and the catalog test, which compares each trigger's arguments with the foreign keys, fails the table). (2) A column ADDED to a table that already has the triggers is unchecked until the installer is called again, so `tests/test_a_firm_table_a_migration_creates_that_names_a_member_carries_the_check.py` reads migrations from 567 on and fails a table created, or a member column added, with no later call of `guard_member_firm` for it. Deliberately NOT done and still open as POST-A-361: the portal-contact half (no table has a foreign key to `client_portal_users`; the one column that names one is a bare uuid on a table only the API writes), a column that names a member with no foreign key, and a member who changes firm.
- **THE MEMBER CHECK LEAVES THE PORTAL CONTACT BARE AND NEVER RE-CHECKS A MEMBER WHO CHANGES FIRM, AND EACH OF THE TWO RESTS ON A PREMISE A TEST HOLDS** (POST-A-361, parts 1 and 3, 1-11-2026, no migration). **THE PORTAL CONTACT:** `client_portal_sessions.portal_contact_id` is a bare uuid and no table has a foreign key to `client_portal_users`, so migration 566's catalog cannot see it. It STAYS bare, because a key is wrong for a LOG three ways: `ON DELETE CASCADE` deletes the access record when a contact is removed, `ON DELETE SET NULL` blanks who it was (the row keeps only the email beside it), and `RESTRICT` stops a contact from ever being removed. What makes a bare column safe is WHO WRITES IT: one place, `core.portal_auth._touch_portal_session`, on the privileged client (since migration 542 nobody signed in can write the table), and all four values it stores (`firm_id`, `client_id`, `portal_contact_id`, `email`) are `ctx.get(...)` of the membership the portal dependency has just resolved for the verified sign-in (`list_portal_memberships`, `select_active_membership`), never a value from the request. Production, read-only on 1-11-2026: 2 session rows, 9 contacts, no row naming a contact that does not exist. **A MEMBER WHO CHANGES FIRM:** 566 checks a row when it is written or when one of its member columns changes, so a member who moved firm would leave rows naming them in the old firm unchecked. Nothing moves a member: no API write to `users` sets `firm_id` (the writers set `first_run_dismissed_at`, `cost_rate_paise` and `default_billable_rate_paise`; onboarding INSERTS a member into a firm once, after asking for an existing row; the one variable-payload writer, `UserRepository.update`, refuses a payload naming another firm before it reaches the database and filters by the caller's firm too), no migration's SQL updates `users.firm_id`, and the signed-in role holds no UPDATE on `users`, `firm_id` or `role` (production and a migration-built database: `false,false,false`; the two policies are the own-row select and the own-row rename). **THE TEST** reads the writers by AST and fails the day a second writer of the session log appears or its writer takes a value from anywhere else, a write to `users` names `firm_id` or has a payload it cannot read (a variable is UNREADABLE and fails, so the blind spot fails towards tripping), the repository's refusal is removed, a migration updates a member's firm, or the signed-in role gains UPDATE; six mutants each fail a test. **WHAT IT CANNOT SEE:** a write through a raw SQL function or RPC (the migrations are read as text for `UPDATE users SET ... firm_id`), a table name held in a variable, string dispatch. **STILL OPEN (ledger POST-A-361 part 2):** 71 person columns with no key and which id each holds.

- **A REQUEST THAT NAMES A PERSON ASKS WHETHER THEY ARE A MEMBER OF THE CALLER'S FIRM, BECAUSE NINE DOORS TOOK THE ID AS TYPED AND THE DATABASE'S KEY CHECK IS NOT A SENTENCE** (POST-A-361, part 2, 1-11-2026, no migration). **WHAT WAS WRONG:** migration 566 makes the database refuse a row that names a member of another firm, but only through a foreign key to `users`; the 79 person columns with no key (a survey of production the same day) are checked by nothing, and even a keyed column's refusal comes back as a failure a person cannot read. Reading the request models found nine doors that wrote a typed person: `task_recurring_configs.assignee_id` (create and edit; a bare uuid, so a stranger's id was simply stored and the daily sweep would later create a task for them), a fee engagement's `assigned_to` (bare) with its reviewer and partner, a lead's assignee on create and edit, a compliance obligation's preparer, reviewer and approver, and a notification's `user_id` (a stranger of another firm would have been sent this firm's title, text and link). The ordinary task door and the task-template door already asked (`user_repo.find_by_id(..., firm_id=...)`); these did not. **WHAT IT DOES:** `services/named_row_ownership.member_is_the_firms(db, firm_id, member_id)` reads `users` by the id AND the firm with the privileged client, before the first write, and answers `404 That team member was not found.` for a member of another firm, a deleted one and an invented id alike (nothing named, or no database, asks nothing). It checks membership and not activity: a suspended member can still be named, as the task door has always allowed, because whether somebody may be given work is a staffing decision and not a tenancy one. Each of the nine doors calls it once per named field. `RenewalIn.assigned_to` was accepted and never stored (the table has no such column), so the field was deleted and not listed. **HOW THE RULE FINDS THE NEXT ONE:** a third family in the derived rule over request models (`tests/test_a_request_that_names_a_row_asks_whose_it_is.py`): a field named `assigned_to`, `assignee_id`, `assignee`, `reviewer_id`, `approver_id`, `preparer_id`, `partner_id` or `user_id` on a body, query or form parameter of a writing route makes the route a door. This family is asked by its own scan and NOT by the call graph's reach: the graph resolves a callee by its bare name when only one method has it, an audit write therefore ends in the MFA reset's lookup of a user, and the first run of the family over the graph said all seventeen doors asked, including the recurring one that plainly did not. A lookup counts only when it is made ON the value the request carried (its text names the field, or the parameter the value was handed on as, up to three calls down) and it is the ownership function, the user repository's own lookup, or a read of `users` by id and firm; the caller's own row and the firm's roster do not count. The scan also stopped mixing two models of one name (`routers/engagements.py` and `routers/engagement_letters.py` each define an `EngagementUpdate`; models are keyed by module and class now). **HOW IT IS HELD:** eleven synthetic scan cases (flagged unasked; asked in the route, through the repository, through a read by id and firm, two calls down; not asked by the caller's own lookup, a roster, a check handed nothing; two same-named models), the ownership function against a stranger, a deleted and an invented member, and one behavioural test per door that checks a stranger is refused and nothing is stored while the firm's own member and a nobody are accepted. Mutation: removing the check from each router in turn fails that router's tests and the scan, and a helper that stops filtering by firm fails the stranger tests. **NOT DONE, ON PURPOSE:** the per-column map of which id each bare column holds and a guard on the writers of the stamped ones (ledger POST-A-361 part 2 keeps it); a person a job picks for itself is not a request and is not asked (the escalation sweep re-assigns an overdue task to the least busy user of a role, found in the firm's own roster). **CORRECTED THE SAME DAY BY AN INDEPENDENT REVIEW OF THE FIRST VERSION (1-11-2026, no migration); SIX OF THE SENTENCES ABOVE WERE WRONG OR WERE NOT YET TRUE, AND EACH IS NOW SO:** (1) **A lead's assignee is keyed to `auth.users(id)`, not to `users`** (migration 059; the production snapshot of 13-09-2026 carries the same key), so it is the one person column that holds a member's AUTH id. The first version asked it with the `users.id` function: an auth id was refused with a 404 and a `users.id` passed the check and then failed the key as a 500, so NO value could be assigned on an edit. The lead door now asks `member_auth_id_is_the_firms` (reads `users.auth_user_id` under the firm). Production held 8 leads, none with an assignee, and 2 members, so no stored row was affected. This is also what the survey's 'one keyed to `auth.users`' was; migration 566 does not cover that column (it guards keys to `public.users` only). (2) **The lead CREATE door protected nothing**: its database branch wrote `assigned_to: None` whatever the request carried (a leftover from when the screen sent an entity type there), so the check refused requests whose value would have been discarded anyway, and the entry was wrong to say the door 'took the id as typed'. It now stores the assignee it has just asked about; no assignee still stores nothing. (3) **A deleted member was not refused**, although the text and the docstring said so: the helper filtered on id and firm only. It now also leaves out `users.deleted_at`, the way the task door's own lookup does (production holds no deleted member today; the change is latent). A suspended member is still allowed. (4) **The scan could be masked by a field called `user_id`.** The call graph resolves `.execute()` by its bare name to the one function of that name in the tree, the MFA reset's, which looks a `user_id` up; a follow was decided on whether the CALL's text named the field, and a fluent chain's text contains it in its receiver, so a door that stored a `user_id` and asked nothing was read as asked (reproduced on a copy of the tree: the other seven field names were flagged, `user_id` alone was not). A follow is now decided on the call's ARGUMENTS only, and the one real door it uncovered, `remove_assignment` (a DELETE matched on firm, client and person together, so a stranger matches nothing), is on the reviewed list with that reason. The scan still has no notion of order or of branches: a check placed after the write, or on one branch only, or a lookup whose result is ignored, is read as asked; none of the nine doors does any of that, and the limit is stated here so a tenth is read with it in mind. (5) **An assignment rule's target was asked only when the request itself said `fixed_user`**: a PATCH carrying only `target_value` on a rule that is already `fixed_user` was stored as typed (it matched no member when the rule was evaluated, so it did nothing, but it was kept). The type that counts is now the one the rule will have (the request's, else the stored one). `target_value` is not a name the scan reads, so this one is held by its own test and not by the rule. (6) **Two comments said the reviewer, partner, preparer and approver columns were keys**; they are bare uuids (migration 108; the production snapshot holds a key only on a fee engagement's client and firm and on a compliance record's `assigned_to`), so for them this check is the only one. **HOW THE CORRECTIONS ARE HELD:** the lead test now seeds a member with an auth id and a deleted one and checks the four values the first version let through or refused wrongly (another firm's, invented, deleted, and a `users.id`), that create stores the named assignee, and that edit moves it; the ownership functions are tested for a deleted and a suspended member; the scan has a synthetic case for the masked `user_id` (a chain and a variable holding a chain) and for the auth-id lookup (with and without the firm). Seven mutations were each caught: the lead edit asking `users.id` again, create storing nothing, a deleted member passing (each of the two functions), the rule patch asking only when the request carries the type, the scan reading the whole call's text again, the scan counting an auth-id lookup with no firm, and the scan not knowing the auth-id function.
